# Regression list

Every failure mode an earlier study learned, with where it was learned and the test that keeps it learned. Issues #3 and #4 turn each entry into at least one test, and release notes name the entries a release guarantees. Part of #2; the inventory that cites these IDs is [`inventory.md`](inventory.md).

## How to read an entry

**ID. Title.** What the core must do. *Learned:* where each study met it, as `STUDY:path:line`, plus the commit or date that fixed it. *Test:* the check #3 or #4 writes.

Source keys (full refs in [`inventory.md`](inventory.md#sources)):

| Key | Repository and ref |
|---|---|
| PPAM | onedata-mcp, branch `ppam2026/14-tools` @ `08e201b`, directory `benchmark/` |
| ICSOC | icsoc-2026-code-agent @ `c1e76c2`; `ICSOC-pkg` is its replication package @ `06223f1` |
| WWW | www-2027-experiments, branch `feat/sib-u-compose` @ `46a5828d` |
| ICSA | icsa-2027-experiments, branch `feat/37-artifact-clear` @ `6fc798cf`; `ICSA-snap` is `feat/34-final-snapshot-code` @ `debba6d6` |
| OMCP | onedata-mcp @ `8c9b10c34efdad29498362fc4be4e785ff2a102b` |

Line numbers come from `git archive` snapshots of these refs. A few WWW scoring and statistics lines were located by a sweep and may be off by a few lines; every other location was read directly.

Groups: [B model backends](#b-model-backends) · [L loop and tools](#l-agent-loop-and-tools) · [R records and replay](#r-records-and-replay) · [C campaign and failure accounting](#c-campaign-and-failure-accounting) · [P provenance](#p-provenance) · [S statistics and cause coding](#s-statistics-and-cause-coding) · [O platform (Onedata)](#o-platform-onedata) · [K replication packages](#k-replication-packages).

## B. Model backends

**B01. Retry transient API errors with capped exponential backoff.** Retry 429, 408, 409, 5xx, timeouts before a reply, and connection errors. Back off exponentially up to a cap, with jitter, and honour `Retry-After`. Turn SDK-internal retries off so every retry is counted and recorded. *Learned:* PPAM `benchmark/llm_adapters/openai_compat.py:44-84` (dde4246, then 1d41f37 after a 14 s budget proved shorter than a 60 s rate-limit window; 5xx not retried); lost in ICSOC `harness/panel.py:337-348` (one try, then `break`); rebuilt in WWW `harness/agent/providers.py:760-823` (d1e22ac5, 18 Oct) and ICSA `icsa/harness/adapters.py:168-198, 334-358` (4f6d4580, 1 Nov; no jitter, no `Retry-After`). Neither source meets the rule as written: WWW caps `Retry-After` at its maximum delay and then applies jitter of ±25 % (`providers.py:819-823`), so a 60 s header can produce a 45 s sleep. The core applies jitter only above `Retry-After` and never shortens it. *Test:* a fake raising 429 three times then answering yields one turn with three recorded retries and sleeps of at least `Retry-After`; a 400 is not retried.

**B02. Exhausted retries are infrastructure.** A transient error that outlasts the retries ends the attempt as an infrastructure failure, unscored and re-run, never as an agent outcome. *Learned:* WWW `providers.py:896-901`; ICSA `adapters.py:186-195` (`InfraError("endpoint")`). *Test:* N+1 transient errors give `failed:infrastructure` with source `endpoint` and no score.

**B03. A request that was sent and is slow is never resent.** A read timeout after the request went out ends the run at its time cap with usage marked unknown; connect, pool and write timeouts are failures to reach the endpoint and stay infrastructure. A retry that would outlast the run's budget is infrastructure; the run's own deadline is checked before the retry limit. *Learned:* ICSA `adapters.py:83-99, 181-185`, `loop.py:195-197` (final-review fixes, 1 Nov, `docs/harness.md:141-156`); WWW `providers.py:864-907` (1810cfb5, 18 Oct). *Test:* a `ReadTimeout` ends as time cap after exactly one request; a connect timeout is retried and then infrastructure.

**B04. Echo the reasoning field when the model needs it.** Per model and off by default, send the previous turn's reasoning back under the field the deployment expects. Keep it in the transcript either way. *Learned:* PPAM `openai_compat.py:185-193` (HTTP 400 without it; 8f00137, 22bd69f); lost in ICSOC `panel.py:359-372`; rebuilt in WWW `providers.py:227-279` (a2ea65c6, 21 Oct) and ICSA `adapters.py:216-226, 264-265` (1 Nov). *Test:* with echo on, the next request carries the field; with echo off, the wire carries neither it nor the internal copy, and the transcript still holds it.

**B05. Anthropic thinking blocks go back verbatim.** *Learned:* ICSA `adapters.py:384-389`; WWW `providers.py:198-200`. *Test:* the next request's assistant content equals the previous response blocks, signatures included.

**B06. Served-model check.** Record the model id, system fingerprint and every echoed setting of each reply. Treat another id, a changed fingerprint, a setting that differs from the request, or a setting that was never requested (a silent server default) as a mismatch that stops the model's dispatch; treat a reply with no id as unreported. A reply carrying several fingerprints is a change by itself; a dated snapshot id (`<model>-YYYYMMDD`) is the same model. *Learned:* WWW `scripts/run_pilot.py:2175-2283`, `loop.py:488-500, 683-699` (id 57e148c3, 19 Oct; fingerprint c396cc38, 20 Oct; mixed fingerprints c2378e26, 21 Oct); ICSA `icsa/servedsettings.py:22-102`, `campaign/scheduler.py:948-965` (f1c242a8, 3 Nov; "the same silent-default bug", `servedsettings.py:1`). *Test:* `judge` returns mismatch for another id, a changed setting and an unrequested setting; unreported for no id; a missing setting is recorded as "not reported", never as the requested value.

**B07. A listed model is not a servable model.** Admit a model only after a one-token completion succeeds; a `/models` listing is not enough. The completion's reply must report its input and its output tokens: the token budget is held from the counts each reply reports, so a model whose endpoint omits them would be the one model the cap does not bind, which confounds every comparison of models. A probe that returns nothing to read cannot show the counts and is refused the same way. *Learned:* ICSOC `panel.py:776-829` (inactive models and models outside the grant passed the listing); the review of the first real study's budget gate (issue #94) for the usage. *Test:* a fake that lists the id but answers 400 on completion rejects the model before the campaign starts; a completion without one count or both is not admitted, for either SDK, and the model's runs are held as not admitted; a backend whose probe returns nothing is refused, and one that passes its inner probe's reply on is admitted.

**B08. Unfrozen settings refuse a study run.** *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/harness/config.py:210-218` (`assert_frozen`; 9dd6dda5, 1 Nov). This is a configuration gate, separate from B06. *Test:* a model or harness config whose status is not `frozen` raises before any request.

**B09. A rejected key is not retried and not recorded.** 401 and 403 end as `key_rejected` after one request; the SDK's message, which may echo the key, is never recorded. *Learned:* ICSA `adapters.py:32-36, 179-180, 342-343` (f1c242a8, 3 Nov). *Test:* 401 and 403 each give one `key_rejected` after one request, with no SDK text in the record.

**B10. Usage is counted on arrival and never invented.** Count tokens before parsing can fail; a 200 without usage or without choices is infrastructure; unreported usage is `None`, never 0; cached prompt tokens count toward the token budget. *Learned:* ICSA `adapters.py:203-214, 360-371, 390-397` (8bc7ce09, 1 Nov; f1c242a8, 3 Nov); WWW `providers.py:17-28, 301-312`. *Test:* a 200 with usage and no choices raises infrastructure and the usage still counts; input tokens equal uncached plus cache-write plus cache-read.

**B11. Output-token limits fit the model and the endpoint.** `max_tokens` is the study value or the model's own lower maximum, checked against the endpoint's context at admission. *Learned:* ICSA `config.py:88-90` (amendment 6); PPAM `benchmark/panel.py:115-119, 169-173` (402 and context errors); ICSOC reset the limits to 4,096 tokens and 8 rounds (`panel.py:259-260`) after PPAM had raised them for empty answers (cb4a49f). *Test:* a model with a lower maximum sends its own value; a limit over the endpoint's context fails admission.

**B12. A reply cut at the output limit is not an answer.** Record `finish_reason`; a final reply stopped by `length` ends as an output cap, checked before the token budget. *Learned:* PPAM `openai_compat.py:158`; lost in ICSOC `panel.py:355-376`; ICSA `loop.py:151, 220-222`. *Test:* a tool-free reply with stop `length` ends as output cap even when the budget is also exceeded.

**B13. Context overflow is the agent's outcome.** A 400 or 413 that names the context is recorded as context overflow and not retried. *Learned:* WWW `providers.py:691-728` (agent-run/9). *Test:* each tested provider message classifies as overflow.

**B14. Vendor extras pass through.** `extra_body` and provider pinning without fallback reach the request. *Learned:* PPAM `benchmark/panel.py:125-150`, `openai_compat.py:125, 137` (a fallback provider returned gibberish); lost in ICSOC `panel.py:338-345`. *Test:* a condition's extras appear in the request body.

**B15. Record what was sent, not what was meant.** Sampling values in the record come from the request object; credentials are stripped from recorded URLs; keys are read at use time. *Learned:* ICSOC `harness/a2_judge.py:114-117` against `lenses/a2.py:34` (a68ff62); WWW `providers.py:915-923`; ICSA `runner.py:61-70`. *Test:* the recorded temperature equals the request's; a base URL with credentials is recorded without them.

**B16. Incompatible per-model rules are refused before any request.** *Learned:* WWW `loop.py:346-351, 971-998` (echo with reasoning-channel recovery; caps below the notice length). *Test:* each incompatible combination raises at configuration.

**B17. Streamed replies must be complete.** When streaming is used: a reply counts only with the end marker, a finish reason and the final usage chunk; an error event after HTTP 200 is transient; a call without id or name, or a token count that rises with no characters, is rejected and retried; a quiet stream is cut by a whole-request watchdog. *Learned:* WWW `providers.py:1308-1396, 1605-1805` (bec52d27, 1b7b7ec8, 79a3d49c; 19 Oct). Applies when a streaming transport is ported, after v0.1 (see [`architecture.md`](architecture.md#release-boundary)). *Test:* WWW `tests/test_agent_streaming.py` cases.

**B18. A model served by several declared deployments is one model.** A model entry may declare `accepted_fingerprints`, the serving fingerprints of the deployments the study decided are the same model; the set is part of the campaign fingerprint. A reply served under a fingerprint of the set is the model, one under any other sets its run aside as a changed identity (C16), and the served model id is checked as before. The admission probe must be served by the model under a fingerprint of the set. Each declared fingerprint is confirmed by the probe or by the first run whose reply shows it, and one never shown is reported as unconfirmed. Each attempt records the turns each fingerprint served, and the export and the report give each fingerprint's share of the analysed turns and runs. A model that declares no set is checked, recorded and fingerprinted as before. A preflight's served-model check (`expcore.models.served.judge`) takes the model's set (`accepted_fingerprints=`) and judges it by the rule the campaign's check applies (`outside_accepted`, one definition): replies from any two declared deployments are a match, an undeclared fingerprint is still a mismatch, a mixed reply counts only when the set lists each of its fingerprints, and a model without a set is judged as before. *Learned:* the first study's real-model smoke on a shared inference service (issue #134): a load-balanced endpoint served one model id from two deployments within one run, so the identity check set the run aside, and most long runs would have been. Then, in the same study's preflight (issue #214), a provider switched a model between its two declared deployments within one session and `judge`, which had no set to take, reported a mismatch the campaign would have accepted. *Test:* a run switching between two declared fingerprints is scored, a third fingerprint sets it aside, a model without the key keeps its fingerprint digest and its checks, the export and the report give the shares, and a resume under another set is refused; `judge` accepts replies from two declared members, flags an undeclared one, leaves a model without a set unchanged, and agrees with the campaign's check on the same replies.

**B19. An abandoned request has a bound on its prompt that is tight where its assumptions hold and the window where they do not.** When the loop gives up a request in flight, or its read timeout fires, nothing comes back, and the only bound was the model's whole context window W for the prompt: about 1.1 M tokens added to a run that had sent 100,000. Both adapters now bound the prompt of every request when they build it, and the record's `route.abandoned_request` carries the bound of the request that was given up: `prompt_bound_tokens` and its basis `prompt_bound_by` (`bytes`, `window` or `none`), the `reply_max_tokens` the request carried, `window_tokens`, and what the conversation tested (`bound_checked`, `bound_exceeded`, `bound_unreported`, `bound_overlapped`). The bound is the last reply's reported prompt and completion, plus the bytes of what was appended since as a chat template writes them (ASCII JSON, with `<`, `>`, `&` and `'` counted as the six bytes `tojson` writes), plus a margin of 32 tokens per appended message and per request; it is W for the first request, after a reply that left a count out, once the bound is W or more, for a history that is no extension of the last reply's (a digest check), and for the rest of a conversation in which a reply reported a prompt above its bound or a request overlapped another. A token is taken to stand for at least one byte, which the tripwire tests and does not prove; the derivation, every term and every assumption are in `expcore.models.request_bound` and [`interfaces.md`](interfaces.md#the-request-given-up). `expcore.campaign.abandoned_bound.abandoned_request_bound` prices it from the record: the reply at the `max_tokens` it carried for an attempt held to it (C27), and the prompt and reply together within W for any other, at the dearest rates of the tiers the prompt bound can reach. The basis it returns carries the tripwire's account for that model in that run (`tripwire_checked`, `tripwire_exceeded`, `tripwire_unreported`, `tripwire_overlapped`, `tripwire_fired_before`; None where the entry lacks the counts), so a study can say what a tight bound rests on. It is recorded only: the budget gate does not read it, and the reservation of C25 and C27 is unchanged. *Learned:* the first study's cost scorer (#189). *Test:* the arithmetic, the bytes (UTF-8, not characters), every fallback to the window, the tripwire and a one-token-per-byte tokenizer as a property; both adapters against a fake client, a resend, a cancel and a timeout; the bound written, loaded and replayed in a record; the dollars for flat, cached and tiered prices, held and not held, and the entries it refuses; the tripwire's account in the basis, clean, fired, absent and per model.

## L. Agent loop and tools

**L01. Malformed tool-call arguments are never run as `{}`.** Do not execute the call; tell the model its arguments were not valid JSON; keep the raw text in history as `{"_raw": "..."}`, because servers reject invalid JSON in history with HTTP 400. Report-and-continue is the default and ending the attempt the declared alternative; executing with `{}` is never offered. Malformed calls are counted per run as a recorded metric. *Learned:* PPAM `openai_compat.py:224-229` and ICSOC `panel.py:380-385` silently ran the tool with `{}`; ICSA fixed it (`adapters.py:102-109, 227-237`, `loop.py:104-107`; `docs/harness.md:25` names the ICSOC base); WWW ends the attempt as `failed:malformed_tool_call` (`loop.py:782-788`; d2d0402b, 19 Oct). Issue #2 listed this item the other way round; the code shows ICSA as the fix. *Test:* arguments `'{"a":'` are not executed, the tool result says so, the resent history is valid JSON holding the raw text, the round counts toward the turn cap, and the run's malformed-call count is 1.

**L02. Unknown tools and wrong argument types are tool errors, not crashes.** *Learned:* ICSA `loop.py:109-111, 148`; WWW `tools.py:693-694, 787`. *Test:* an unknown name returns an error listing the tools.

**L03. Turn cap counts rounds.** A turn with several calls counts once; a tool-free final reply after the last round is allowed. *Learned:* ICSA `loop.py:229-234`. *Test:* three calls in one turn count once; cap + 1 rounds ends as turn cap.

**L04. The agent is charged only for its own time.** Check the wall cap before every model and tool call; exclude waits on infrastructure (store, gate, slot, lock, output validation) from it; the agent's own cap decides before any whole-time ceiling. *Learned:* ICSA `loop.py:185-194`, `runner.py:442-452`; WWW `loop.py:636-651` (ac621236, f7d6a4d6; 20 Oct). *Test:* a slow fake infrastructure call leaves charged time under the cap; own time at the cap gives a wall cap even when the ceiling is also passed.

**L05. No hidden per-call limit.** A tool call is bounded by the run's remaining time, and none starts with less than a second left. *Learned:* ICSA `loop.py:98-101, 112-116` (a hidden 600 s cell cap would have looked like a measured outcome; be25edbc, 1 Nov, amendment 3). *Test:* the timeout passed to the sandbox equals the deadline minus now.

**L06. Tool output is capped by a declared rule, and the rule is pinned.** The cap counts characters, keeps its notice inside the cap, and is stated in the tool description so the tools hash pins it; the full raw result stays in the record. Errors are capped like any output, with the error class first; the full raw error stays in the record, with a truncation flag on what the model saw; the cap value is part of the configuration hash. WWW exempted errors from the cap. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/harness/capping.py:11-33`, `loop.py:93-96`; WWW `harness/agent/tools.py:13-19, 121-141, 241-303` (9f28d89f, 61474bdc; 19 Oct); PPAM capped only the record and sent the model the full text (`openai_compat.py:34, 238-247`); ICSOC had no cap. *Test:* an output over the cap ends with the notice and stays within it; a long error keeps its class in the capped text, its raw form in the record, and a truncation flag; two caps give two tools hashes and two configuration hashes.

**L07. A truncation by the data source is flagged.** A result exactly at the source's own row or size limit is marked as possibly cut. *Learned:* WWW `tools.py:259-263` (2615bfd0, 20 Oct). *Test:* rows equal to the source limit set the flag.

**L08. A crash in harness code is not the agent's failure.** Record it as a harness defect with a line-free stack hash, set the attempt aside, and re-run it after the fix. *Learned:* WWW `loop.py:277-296, 805-822`; ICSA `runner.py:349-352` and the campaign's same-row re-queue (`store.py:234-249`). *Test:* a tool raising on valid input yields `harness_defect`, never a scored failure.

**L09. Calls after a terminal call are answered "skipped".** History stays byte-identical across a retry branch. *Learned:* WWW `loop.py:611-617, 905-919`. *Test:* calls following a submit are logged skipped.

**L10. Leaked tool calls are detected, not nudged.** A call written as text or in the reasoning channel is labelled. *Learned:* WWW `providers.py:377-511`; PPAM `rescore.py:200-243`. *Test:* each recorded leak shape is labelled; domain JSON naming no tool is not.

**L11. An empty final reply differs from no reply.** *Learned:* PPAM `oracles/placement.py:214-224` (324b72e); ICSOC `panel.py:408-418`. *Test:* the record distinguishes empty content from rounds exhausted.

**L12. MCP failures are split by side.** A closed transport or an internal server error is infrastructure; any other MCP error goes to the model as tool output. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/harness/knowledge.py:84-95` (ruling of 1 Nov, b449aff1). *Test:* invalid params produce a tool error; a closed connection raises infrastructure.

**L13. The tool manifest is frozen and enforced by the server.** Names, descriptions and schemas must equal the frozen manifest; the allow-list is set server-side; no client-side filter, so a server exposing an extra tool fails to start. An allow-list naming a tool the server lacks also fails at start. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/knowledge/launch.py:53-80`, `pin.py:82-94`, `harness/knowledge.py:58-60` (bc30a8e5, 1 Nov); PPAM `benchmark/_tool_export.py:22-24`, `test/unit/test_tool_allowlist.py`; ICSOC `panel.py:319-321`. *Test:* an extra tool and a missing tool each fail start.

**L14. A tool server that does not start in time is broken.** *Learned:* ICSA `runner.py:36, 326` (120 s). *Test:* a server that never answers fails start within the bound.

**L15. Blocking waits never run on the event loop.** *Learned:* PPAM `oracles/placement.py:209`, `fixture_runner.py:379` serialised trials (`run_panel.py:26-28`); ICSOC used `asyncio.to_thread` (`runner.py:578-595`). *Test:* two concurrent fake waits overlap in wall time.

**L16. A tool call to an MCP server ends at its `timeout_s`, whether the loop made it or not.** `McpToolProvider.execute` bounds the call by the `timeout_s` it is given, and a server that is up and never answers raises `CallTimedOut`, an `InfraError` of kind `knowledge` that names the tool and the bound. A call made outside the loop (a pre-run check, the day-0 outage check, a health probe) has no other bound, so it hung the attempt, with the lease and the federation held, and recorded nothing. Inside the loop `timeout_s` is the agent's time left, so the call's bound is the loop's own and never longer, and the two expire at one moment: the watch judges the call's expiry as the loop's cut (a time cap, or the ceiling's infrastructure) whichever the event loop runs first, so a hung call at the cap is the agent's outcome and never `knowledge` infrastructure that sets the attempt aside. *Learned:* #204, the first study's review (an `execute` that ignored `timeout_s`, its docstring making the loop's time the call's only limit). *Test:* a server that accepts a call and never answers raises `CallTimedOut` at the bound, naming the tool, and its child is gone; the session serves the next call; a loop run with a hung call ends at its time cap with the call cut; an expiry at the cap or the ceiling is the loop's cut and replays as one, and an expiry with time left is a `knowledge` failure; the outage check records the client's own expiry as `blocked` and timed out, never as `no answer`.

**L17. A tool server's hang past a declared per-call bound is the platform's, not the agent's time, and the bound never reaches a code cell.** With `[agent] tool_server_call_bound_s` declared, a call to a tool server (a provider with a true `tool_server` attribute, `McpToolProvider`) is handed `min(the bound, the agent's time left)` as its `timeout_s`. When the bound is the shorter and the provider runs out of it (`CallTimedOut`), the call is `failed` as `knowledge` infrastructure and the attempt is set aside, never the agent's time cap; when the time left is the shorter, L16's cut decides, a time cap or the ceiling's infrastructure. Every other call, a code cell above all, is handed the time left whatever is declared, what the model is told of a cell that ran long is the text it has without the key, and a call to such a tool that the loop cuts is never judged by the bound. Before the key, a hang inside the loop waited out the whole cap and was scored as the agent's time, so a study whose conditions differ in how many tools they call charged platform hangs to the agent unevenly; and the first form of the bound, `tool_call_bound_s`, was handed to every call, so a study that declared it to classify tool-server hangs also cut every agent code cell at it. Each started call's `elapsed_s` is in the record, so hangs and their cost can be seen per condition, and beside a declared bound `agent_config.tool_server_tools` names the tools it reached. A call to a tool server that the loop cuts at the cap or the ceiling at a reading at or after the bound's deadline had hung first and is judged by the bound too, so the winner does not depend on whether the provider's unwinding or the loop's cut ran first. A study that declares no bound keeps L16's behaviour, and its configuration hash, the record's `agent_config` and the campaign caps are the same bytes: the settings are left out, never a null. *Learned:* #235, a follow-up of #204 (L16), and the review of a study that declared the first form. *Test:* a hung call to a tool server under a declared bound ends as `knowledge` infrastructure having been handed the bound; a bound past the time left hands the time left and ends as a time cap; a call to a tool server cut at the cap after the bound passed, by a provider that honours it or one that ignores it, is `knowledge` infrastructure recorded `cut`, and one cut at the cap before it passed, or at a ceiling nearer than the bound, is the cap's or the ceiling's; the minimum is taken again at every call; a call within the bound is untouched; **a code cell, or any tool that is no tool server's, is handed the time left with the bound declared and is cut at the cap as the agent's time cap even after the bound passed, with the same text and the same record as without the key, through the code tool and the sandbox it runs in; one run with both kinds hands the bound to the tool server's tool alone; the real MCP client under a declared bound ends a hang at the bound;** an undeclared bound hands the time left as before; every started call records its elapsed time and a call that never started has none; the bound, the list of the tools it reached and `elapsed_s` are optional in version 3, refused for an earlier version and when not a finite positive number, a list of names written with the bound and only with it, or a non-negative number; a record written before them replays to its own bytes, a changed `elapsed_s`, bound or list diverges, and a list edited and sealed again diverges at the call; both orders replay, and a code cell cut after the bound replays as the agent's time cap; the sixth merge-point fixture pins the bound, the list and the elapsed times; `[agent] tool_server_call_bound_s` is read as seconds, refused when it is not positive and finite, is in the campaign's caps only when declared, and the old `tool_call_bound_s` is refused at load naming the new key.

## R. Records and replay

**R01. Records are written atomically and read back byte for byte.** Write to a temporary file, fsync, rename; a record written back equals its stored bytes; fields added by a later schema are never written into an older record. *Learned:* WWW `harness/agent/record.py:193-198, 341-351, 378-404` (a60bebc8, 18 Oct); ICSA `campaign/export.py:160-166`. *Test:* load and dump of every fixture record is byte-identical.

**R02. Unknown schema versions and fields are refused.** Validate before writing and at load. *Learned:* WWW `record.py:355-362`; ICSOC `runner.py:690-695` with `schema.py:315-812`. *Test:* an unknown top-level field fails load.

**R03. Replay re-derives every derived field without a model call.** Serve model turns, tool results and clock readings from the record; re-run the real loop; compare transcript, tool-call log, outcome, caps and verdicts; stop at the first divergence. *Learned:* WWW `harness/agent/replay.py` (13 divergence points, 39-454); ICSA had only re-execution of recorded code cells (`icsa/inspection/replayrun.py:402-499`). *Test:* WWW's divergence cases (`tests/test_agent_record_replay.py`) ported to the core format.

**R04. Analysis refuses records without a current replay check.** The check is bound to the code identity and a hash of every input; a check made under other code, with changed inputs, or with an older schema is refused. **A record is known by its name, and every other file of a batch is accounted for or fails the check.** A record is a file named as the core writes one, `<SHA-256 of the run key>.<attempt>.json` (`records.record.is_record_name`, the one form `record_filename` has had in every version, so every older record is still found), and nothing else is read as one, whatever its suffix or content. Every other file is a hashed-only input (an outcome marker, the campaign's snapshot, a declared side file, R14), the check file at the batch's top, or a dot-name (an interrupted write's temporary, which the check never reads, R15). Anything else is unexpected: not hashed, not replayed, and a failure of the check against its own path at stage `unexpected`, counted apart from the records. `merge` refuses a shard holding one, naming each file; `require_current_check` walks the batch again and refuses one added since, since such a file changes no input's hash; `archive_batch` and an anonymised package's build refuse one too, and `rescore_files` and `archived_records` read records by name. An unexpected file fails the check rather than only being reported because the check vouches for every byte that the analysis and a package read from `out/runs`: a file it passed over would be evidence nothing vouches for. Before, a stray `.json` file failed the check as a record that does not load, and a file of any other suffix was passed over in silence. It is never a file the study did not cause (R15): the core writes everything under a dot-name first and renames it, so a kill leaves only dot-names; reporting it writes nothing beside the batch (R16) or into a shard (#305). A study's own evidence enters a run directory only as a declared side file. *Learned:* WWW `harness/agent/replay_check.py:838-918` (3d1a7965, 18 Oct; 2c1d4fc4, 20 Oct). The offline rehearsal of the HPC path (PR #328): a plugin that collects its sandbox's metadata into `request.run_dir` had each file read by `check` and `merge` as a record that does not load, and one of another suffix would have been passed over. *Test:* each refusal case of `require_current_check`; a stray `.json` file and a file of any other kind each failing at `unexpected` and never read as a record; a link to a directory (which the walk does not enter and a merge's copy would follow, a dot-name included), a dangling link and a FIFO each unexpected; a record's bytes under another name not replayed; a stray added after a passing check refused by the analysis, the archive and an anonymised build; the check file, a dot-name and the hashed-only files not unexpected; the backends a batch needs read from its records alone; the golden records and every version-3 fixture, laid out under their record names, passing; an archive yielding its records alone; `rescore_files` re-scoring records by name only; a merge of shards with a stray refused naming each file; a merged batch's own files neither records nor unexpected; and the rehearsal's case, a plugin writing evidence into `run_dir`, reported as unexpected and accepted once declared.

**R05. Decisions served during replay are recomputed offline.** *Learned:* WWW `replay_check.py:325-351` (bee4404a, 20 Oct). *Test:* a recorded decision that the offline recompute contradicts holds the batch.

**R06. The echoed request is pinned by a digest.** *Learned:* WWW `replay.py:116-132` (c8b3f32d, 21 Oct). *Test:* tampering with recorded reasoning diverges on replay.

**R07. Records hold no interpreter-specific text.** *Learned:* WWW records hold Python 3.12 error strings and replay only under 3.12 (`tests/test_real_records_roundtrip.py:51-54`). *Test:* exception text is stored as a class name plus a stable message.

**R08. Every archived record set is covered by the roundtrip test, and no output is archived without its replay check.** *Learned:* WWW sharded archives escaped the test glob, and a runner killed by SIGTERM left no replay check (`results/dev-natural-20261020/README.md:34, 42`). *Test:* every `records*.tar.gz` is collected; archiving an output without a check fails.

**R09. An infrastructure failure keeps its partial transcript.** *Learned:* ICSA `runner.py:413-431` (`infra.json` with usage seen so far). *Test:* an endpoint failure at turn 3 leaves turns 1-2 and their usage.

**R10. Unknown is `None`, never 0.** Usage, byte counters, tokens into context and undefined metrics. *Learned:* ICSA `records.py:92`, `sandbox/sandbox.py:1294-1322`, `scoring/cost.py:23-42`; WWW `providers.py:17-28`. *Test:* a missing counter serialises as `null` and is excluded from means.

**R11. Run keys are unique and replicates are real.** *Learned:* PPAM appended duplicate rows when a run id was reused (`run_panel.py:83-84`, `_trial_artefact.py:76-89`); ICSOC's `run_sweep(trial_k=N)` ran one trial per cell and recorded `trial_k=1` (`harness/sweep.py:110-148`, `sweep_driver.py:116-147`, still in ICSOC-pkg). *Test:* expanding a factor schema with three seeds yields three distinct run keys; a duplicate key is refused.

**R12. Secrets never reach a record.** Redact environment-named secrets in strings and keys; never record token values (only id, subject and caveat types). *Learned:* WWW `record.py:189-304` (bca7e5e9, 18 Oct); ICSA `icsa/knowledge/tokencheck.py:42, 73`. *Test:* a planted key value is absent from the written record and a warning is raised.

**R13. Re-scoring is offline and versioned.** Parser and scorer changes re-score saved records without model calls; the parser version is recorded, with a content hash when git is absent. *Learned:* PPAM `benchmark/rescore.py:128-197`; ICSA `icsa/scoring/parser_version.py:13-26`. *Test:* re-scoring a fixture corpus needs no network and records the parser version.

**R14. A study's declared side files are verified with its records, fail-closed.** A file a run writes beside its record, which the record names by SHA-256 and the analysis reads, is declared in `study.toml` (`[[study.side_files]]`). The replay check hashes it into its inputs and pairs it with the one record in its directory, and the analysis and a replication package's `make check` verify it again. A side file that is named but missing, changed, swapped, beside a record that names no hash for it, or left where no record is, and a hash that is not 64 lower-case hex digits, are refused. The check records the declaration, so a check made under another one is refused too. A study that declares none is checked as before. A scorer that needs a side file's content is handed the bytes the loader verified, never a path, and a side file over the cap the loader reads is refused. *Learned:* #109 (the first study on the core wrapped the check to verify its compressed per-run logs, because `check`, `analyze` and a package's `make check` passed with one changed, removed or added); #144 (its scorer computed a measure from a side file it opened itself, after the analysis had verified the file). *Test:* each refusal through `check`, `analyze` and an unpacked package's `make check`, a passing study end to end, and a check file without a declaration unchanged; a scorer of side files scoring alike through `analyze`, `regenerate`, `rescore_files` and `make check`, a changed side file refused before any scorer runs, and the cap.

**R15. A declared side file is placed so that a kill is recoverable, and `check` never refuses what the study did not cause.** A side file taken from the sandbox's `/work` is written under a temporary dot-name the check never reads (`.<pattern>.tmp-<attempt>`), then the record that names its SHA-256 is written, then the file is renamed to its declared name; the helper refuses to rename before the record names it, a name the declaration does not hold, and a file over the cap. `check` settles what a kill leaves: it renames a temporary file whose hash the one record beside it names, and, holding the campaign's lock, removes a temporary file no record names (a check made while a dispatcher runs removes nothing, since an attempt that is staging has no record yet and its partial hash is not a record's). It never renames a file with another hash, in a directory of several records, or over a file already in place (the commit overwrites none either), and the pairing still verifies every file in place, so a wrong file is never accepted. **`merge` settles each shard in a private copy of its records before it replays them, and `check` of a merged batch settles that batch, the same way** (#304): a rename a record names is finished, a partial file no record names is dropped from the merge's copy (nothing else writes there) but removed from nothing else, a directory that cannot be written is reported, never written to, and a temporary file of other bytes is never renamed, so the replay still refuses it. A shard's `out/` is never written to by a merge, refused or not, so a shard fetched read-only is merged as it is. *Learned:* #248 (the first real study needed `extent_ranges.json.gz` beside each record; a file written to its final name before the record orphans on a kill, which `check` refuses until removed by hand, and one renamed after the record leaves a hash with no file); #304 (a merge of shards copied back from another host was refused as missing a side file that `check` run on the shard first would have renamed). *Test:* a kill after the temporary file, after the record and after the rename, each ending in a passing check and a second pass that finds nothing to do; a wrong-hash temporary file never renamed; a check made while a dispatcher runs; the size and name refusals; a directory that cannot be written reporting what it left and changing nothing; a merge of a shard killed after its record (finished in the merged batch, whose check passes), of a sealed one (every directory read-only), of one holding a redundant copy and of one holding a partial file, each leaving the shard's `out/` byte for byte, with its paths and modes; a wrong-bytes temporary file still refused, and a refused merge writing into no shard; the same states through `check` of a merged batch, which makes no lock file and touches nothing outside `runs/`.

**R16. Reading a finished or sealed store writes nothing beside it.** The campaign keeps its store in WAL mode for good, and an open of a WAL database in place, a read-only one included, makes its `-shm` and `-wal` beside it and fails in a read-only directory. A finished store (no dispatcher holds it) is read from a private copy: its file and its `-wal` (never its `-shm`), each signed before and after the copy by device, inode, size, modification and change times and the SHA-256 of its contents, the bytes copied required to hash to both, copied again when either changed and refused, naming the file, when every bounded attempt saw a change; the copy is opened read-write in a temporary directory, so the `-wal` replays, and removed after. `check`, `merge` and `run --after` read a store so, under a reader's shared hold of its lock, the existing lock file opened read-only and never made, held across the copy. A store a dispatcher holds is refused by `merge` and `--after`, and by `check` unless `--partial`, which backs the live store up in place over a read-only connection, the one consistent read of a store being written. A store whose lock cannot be held (the file missing, unreadable, or on a file system without `flock`) is refused by all three, `--partial` included. The sealed store is the snapshot the check hashes: one rollback-journal file, which every later reader (the analysis, `report`, `regenerate`, the check of a merged batch, a package's build and its own check) takes as bytes and opens in memory. So a read-only `out/` is read as it is, and no read changes a store's directory, a batch, a merged batch or an unpacked package by a byte. *Learned:* a sweep of another study found that a read-only open of a WAL store still creates `-shm` and `-wal`; the audit that followed found the check's, the merge's and `--after`'s reads leaving them in a store's `out/`, failing on a read-only one (shards copied back from other hosts may arrive so), and `--after` making a lock file beside any file it was given, a package's snapshot included. *Test:* `check`, `merge` and `--after` on a read-only `out/`, each leaving it byte for byte; a killed dispatcher's rows that only its `-wal` holds, read through the copy; a store changing during the copy copied again, and one that keeps changing refused, also on tmpfs by a writer that changes bytes in place and keeps size, modification time and inode, and with every stat field frozen, by the content hash alone; a live store refused by `check` without `--partial` and backed up in place with it, and refused by `merge`; `--after` given a sealed snapshot writing nothing beside it; a missing lock file refused by `check` (with `--partial` too), `merge` and `--after`, an unreadable one by `merge`, an unlockable one by `check --partial`; the reader's lock never making its file, shared between readers and refused by a dispatcher's; the snapshot in rollback-journal mode; and `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and an unpacked package's own check leaving their directories byte for byte.

**R17. An analysis built without the platform scores with the scorers the run had, and a run never builds that way.** A plugin module may define `build_analysis(context)` beside `build`, for a study whose `build` needs a platform that is gone when a batch is analysed (`study.analysis_plugin`). The analysis paths (`analyse_study`, so `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and a package's check) call it when it is defined and `build` when it is not; no start, resume, shard or `campaign_fingerprint` does, and only the loader's own module and `_analyse_loaded` name it. Only its scorers are read and checked. The campaign fingerprint's `scorer_digest` (names and versions) is compared at analysis, in every snapshot of a merged batch: another digest is refused before any record is scored, naming the analysis plugin's scorers and never a digest or version, and so is a snapshot that records no valid digest (every fingerprint the core writes holds one; a store without one was changed or is damaged), so the outputs are the same bytes whichever builder serves an analysis that is not refused and re-scored nothing. An analysis built by `build` is never compared (R13's re-scoring after a scorer change). A scorer corrected after the run is analysed on purpose with `analyze --rescore` and `report --rescore` (`allow_rescore=True`), which relaxes only a digest that differs: a store that records no valid digest, or no snapshot, is refused with it, and the re-score is recorded, never silent, as R13 records one (the corrected scorers' versions in `scores.json`, `analysis.json` and the report's `Scorers` line) with the digests the snapshots recorded and the digest of the scorers used (`analysis.json` `rescore`, one line of the report's provenance), while an analysis that re-scored nothing has the bytes it has without the flag. A replication package of a re-scored batch is built and checked with no flag, since the decision was recorded at `analyze`: the package's build, check and anonymised analysis pass the `rescore` entry of the shipped `analysis.json` to the guard (`regenerate(honour_shipped_rescore=True)`, `analyse_study(shipped_rescore=...)`), which lets a differing digest through exactly when the entry equals what it would record now (the snapshots' digests and the analysis plugin's), refuses any other entry or none as an analysis does, and relaxes nothing else; the byte comparison of the shipped outputs stays the guard. *Learned:* the coordinator's request of 17 Nov 2026 and its extension the same day, for the next study's HPC batches: its live `build` needs the rendered federation, which no longer exists when a batch is analysed after copy-back, and analysis needs only the scorers; two builders that could score differently would make the analysis a different one from the run's. *Test:* a run, a resume, a shard and the fingerprint without a start call `build` and never `build_analysis`, while `analyze` and `report` call `build_analysis` and never `build`, and an AST scan of `src/` and `deploy/` finds no other file naming the analysis loader; with `build` failing as it does without the federation, `analyze`, `report` and `regenerate` still give the bytes they gave before; one plugin file with both builders gives byte-identical outputs whichever serves the analysis; another version, an added scorer or none is refused naming the scorers, with no digest or version in the message and nothing written; the same scorers pass under `build`; a module without `build_analysis` loads by its `build`; a `build_analysis` that is not a function or gives no plugin is refused naming it; an analysis plugin needs no prompt, tool or backend, only distinct scorers; a store without the digest, a blank, malformed or upper-case one, or no snapshot is refused counting the snapshots, even for the same scorers and beside snapshots that agree, while the same store analysed by `build` is not; another digest is named before one that records none; with the flag, a corrected scorer analyses and `analysis.json` and the report hold the corrected versions, both digests and the line in the provenance, `regenerate` derives the same bytes only with it, and none of these is there when the digests agree or the module has no `build_analysis`, where every output has the bytes it has without the flag; without the flag the refusal is as before and says that `--rescore` exists; with the flag a store without a digest, no snapshot, and a snapshot without one beside one that differs are all refused; the flag is an option of `analyze` and `report` only; a re-scored batch builds a package that passes its own check, `package-check` and `make check` with no flag, ships the analysis bytes the study wrote, and so does an anonymised one; a corrected scorer with no `rescore` entry is refused by the build, the check and the anonymiser with the guard's message (the scorers and the flag, no digest) and no archive; an entry for the scorers of a third set, one whose run digests are not the store's, or one checked with the run's own scorers again is refused by the guard or fails the byte comparison, as does an entry on a batch that re-scored nothing; an entry equal to the record relaxes only the differing digest (a snapshot with no digest or no snapshot is still refused) and an entry that is not a mapping, has an extra key or another shape is refused; `analyze` does not honour the entry; a batch that was not re-scored packages and checks as it did.

## C. Campaign and failure accounting

**C01. An API failure is never scored as the agent's.** *Learned:* PPAM scored a returned adapter error as PASS or FAIL (`openai_compat.py:139-150` into `trial_runner.py:124-131`); ICSOC excluded leg errors as no-data (`schema.py:304-313`; 861596c). *Test:* an adapter error is infrastructure and absent from rates.

**C02. The infrastructure-kind vocabulary is closed and survives to the export.** *Learned:* ICSA folds `knowledge` and `unattributed_kill` into `sandbox` at the campaign boundary (`icsa/harness/run.py:113-116`, `campaign/errors.py:4`), so `retries_by_kind` cannot show them (`campaign/export.py:153-154`) although `loop.py:55` and `docs/harness.md:180` promise it. *Test:* every harness kind appears under its own name in the export.

**C03. Attempts are charged by cause.** Infrastructure attempts count against the attempt limit; key rejections, outage-discarded attempts and interrupted attempts do not. *Learned:* ICSA `campaign/store.py:7-9, 208-211`, `scheduler.py:2285-2298`. *Test:* a mixed sequence charges exactly the infrastructure attempts.

**C04. A kill at any point resumes cleanly.** Running attempts become interrupted at the next open and are never charged; an outcome marker written before the kill is committed, never re-run. *Learned:* ICSA `store.py:192-201`, `scheduler.py:2498-2542` (41fbfc8e, 1 Nov; `tests/campaign/test_killresume.py`). *Test:* crash injection before the marker, after the marker and after the metadata write.

**C05. An interrupted attempt costs what its call log recorded and its request in flight, or its bound, never 0.** With a budget, each attempt keeps a call log as it runs (`campaign.interrupted_spend`): its spend guard counts each request in the store (`calls_sent`) once it is cleared and before it goes out, and adds each reply's counts (`calls_usage`: input, output, cache writes, cache reads; `null` for a reply the endpoint billed and the backend could not read; `"failed"` for a request whose last send failed after it may have been billed, closed in the same transaction as that send's top-up, so the top-up pays for it once and it is never in flight again), each in a transaction of its own, written to the spend journal after it commits as a `call` line of its own, with the attempt's id, the campaign and the shard, a request's before it goes out (O62), except the close of a failed last send, which rides on its top-up's own `attempt` line (`closes`), so a write cut short loses both or neither; the `attempt` lines name how many call lines they follow (`calls_sent`, `calls_closed`), and a resume sums the call lines by attempt id, a set that disagrees with its attempt lines charging the bound and one that names an attempt the journal never began, or another run, refusing the start, so the journal grows linearly in the calls. Recovery records the basis an interrupted attempt is charged on (`spend_basis`): `measured` (every request answered: the replies' counts at the model's price, each request at its own tier), `measured_plus_inflight` (one request sent and not answered: that plus one request's bound, the most one request can cost, `request_bound_usd`), or `bound` (its whole bound, as before) when no log was kept, the log holds no request (a kill before the first request and an executor whose requests bypass the recording look alike), a reply left a count out or was not read, more than one request is unanswered, there are more replies than requests, a count is no count, or the log cannot be read. A store whose ledger names a spend journal, or that was copied back to run again (`snapshot.rebase_run_dirs`, which a snapshot's restore and the shard driver's archive resume call, marks it `copied_back_utc` in its meta), may be a snapshot whose log the attempt outgrew, so recovery records `bound` there, and only the start's count of the store's own journal (`lost_spend.reconcile`) gives it the journal's log, the longer when one extends the other (`bound` when neither does), with a `spend_basis_confirmed` event; a snapshot resumed without its journal keeps the bound. An attempt the journal lists and the store lacks is charged from its journaled log (`lost_in_kill` says the basis). The gate charges a row by its recorded basis only while its log still says it, else its bound, and never caps the measured part: nothing recorded is charged less. Top-ups are added on top; an interrupted attempt stays out of the mean cost of a run however it was charged. The export lists each interrupted attempt with its basis (`interrupted_spend`, only once one records a basis) and the report counts them per model. **An attempt whose executor raises after its model loop** (a post-run guard finding a leak in a tool answer: the record `record_run` built is lost with the exception) used to be recorded with unknown usage and charged its whole bound. The recording tells the attempt's spend guard when `run_agent` returns (`models.spend.after_loop`, `AttemptSpend.loop_ended`), and only an attempt that raises past that point is settled from its log (`campaign.after_loop.settle_usage`): when the log is complete, that is it holds at least one request and every request in it is closed with its counts (a reply's input and output known, or the request closed by a failed last send, whose top-up is its cost), the attempt keeps the replies' counts as its usage, complete, with the basis `measured` on its row (`spend_basis`) and in its marker, and is charged what they cost; when the log is unreadable or incomplete (none kept or not readable, no request in it, a request sent with no end entry, a reply not read or left a count out) its usage stays unknown, it is charged its bound as before, and its row says `bound`. A request in flight is the bound here, not `measured_plus_inflight`, which only an interrupted attempt is charged. An attempt that raises during its loop, with a request in its log, never gets a basis and keeps today's usage, unknown, charged its bound; so does one without a budget (no guard, no log). **An attempt that failed with no request in its complete log** (any failure before the loop ended: a fault that could not be induced, a state error, a failure of the executor before its first request) used to be recorded with unknown usage and charged its bound unless it was a first-turn refusal (C31). It is now charged nothing when its call log shows no request and nothing contradicts it (`campaign.no_request.no_request_in`, the one place that decides it): the log is readable and consistent with no request begun, no reply and no request closed by a failed send, the row took no top-up, and the backend's own count (`requests_sent`, an optional attribute that the core's adapters keep) is exactly 0: the log sees only the requests the core's recording makes, so an empty log alone cannot be told from that of an executor that sends another way, and a backend with no count, or a count that is None, above 0, no whole number or unreadable, confirms nothing. Such an attempt keeps zero tokens and no turn, complete, with the basis `measured` on its row (`spend_basis`) and in its marker and a `usage_measured_zero` event whose reason is "no model request in a complete journal", and the budget gate's spent figure, the export and the report count 0 USD for it. A log that is unreadable or incomplete (a request begun and not closed), a send with no reply (it may have been billed in full), a top-up and a backend that counted a request keep the bound and record no basis, as before; an attempt past its loop is the case above, whose empty log is a contradiction and charges the bound, and an attempt killed before its result was stored is recovery's. A study's own backend that keeps no `requests_sent` keeps the bound for such an attempt, as before. This changes the charge recorded for such an attempt: the budget gate, `usage_complete` and a done run's measured cost (`mean_run_usd`) see the measured figure. Without a budget no log is kept and nothing changes. *Learned:* ICSA `campaign/attempt_bounds.py:1-14` (82359471, 4 Nov). Another study's pilot (16 Nov): with a 60 USD ceiling and a 32.975 USD bound for one model, one attempt killed in flight was charged its whole bound, so the resumed batch held that model's remaining runs ("65.95 USD ... over the ceiling of 60.00 USD") though the attempt had spent a fraction; the same hits an HPC shard killed mid-run, whose spend journal measured only attempts that ended. The loop writes no turn as it goes (the record is written at the end), so no partial transcript existed to read: the log is new. The Codex review of the branch found a snapshot of a store never journaled trusted as the live store, and a request whose last send failed charged twice (its top-up, and again in flight). The coordinator's review then asked for a line per call in the journal, in place of attempt lines that each repeated the whole log, which grew the journal with the square of an attempt's calls; its Codex delta review found that a close written as a line after its top-up's charged the request twice when a write was cut between them, so the close rides on the top-up's line. A study's pilot: a post-run guard that found a leak in a tool answer raised after the loop, and the attempt, whose every request had been answered and journaled, was charged its bound. The same pilot (17 Nov): a model's bound was about half its ceiling, and two attempts that failed at start, before any request, were each charged that bound, so the model's budget was spent with nothing sent and its batches were held. The call log of those attempts held no request. *Test:* two replies recorded and a request in flight are charged the replies and one request's bound; a kill mid-call charges that request's bound; every request answered charges the replies, and the attempt is left out of the mean; an unreadable, truncated, inconsistent or count-less log, an unread reply and a log with no request charge the bound; no log records no basis; a journaled store, or an unreadable ledger, records `bound` at recovery; a row whose log no longer says its basis charges the bound; cache parts and tiers are priced as a finished attempt's; random logs are never charged less than recorded; the guard counts a request only once cleared, and the recording hands it each reply's cache parts and an unread reply as unread; the store refuses a note for an attempt not running or without a log; through the scheduler, a dispatcher killed after its replies charges them at the restart; the journal has a call line per request and reply, with the attempt's id, the campaign and the shard, and grows linearly in the calls; a call line of an attempt the journal never began or of another run refuses the start, and attempt lines that disagree with their call lines charge the bound; a failed last send's top-up and close are one line, so a write cut short charges it once, in flight, and a whole one charges its top-up once; a request whose last send failed (its retries ran out, or its resend did not fit) is charged its top-up once; a restored snapshot's attempt is charged by its own journal's longer log, a snapshot resumed without its journal keeps the bound, a store copied back to run again without a journal does not trust its own log while the store that ran it does, a journal that disagrees charges the bound, and a live store ahead of its journal keeps its own; a lost attempt is charged by its journaled log and `lost_in_kill` and its report say the basis; the export and the report show each basis, a merged export with each shard; and the pilot's case (a 60 USD ceiling, a 32.975 USD bound, one attempt killed in flight) holds the next run charged at the bound and admits it charged by the log; an attempt that raises after its loop keeps its replies' measured usage, its row and marker say `measured`, and its charge is below its bound; an unreadable log, one with no request, a request with no end entry, an unread reply, a reply without a count and no log at all charge the bound and say `bound`; a raise during the loop that sent a request and one without a budget keep the bound and record no basis; a failure before any request, an induction failure (an `InfraError`) or a state error (a plain exception), is charged 0 with the basis `measured`, leaves the budget gate's spent figure unchanged, is counted complete by the export and has its reason as an event, and two such failures leave the budget of a model whose bound is about half its ceiling able to run every run; a log that is unreadable, one with a request begun and not closed, a send with no reply or one closed by a failed send, a reply count above the requests, an attempt with a top-up and a backend that counted a request the log never saw (an executor that sends outside the recording) charge the bound and record no basis, and so does a backend that keeps no count, with or without a request sent outside the recording; a kill after the marker commits the zero and the basis; an attempt without a budget keeps no log and its usage unknown; the decision itself is a function of the log, the top-up and the backend's count; a kill after the marker commits the measured counts and basis; a marker without the basis still reads, and one naming another basis is refused.

**C06. A campaign never resumes into a different experiment.** Refuse a resume whose manifest fingerprint (tasks, conditions, models, caps, prompt and tool hashes, scorer digest) or pinned sandbox image differs. *Learned:* WWW `run_pilot.py:1413-1645`; ICSA `store.py:136-153`, `scheduler.py:328-338` (7c70f8f0, 1 Nov). **A space is the same experimental condition across federations when its declared identity and its data identity match.** An entry of `[[spaces]]` that declares its data (`data_manifest`, a manifest of every file with its size and SHA-256) is fingerprinted by its label, name, prefixes, support, data provider, token planes and import declaration and by that manifest's content hash (`StudySpace.digest`), never by its Onezone id, which every federation's `up` makes anew, nor by a path: shards of two federations holding the same data are one campaign, and one file different makes them two. An entry that declares no data keeps the digest it always had, the Onezone id in it, so shards on two federations whose data is unknown never merge, and every store started before the key existed resumes unchanged. The one-space form (`[space]`), whose fingerprint held nothing of its space, so that its shards on two federations merged whatever data they held (only a plugin's environment identity caught it), takes the same opt-in key: with it the fingerprint gains the space's data identity (`space_data`), and without it the fingerprint is exactly as it was. A store recorded under that id-bearing form whose spaces now declare their data resumes under its recorded fingerprint, unchanged, when it is exactly the campaign's with the platform's half in that form (`StudyPlugin.platform_legacy`, `PlatformConfig.legacy_fingerprint()`); the merge compares the shards' recorded fingerprints as they are, so a store of the earlier form never merges with one of the later. *Learned:* the offline rehearsal of the HPC path (16 Nov 2026): the spaces' digests held their Onezone ids, so two shards on two federations of one layout would have been two campaigns as soon as the study's plugin declared its platform. **The platform's fingerprint holds every declared setting that shapes a run, and nothing that is one federation's own.** `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` holds each run-shaping setting of `platform.toml` after its default, in one canonical form: the token lifetime, the one-space form's support, name and prefixes, the sandbox's images (by id), limits, mount, trust paths, interpreter, user, kill grace and placeholder (hashed), the tool server's commit, start bound and conditions, the data provider, range map and the placement bounds a run's instruments read during the run, and the fault injector's targets and every bound of its restore and heal; never an address, id, port, host, network or Compose project name, path, secret, a host's own command, or what is checked before a campaign (`expcore_onedata.runsettings`). Every key the loaders accept is classified in or out, so a new one cannot stay out unseen. *Learned:* another study's tests at 655ea0a6 (16 Nov 2026): of 23 declared settings tried, 20 were not in the fingerprint, so two shards on different limits or fault timings could have merged once studies fingerprinted the platform by `fingerprint()` instead of the rendered file. *Test:* changing any fingerprinted input refuses the resume; two federations holding the same data give one platform fingerprint whatever their ids, and their shards merge; one file different gives another and the merge refuses; an entry without its data keeps the old digest byte for byte; the earlier form has every entry by its old digest; a manifest that cannot be read or checked is refused naming the key, never the path; a store of the earlier form resumes under it with its record unchanged, the later form alone or another earlier form is refused, and the merge refuses an earlier-form shard beside a later-form one. **An ingested space on an HPC federation declares its data (HPC driver).** A space whose data comes from the study's own ingest, after `up` (`baseline = false`, no import, its `@data_manifest.<space>@` named by the platform template), holds nothing at `up`, so a manifest made from its baseline is empty, and two federations that ingested different bytes fingerprinted alike, which defeated the data identity above (#326) for it. The space now declares its manifest (`data_manifest` on the space in the layout, absolute or under `@study_artifacts@`, in the core's import-manifest format, so `Manifest.digest()` is the hash the fingerprint holds), `up` renders that path and loads it before any job is held, refuses a rendered file whose entry for a space names a `data_manifest` of the template's own (nothing would check it), and holds one that declares none to the empty manifest. *Test:* an ingested space's declared manifest rendered for the space and loaded in the preflight (a missing or malformed one refused naming the space, never the path); two federations declaring the same manifest give one platform fingerprint whatever their ids, a manifest with one file different gives another, and the same path edited gives another; the layout's key refused on a baseline space, on an import and when it is no absolute path or one under the placeholder of plain names; a template that hard-codes a `data_manifest`, or points a non-baseline space at the root's own manifest, refused; an ingested space not checked at `up` and the baseline spaces still checked. Two renders of one template on two federations (their own addresses, ids, ports, names and roots) give one fingerprint holding none of those values; changing any one setting classified in gives another; a setting at its default and one left out, `2` and `2.0`, and a set in another order give one; and a guard reads every key each loader's `reject_unknown` accepts and fails on one neither in nor out. **The core is named by its code, however it is installed.** The environment the fingerprint holds records the core by its identity (`provenance.core_identity`): `{kind: core, commit, tree, dirty, python_sha256}`, its commit, its git tree, whether that tree was dirty and a digest of its Python source (`expcore` and `expcore_onedata`, by path below the directory holding them, the example study left out). It is read live from git when the imported core is the `src/` of a checkout whose top is the core's own project (an editable install), and otherwise from `expcore/_build_identity.json`, which the build hook (`hatch_build.py`) writes into the wheel and the sdist from the same reading of the checkout it builds from, and which a wheel built from an sdist takes from the sdist. Dirty is a file a build reads (`core_identity.builds_from`: `src/`, `examples/tiny_study/`, the project and hatch configuration and build files, the readme, the ignore rules hatchling reads, the token hashes) that is not HEAD's, read from the files themselves (each hashed as git would and compared with HEAD's tree: changed, new whether git ignores it or not, removed, its mode changed, bytecode caches aside), so no ignore rule or index flag hides one, or a link there or on the way there (`src` or the example's directory made a link to an identical copy is dirt) (git holds where it points, not what it leads to; the digest follows it); a change elsewhere (a note, a test, the session log this project's hooks keep writing) and the marker uv leaves at the top of its git checkouts reach no build and no import and are no dirt; an installed core is re-hashed on every read, and one whose source differs from its build's digest is stale, which is dirty; a core with neither git nor a build identity has an unknown commit. So a resume, check or merge under another commit of one version is refused, and editable, git-URL, directory, wheel and sdist installs of one commit record one entry. A store an earlier core recorded holds the core by how it was installed (a git URL's commit, an editable checkout's digest, or nothing for a wheel or a directory: `environment.is_earlier_form`); it resumes, is checked, analysed and packaged with the core in use read in that same form, exactly as the earlier core compared it (`study.core_legacy`), saying so on stderr, so it resumes, checks and analyses exactly where the earlier core would have: a store of an editable or git-URL install only under the same code, one of a wheel, directory or index install under any core of its version, since that form never named the code (a refused resume says so), and every new campaign records the identity. A new merge takes no shard of the earlier form, which does not say which core ran it (C29); a batch an earlier core merged is still checked and analysed in that form. *Learned:* a non-editable install of the core named no code, so shards run on different core code could merge, and one commit installed two ways recorded two entries, so shards that were one campaign did not (16 Nov 2026). *Test:* one commit installed editable, from a git URL, from a directory, from a wheel and from the wheel of its sdist records one entry naming its HEAD and tree, and a second commit at 0.1.0 another; a dirty build says dirty; two dirty trees at one commit differ; an installed core edited since its build is stale; a repository around an unpacked core is never taken for the core's; a resume at another commit is refused naming both; an earlier-form store resumes unchanged, is checked and analysed, and one whose install now reads otherwise in that form is refused; a Python file git ignores in the packages, or a link there, is dirt, and a linked subpackage's code is in the digest. **A shard never starts on a core that names no code.** `shards.py plan`, `lines` and `step`, and the fleet's `plan` and `step` (which build their lines themselves), resolve the core's identity as the run will (`provenance.core_identity`: through the manifest's `python` for `plan` and `lines`, in the step's own interpreter for `step`) and refuse an unknown or stale core before any step line is printed or anything is made on the node, naming the remedy: a git checkout of the core, or a wheel built with its build identity. A dirty core is the run's own refusal. *Learned:* the first real-model probe on the HPC allocation (17 Nov 2026): a core installed by `git archive` has no commit, `expcore run` refuses to start on it, and the shard reached its node, started and failed there. *Test:* a manifest whose interpreter imports a bare copy of the core, or a wheel's files edited since its build, is refused by `plan` and `lines` with nothing printed, and by `step` with nothing made (no step directory, no result); a git checkout and a wheel with its build identity are taken; an interpreter that cannot report the core's identity is refused, saying so; and a `step` command on a bare copy exits 2 with the remedy and leaves the node's tmpfs and the results empty; the fleet's `plan` refuses such a core with no line printed, no `shards.toml` and no shard assigned, its `step` launches nothing and records no launch, and a `step` with nothing to launch reads no core.

**C07. One dispatcher per store.** Take a non-blocking exclusive lock; warn on network filesystems where the lock is unreliable. *Learned:* ICSA `scheduler.py:211-216`; WWW `run_pilot.py:2067-2096`. *Test:* a second dispatcher on the same store exits.

**C08. Run order is seeded and stable when the plan grows.** *Learned:* WWW `run_pilot.py:1782-1855`. *Test:* adding runs keeps the relative order of existing ones; resuming with another order seed is refused.

**C09. Exhausted runs reach the analysis.** Runs that used all attempts are exported with reasons, so no denominator loses them. *Learned:* ICSA `campaign/export.py:1-16` (165f0f1d, 1 Nov). *Test:* an exhausted run appears in the export, and the analysis refuses an export without the key.

**C10. Harness bugs are neither infrastructure nor outcomes.** Re-queue from the same row and seed; three of a kind stop a model; a wider spread stops all. *Learned:* ICSA `scheduler.py:2314-2318`, `campaign/harness_stop.py:1-15`. *Test:* three harness errors for one model stop its dispatch.

**C11. Health that cannot be read is unhealthy.** A failed pre-run check holds all dispatch for one attempt per outage; an unreadable gate state at restart opens closed. A deferral is bounded by `max_defer_s`, and a check that declares the longest it may stay deferred (`max_deferral_s`, a `DeferralBound`) holds `max_defer_s` to it, with the core's own waits (`recheck_s`, `poll_interval_s`, `health_timeout_s`) it declares it includes: a shorter one is refused at the start, so a wait the platform ends by itself never becomes a blocking hold (O35). The platform declares that bound a second time, from its settings with no health object between (its run admission's `declared_deferral_s`), so a study's own check around the platform's that hides `max_deferral_s`, or declares less, is refused at the start too instead of holding the campaign to nothing. The platform declares it a third time, from its own configuration through the platform registry, where no object of the study's plugin sits between: a module `[study] platforms` lists may expose `declared_deferral_from_config(path)`, the core calls it with the platform configuration the study points at (`[study] platform_config`, by default the `platform.toml` beside study.toml) before any study code runs and gives the scheduler the answers (`Scheduler(platform_deferrals=...)`, `PlatformDeferral`), so a study that wraps its run admission as well as its check is still refused. The core also sees the platform being built, whatever the plugin hides: the platform's own loaders record, while the study's build is open, the module a study lists for the platform and the file they read (`expcore.config.platform_loads`), and the scheduler refuses a record of a module the study does not list, so a plugin that hides both declarations and lists nothing is refused too; a loader run outside a study build records nothing. A detail that leads with a reason only an operator can end (`OPERATOR_ACTION`, "operator action needed": the fault gate's after a failed heal, O35) reads as that hold, never as a wait that ends by itself: the status's alarms for the deferral and for the closed gate say it first and drop the words of a run holding the platform and of a gate that resumes by itself, the hold the run says carries the reason, and the export lists the outage (`operator_holds`) for the report's section "Holds that needed an operator", which a campaign with none does not have. *Learned:* ICSA `campaign/healthgate.py:1-21`, `scheduler.py:2031-2067`. *Test:* unreadable state blocks dispatch until a check passes; a `max_defer_s` shorter than the check's declared longest deferral is refused when the scheduler is built, before the lock or the store, naming the figures, the platform's words, each of the core's waits it declared with its value and the fix, a total equal to it starts and one second less is refused, a bound that declares no waits of the core's adds none, a check without the hook or answering None is held to nothing where the platform declares nothing, and an answer that is not a `DeferralBound` of a finite, non-negative number of seconds, a detail and non-negative whole counts is refused; a platform that declares a deferral (`declared_deferral_s` on its run admission) refuses a health check that exposes no `max_deferral_s` (a closure around the platform's), one that answers None and one that declares a smaller bound (fewer seconds, rechecks or checks), when the scheduler is built, before the lock or the store, naming what the check declares, the platform's figure and words and the fix, while a check that passes the method on, or declares the same or more, starts and is still held to `max_defer_s`; a platform that declares none, an admission without the method, no admission and no health check are unaffected; the same refusal comes from what the platform's module declares from its configuration (`PlatformDeferral`, read by the core and handed to the scheduler), naming the platform and what it declares, whatever the admission declares or hides (a study wrapping both its health check and its admission is refused, one that passes `max_deferral_s` on starts under a `max_defer_s` that covers it, a platform that declares none, no platform listed and no health check are unaffected, and an admission that declares more than the platform's configuration is still refused as before); a deferral that the run admission or the health check declares from a platform module the study does not list in `[study] platforms` (`DeferralBound.platform`, `Scheduler(listed_platforms=...)`) is refused when the scheduler is built, naming the module and saying to list it, before the lock or the store and with or without a health check, while a listed module (and the platform a listed module's own answer names, as a study's module that re-exports a platform's function speaks for it; one that answers nothing covers nothing), a bound that names no module or waits for nothing, a platform that declares none and a scheduler built with no list are unaffected, and a platform name that is not text is refused naming the method; the platform's own loaders record that they ran (`record_platform_load`, which another platform layer calls from its own loader; the Onedata layer's are `load_platform` and `load_faults`, recording `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds` and the file, a refused load included) in a registry that each study build opens empty (`watching_platform_loads`), and the scheduler refuses a record of a module the study does not list (`Scheduler(platform_loads=...)`, `check_loads_listed`) when it is built, naming the module and the file's name and saying to list it, before the lock or the store: a plugin that builds the platform through the core's loader, wraps both its health check and its admission so neither declares anything and lists nothing is refused, the same study listing the module starts, a study that builds no platform is unaffected, two study builds in one process do not inherit each other's entries, a loader run outside a study build (a platform's own tooling) is not recorded and not checked, a listed module covers its own platform and the platform a listed module's function ran the loader for to answer (`PlatformDeferral.loaded`: a study's module that re-exports a platform's function speaks for it even when it answers None, one that loads nothing covers nothing), and the record is the module and the path only, never a value, and no record or fingerprint depends on it (what is loaded after the build returned, a configuration made by hand without a loader, and one that an installed helper retained from an earlier build of the process, is outside it), and one study build is open in a process at a time (a second thread's build is refused at once with a RuntimeError, never made to wait, and the watch ends before the scheduler starts, so a campaign holds none), so a loader that runs while a build is open, a worker thread's included, records into it, and a build that loads nothing is never refused for another's platform; the core reads the declaration through the platform's module and not through an object of the study's: `[study] platform_config` names a path inside the study directory (a path or a link that leaves it is refused at load;
