by default the `platform.toml` beside study.toml, or none, and a default that links out still loads), the file is read only where a listed platform declares from it (a missing one, or one outside the study directory in a study built in code, is refused there, naming the key, and the platform is never asked to read it, so a replication package without its platform.toml still loads), a platform with `declared_deferral_from_config` that the study lists is asked with it, one without the function or one the study does not list is not, a study that lists such a platform and points at no configuration is refused naming the key, a hook that raises a `ConfigError` reaches the study as the platform worded it, any other exception names the platform and its type and never its text, and an answer that is not None or a `DeferralBound` of a finite, non-negative number of seconds, a detail and non-negative whole counts, or a hook that is not a function, is refused naming the hook; and a declaration that is not None or a `DeferralBound` of a finite, non-negative number of seconds, a detail and non-negative whole counts is refused, naming `declared_deferral_s`; a detail that leads with the reason (its first clause, up to the first semicolon) is worded as a hold in the deferral alarm and the closed-gate alarm and carried by the hold the run says, a deferral past `max_defer_s` and a closed gate alike, while any other detail, one that mentions the words later included, reads exactly as before; the export lists each such outage (the latest reason an answer of the outage led with, kept in the outage's record apart from its details, so a repair that opens the gate does not lose it, its start and end, whether it blocked and whether it charged a run) only when there is one, a merged export joins them with their shards, one shard's lacking the key or not, and the report's section is there only then, with a Shard column for a merged batch.

**C12. Unknown cost holds like over-budget.** Budgets are enforced, not only estimated. A hold over a ceiling is a stop a release can end: it names its serial and, with a release file, the line that ends it (C13). *Learned:* ICSA `campaign/gate.py:5-6`; WWW estimated cost per record but stopped on nothing (`run_pilot.py:1148`, `harness/agent/cost.py`). *Test:* a model without a price is held at the budget gate.

**C13. A release counts once and only for the stop it names.** Each stop, a model's stop or the all-stop on harness errors and a budget hold alike, is given a serial `<count>.<random>` in the transaction that records it, and its hold, its event and the status show it. A release file lists lines `<name> <serial>` (`m1 3.k7f2qd9a`, `ALL 3.k7f2qd9a`), and a line ends a stop only when it is that stop's exact line, and is used up when it does. The file's modification time decides nothing: it comes from the kernel's coarse clock or from another writer's or a file server's, so a release written a tick after the stop can carry an earlier time and a file written before it a later one. The count, kept in the store, gives order and makes the serial readable; the random part (40 bits) is there because a store copied back from a snapshot (O62) gives the count again, and a line written for a stop the copy lost must not end the next stop of that name. A bare name (the form before serials) or another stop's serial ends nothing and is said once, with the line that would end the stop in force; a stop recorded before serials is given one at the start, before anything can end it. *Learned:* ICSA `campaign/release.py:1-6`; the core's own mtime rule (a release counted only if the file was newer than the stop's microsecond clock) refused a release written 0.47 ms after a stop on an HPC node, whose file times run 0.5 to 1.5 ms behind the process clock, and ended a stop with a file written before it but stamped ahead. *Test:* a stale or reused release file does not resume dispatch; a line with an earlier serial, a bare name, or a file written before the stop stamped ahead of it releases nothing; the stop's own line releases it once whatever time the file carries, behind, equal or ahead; a restart keeps the serial and never gives one twice; a store from before serials is given serials at the start, refuses a bare name naming the line, and the line shown releases once; a store copied back to before a stop refuses the lost stop's exact line for the next stop of that name, whose own line releases it once; the budget hold the same.

**C14. Overrides reach execution.** The endpoint the campaign admitted is the one the run uses. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/harness/run.py:63-72` ("admission checked server A while the harness file named B"). *Test:* a campaign override of the base URL is the URL in the run record.

**C15. Endpoint health is tracked per unit.** Pause a unit after a threshold of failures; clear the pause only on a success. *Learned:* ICSA `scheduler.py:2411-2490`. *Test:* N failures pause the unit; one success clears it.

**C16. An endpoint whose identity changed during a run makes the run infrastructure.** *Learned:* ICSA `scheduler.py:2254-2255`. *Test:* a slot fingerprint change between start and end retries the run.

**C17. An unexpected worker exception stops dispatch loudly.** *Learned:* ICSA `scheduler.py:2160-2169`. *Test:* a raising worker stops dispatch and leaves its run for recovery.

**C18. Two conditions never share attempt keys.** *Learned:* WWW A/B arms of one route model skipped each other's attempts (the 21 Oct tolerances result README, lines 152-154). *Test:* two conditions with the same model produce disjoint run keys.

**C19. Leases on shared mutable resources fail safe.** A dead holder's key is not re-granted until its sandbox is shown gone; an unproven teardown quarantines the resource; with every resource quarantined the run is unstarted, using no attempt; a file lock does not cross hosts, so a key leased on two hosts is refused; an unreadable reservation reads as held. A leased platform slot is free once its sandbox is shown gone and its tokens have ended: by their journalled expiry plus a margin, or by a revocation at the run's end that is confirmed (each slot user's generation of temporary tokens rose, and every token the run held verifies as `tokenRevoked`) and settled (330 s by default, the worst case of the change reaching every provider; a settle below that bound, 315 s, is refused). The revocation waits for the sandbox and the tool server to stop and for no mint or start thread of the key to run, and any failure records no revoked line, so the expiry rule stands; a revoked line caps only the slot's lines before its start line, which is written before the first bump, so a mint journalled after it keeps its own end; `python -m expcore_onedata.lease revoke` does the same for a slot held from before while it holds the slot's lease lock, refusing one whose lock a run or a running campaign holds, or whose sandbox is not shown gone. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/harness/slot_lease.py:55-58, 112-146, 208-215, 280-319`, `reservation.py:15-16, 105-132` (1c525365, 1 Nov; f1c242a8, 3 Nov); the slot rule from a study's live batch on this core (13 Nov 2026), where every launched run held its slot for its tokens' whole lifetime, about 25 h at an 88,200 s ceiling, because no token was revoked. *Test:* each case with a fake resource and a fake reaper; the journal's cap, the probe, revoke, confirm, settle and free, each branch that fails closed, a start thread that blocks the revocation, and the command with its refusals, against the fake federation.

**C20. Store schema changes are crash-safe.** Add columns one at a time; read-modify-write shared metadata under `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`. *Learned:* ICSA `store.py:80-83, 111-122`. *Test:* a kill between two migrations reopens and finishes.

**C21. A snapshot of a live store is consistent.** Copy with SQLite's online backup API over a read-only connection (not `VACUUM INTO`, which fails on generated columns in older SQLite) and check integrity; a pulled run directory counts only with a marker matching its attempt; the snapshot code must equal the campaign's. *Learned:* ICSA-snap `snapshot/fetch.py:5-16`, `snapshot/codecheck.py:1-12`. *Test:* a partial pull is refused; a code mismatch is refused.

**C22. Model-level infrastructure faults are confirmed before relabelling.** A trigger only holds a model; a frozen probe confirms the fault. *Learned:* WWW `harness/agent/infra_fault.py:1-80`, `fault_probe.py:1-35` (96 attempts of one model excluded; 5f817def, 21 Oct). Follows in v0.2 ([release boundary](architecture.md#release-boundary)). *Test:* WWW `tests/test_infra_fault.py`.

**C23. The report accounts for every planned run and every pair.** Per condition: the planned runs and how each ended (analysed, exhausted, unfinished, left out) and the attempts set aside; per contrast: the pairs used and the pairs missing by cause (treatment outcome `None`, treatment excluded, exhausted run, unpaired or left out, other `None`); every condition in the infrastructure table, a row of zeros included. The same counts in `analysis.json` and `report.md`, and an export whose run lists do not add up to its plan is refused. *Learned:* the core's own end-to-end test of its example (issue #38): an exhausted run's condition was visible only in the export, a condition with no failure was missing from the infrastructure table, and the pairs a contrast lost were only in `analysis.json`. *Test:* a constructed export gives each condition's counts and zero rows; pairing separates an exhausted run from an unpaired one; the example's `analysis.json` and report agree.

**C24. A tiered price is applied request by request.** A model's price may be tiers by the request's prompt length, each with a prompt-token boundary and its own input, output and cache prices; a request, its reply included, takes the prices of the tier its prompt fell in. The store keeps each turn's usage, and the cost measure prices each request from it, so a run's cost is what its record's turns give; a tiered attempt without per-turn usage, or with a turn that did not report a count, is charged its bound, never priced at a guessed tier. A model without tiers is priced from its totals as before, and its entry, and so the campaign fingerprint, is unchanged. *Learned:* the first real study on the core (issue #94): a model whose price doubles past a prompt length could only be priced at one tier everywhere. *Test:* tiers load and each prompt finds its tier at the boundary; a table that does not price every prompt once is refused; two requests either side of the boundary cost each its own tier, in the store and from the record read back; a flat price is charged its totals to the bit and keeps its fingerprint; a tiered attempt without per-turn usage is bounded, and one known to hold no turn, a run that found an outage before it started, costs 0; the marker keeps the usage.

**C25. A run's reservation is the most its caps let it cost, the request past its token cap included.** The token cap counts each reply's input and output, summed over the run, and is checked before each request and after each reply, so a run can pass it by one request, whose prompt the context window bounds. A run makes at most one request more than its tool rounds. For a model held to its reply maximum (C27), the requests within the cap are reserved the worst of `input + output <= cap` with output at most the rounds times the reply maximum, at the dearest rates of any tier (input at the dearest of the input and cache prices), never more than the whole cap at the dearest rate, and the request past the cap the window at the input rate and one reply at the output rate. For any other model, the stub and a study's own backend among them, the whole cap and the whole window at the dearest rate; a stub's scripted usage beyond the window is outside the guarantee. With a budget, a run does not start or resume with a planned model that is not free and declares no context window; loading the study for a check, an analysis, a report or a package's check does not need one. *Learned:* the first real study on the core (issue #94): the whole cap at the dearest rate reserved more for one run of a cheap model under a generous cap than the campaign's ceiling, so every run was held; and its review found that a run can end one request past its cap, which no reservation covered, that a stub's reply is not held to the reply maximum, and that requiring the window at every load stopped the analysis of a study made before the rule. *Test:* the worked bound and its parts; output dearer and the replies filling the cap, input dearer; the stub keeps the cap and the window at the dearest rate, and a stub reply longer than its maximum is within the reservation; a run one full request past its cap is within its reservation; random prices, caps and runs that may pass the cap never cost more than their reservation, whose part within the cap is never above the old bound; a model with no window is refused by the gate and the scheduler, not without a budget or when free, and a finished study without windows still checks, analyses, reports and regenerates while `run` refuses it.

**C26. A send the token cap cannot account for reserves its own bound before it is sent.** Two kinds of send escape a run's reservation: a send that failed after it may have been billed (a connection error or a 5xx), which the retrier sends again, and every request after a reply that left its input or its output unreported, since the loop counts the missing count as 0 and the cap then no longer bounds the run. Each is charged a top-up of one request's bound, the bound of the request past the cap, at the dearest prices of any tier: reserved against the campaign's ceilings before the send goes out, under the dispatcher's locks, charged at once and never refunded; the last failed send of a request, after which nothing is sent again, is charged too. A top-up that does not fit is refused, the send is not made, and the attempt ends as infrastructure of kind `budget_hold`: set aside, never treatable, not charged an attempt, and counted per condition and model with the other set-aside attempts, in the export and in a table of the report with a row of zeros for each pair the plan runs. The refusal is the request's recorded failure, so the record replays. Admission's probes are sends too: with a budget, each send of a probe, retries included, is reserved one request's bound by the window before it goes out and charged in the store, an answered one settled to what its reply's counts cost; a probe that does not fit is not sent and its model is not admitted, its reason saying the budget is spent, and a start takes a release before its probes ask for room. The loop's token counting and `token_cap` are unchanged, and without a budget nothing is topped up. *Learned:* the review of the first real study's budget gate (issue #94): a retry of a billed send and the requests after a reply without usage could take a run past its reservation, and so the campaign past its ceiling. *Test:* the retrier tells the guard of each 5xx and connection error, not of a 429 or a 408, and of the last one with no resend; a refused resend is never sent; the recording clears each request and a refusal is its failure; a top-up that fits is spent at once and never refunded, one that does not is refused with the figure; eight top-ups asked for at once take only the room there is; a kill keeps the top-ups on both recovery paths; through the scheduler, a top-up that does not fit is a budget hold set aside, uncharged, among the condition's attempts set aside, in the export and in the report's table by condition and model, and its record replays, while one that fits is charged, and without a budget nothing changes; a campaign whose budget cannot cover a probe sends none and admits no model, at any start; each probe send is charged in the store, kept across a restart and settled to its reply's counts; a probe resent after a 5xx keeps the failed send's bound and reserves the resend, or is a budget hold unsent; a release is taken before the probes ask for room; an attempt held before any reply costs its top-ups alone, and the export counts its usage as complete; a row written before a billed reply that became no turn was checked, from a marker of the time or a store an upgrade brought forward, holds no turn and is still charged its bound; a running attempt whose worker crashed in our own bookkeeping is charged its bound while its siblings run, so a sibling's top-up that only fits without it is refused; a 200 that carries usage and no reply the adapter can read is told to the guard with its counts, for either SDK, and the campaign charges the attempt its bound; a record of a version before the reply-count rule keeps the totals it was written with and replays unchanged, and one of a version under the rule keeps its totals incomplete and round-trips; the report's table counts only budget holds, per condition and model, with a row of zeros for each pair the plan runs; a hold an outage overlapped is counted by its own kind in the export's `budget_holds` and in the report, and an attempt of another kind is not; random runs with retries and replies that leave counts out never cost more than their reservation and top-ups, which some of them do without the top-ups.

**C27. The bound by the reply maximum needs an endpoint shown to hold replies to it.** A run is reserved by the reply maximum R, at most the rounds times R as output and one reply of R past the cap, only for a model whose backend the core's own factory builds (an OpenAI-compatible or Anthropic adapter, which sends R as `max_tokens` in every request) and whose endpoint the admission probe showed applies it: with a budget, at each start, a request with a `max_tokens` of 16 that asks for a long reply must come back cut at that limit, its output count within it. Any other model, one a plugin's factory builds whatever its `backend`, or one the probe did not show, is reserved by its context window and is still admitted, with an event. A reply that later reports more output than R is an event and takes the model off R for the rest of the campaign, a resume included: every run of the model is reserved by the window from then on, those in flight too, and each later request of a run reserved by R goes out only while the campaign is within its ceilings, else it ends as a budget hold. Which bound a run was reserved is the decision of the assessment that admitted it, never the model's status read later, and its attempt's row records it at dispatch: an attempt whose usage is unknown, an interrupted one among them, is charged that bound at every later start, also before admission has shown any model held to R, and never less when a start shows its model held; only a model taken off R charges its attempts by the window whatever their rows say, and a row from before the record follows the gate's view of the model. A resend is cleared again right before it goes out, after the retrier's wait, since the model may have been taken off R during the wait: a top-up reserved by R then reserves the rest of its bound by the window, or the resend is not sent. The reply that broke the rule is outside the guarantee; every request after it is covered. Without a budget no probe of R is sent. *Learned:* the review of the first real study's budget gate (issue #94): R was assumed from the backend's name, so a plugin factory wrapping a core adapter, or an endpoint that ignores `max_tokens`, got the smaller bound; and an interrupted attempt was charged the bound of the model's status at each start, so the start's first gate, which holds no model to R, charged an attempt reserved by R the bound by the window and refused the probes under a ceiling the real total fitted, on every restart. *Test:* the bound by R only when the caller says the model is held to it; only a backend of the core's own factory that sends R is probed, and only with a budget; the probe shows R held only by a reply cut at its small `max_tokens`, not by a longer one, one that stopped by itself or one without its count, for either SDK, and a refused probe is a reason; through the scheduler with the core's factory, a probe that showed it reserves by R, one that did not reserves by the window with an event, a plugin factory's model is reserved by the window and never probed; the store's mark holds across gates and an unreadable mark takes every model off R; a reply past R is an event, the runs in flight are reserved by the window and a later request that no longer fits is a budget hold; a run admitted by R keeps that decision when another attempt takes the model off R before its guard is built, so its first request is checked and held; an attempt reserved by R and killed is charged that bound at the restart, whose probes and runs then fit a ceiling the bound by the window would not, and one reserved by the window stays charged so after a restart that shows its model held; a row recorded by R is charged by the window once its model is taken off R, at any gate, and a row from before the record follows the gate's view; a billed reply past R that the adapter cannot read still takes the model off R; a reply past R of a run reserved by the window takes a model the campaign holds to R off it; a one-token completion answered past its max_tokens of 1 takes the model off R at once, and the probe of R is not sent; a resend whose top-up was reserved by R before the retrier's wait, the model taken off R during it, reserves the rest of its bound by the window, or is a budget hold and is not sent; every resend is cleared again after the wait, whatever failure it follows; random runs against an endpoint that ignores R, or holds to it until one reply goes past it, never cost more than their reservation and top-ups.

**C28. A sharded campaign partitions its plan by pair, the same way on every host.** A campaign run as N shards (`run --shard i/N`) assigns each (task, model, seed) of the whole plan to a shard by ranking each model's pairs by SHA-256 and dealing them round the shards, so every condition of one task, model and seed, and so every pair any contrast forms, lands in one shard, and each shard gets the floor or the ceiling of n/N of each model's pairs; the shards are disjoint and together cover the plan, a sharded plan cannot grow on resume, and the rule is kept with the shard so a store made under another is refused. *Learned:* #154, a main study spread over many hosts. *Test:* the partition over several shard counts, the spec, its labels.

**C29. Shards merge into one batch only when they are one campaign run against one environment.** A merge refuses shards with different fingerprints, rules or counts, overlapping or missing runs, a shard whose own replay check fails, and shards whose recorded environment identities (platform configuration, layout, each space's verified ingest manifest) differ. Before the whole fingerprint is compared, the core is (`merge.check_core`): a shard whose environment records the core at another commit is refused naming both commits, and so is a shard recorded by an earlier core, by how it was installed, beside one recorded by the core's identity (C06). The merge command refuses a shard of the earlier form even among its own kind (`merge.refuse_earlier_form`): for a wheel or directory install that form named no code, so two such shards could have run two cores and compare equal; they merge with the core that ran them. An identity is names mapped to SHA-256 digests only, since packages are public. *Learned:* #154. *Test:* the identity's form; the merge's refusals; a merged batch's scores and analysis are one driver's byte for byte, and its export and report one driver's apart from what is per driver (each run's dispatch position, start and lease wait, and so a condition's median dispatch position) and what only a merge has, since one driver has no shards to break it down by: the shard tags, `shard_retries_by_kind`, each analysed entry's `wall_s` and `turn_latency_s` (clock readings, checked against the shard's store and the record instead), and the report's `## Shards` section; two shards run on two commits of the core at one version are refused, naming both; an earlier-form shard is refused beside a later one and among its own kind, and a batch an earlier core merged from such shards is checked, analysed and reported.

**C30. Shares of a campaign's caps and budget never sum past the totals.** Each shard gets an equal part of every `[scheduler.unit_caps]` total and of `max_usd` and `per_model_usd`, the remainder to the first shards; dollars in whole micro-dollars rounded down. A total below the shard count is refused. *Learned:* #154, a model's concurrent sessions and the budget are limits of the whole campaign, not of one host. *Test:* the shares over several totals and counts.

**C31. An instrument that finds its manipulation unrealised sets the attempt aside.** An instrument may read the state at the agent's first turn (`at_first_turn`, before the loop starts the agent's clock, with a reading of the run's clock that the record logs) and may check, once every snapshot is in (`check`), whether the manipulation held. Either refuses with an `InfraError` of a kind no condition can declare as its treatment, and the attempt is set aside and run again like any infrastructure failure. A refusal at the first turn reaches the executor's caller before any request is sent, and the attempt is charged no tokens, unless its backend counted a request. A refusal after the run is recorded (`instrument_refusal`, an optional field of `expcore-record/3`) and applied in the one place a finished attempt is judged after its run, `AttemptRunner._judge`, after the health check and before the identity check, for an attempt the loop scored and for a scored treatment attempt alike, an outage flag on the attempt included (the treatment rule comes before it, so such an attempt is scored until the refusal rewrites it; it is then discarded as the outage's, not charged, and run again); one already set aside keeps its outcome. A kind a condition may declare, or any other exception, is a defect of the instrument. A study whose instruments have neither method takes no reading and writes no field: its records are unchanged. Instruments nest: they start in list order and finish in the reverse of it, so an instrument listed earlier finishes later, and a probe listed after a fault injector or a progressive ingest reads the state before the restore. The record keeps its instruments in list order, a `finish` that raises does not stop the others, and the first-turn hooks and the checks stay in list order, so the first listed instrument that refuses is the refusal the record keeps. *Learned:* #135, found by the first study's second design, whose manipulation was defined at the agent's first turn, and whose attempts were scored whether or not it held, so a study could only mark their outcomes as not determined. *Test:* the hook's reading, order and refusals; a check's refusal in the record and nothing in a record without one; a malformed or treatable refusal refused at load; the scheduler sets a scored attempt, and a scored treatment attempt, one the health gate reported an outage for too included, aside and runs it again, and leaves a set-aside outcome as it was; the example study's records have neither the reading nor the field; the fourth merge-point fixture replays byte for byte; the finishes are the reverse of the starts, an instrument listed after another starts after it and finishes before it, the hooks and the checks keep list order and the first listed refusal is the one recorded, a finish that raises stops no other, the record's `instruments` are keyed in list order, and a probe listed after the real fault injector reads before the provider is restored while one listed before it reads after. A class that wraps an instrument is told what it drops: `OPTIONAL_HOOKS` is every optional name the discovery looks an instrument up by (a test compares it with the module's own lookups, and the lookup refuses any other name), and `missing_hooks(wrapper, wrapped)` names the hooks a wrapper does not pass on, by the core's own rule of what a hook is.

**C32. A shard never holds pairs it cannot run, and a campaign can shard by model.** A shard that holds pairs of a model whose concurrency share is 0 is refused at start, naming the model, under either rule. `[shards] per_model` in `campaign.toml` (rule `expcore-shard/3`, in the campaign fingerprint) maps shard i to one model in declared order, deals that model's pairs among its own shards as C28 deals them, and gives each shard a cap of one run, so a model's shard count is its concurrent sessions; a model's `per_model_usd` is split over its shards, and `max_usd` over the priced models' shards in proportion to planned runs times one run's reservation, in micro-dollars rounded down, free models' shards getting 0. Refused: counts above a unit's cap, an N that is not their sum, a model of the plan without a count, and a budget that cannot pay one run's reservation for every priced shard. Stores made under `expcore-shard/2` still resume and merge. *Learned:* #154, one federation per concurrent run makes 60 to 140 shards against a model's 20 concurrent sessions, and the balanced deal handed pairs to shards with none of the model's sessions. *Test:* the mapping and the per-model balance, the refusals, the budget split, a campaign split by model and merged equal to one driver as C29 says, also when only some of its models declare accepted fingerprints (B18), so the shards' exports differ in `deployment_shares`. The layout of one priced model beside two that cost nothing (marked free, or priced at 0 with a context window), one shard each, a pilot that prices a single model: the priced shard takes the whole `max_usd` and its model's whole ceiling and the others get 0 and run with no gate (`test_c32_the_one_priced_models_only_shard_takes_the_whole_of_max_usd_and_its_ceiling_and_free_shards_get_none`, `test_c32_in_the_one_priced_layout_the_free_shards_run_with_no_budget_and_the_priced_one_on_the_whole_of_it`); a `max_usd` or a ceiling below one run's reservation of the priced model is refused, the `max_usd` refusal listing each priced shard with its model (with one priced shard, two, and five) (`test_c32_a_max_usd_below_one_runs_reservation_of_the_one_priced_model_is_refused_naming_its_shard_and_model`, `test_c32_a_max_usd_below_the_reservations_of_two_priced_shards_is_refused_naming_both_and_not_the_free_one`, `test_c32_a_ceiling_of_the_one_priced_model_below_one_runs_reservation_is_refused_naming_shard_and_model`); the two free models of one unit capped at 1 are refused at load, and with a cap of 2 or none they load (`test_c32_two_models_of_one_unit_capped_at_one_cannot_have_a_shard_each`, `test_c32_two_models_of_one_unit_capped_at_two_or_not_at_all_load_with_a_shard_each`); the three shards run for real with a fault condition among the conditions and merged equal one driver (`test_c32_with_one_priced_model_its_shard_ran_on_the_whole_budget_and_the_free_shards_on_none`, `test_c32_one_priced_and_two_free_models_one_shard_each_merged_give_the_scores_and_analysis_of_one_driver`); and shards whose fault settings differ are refused at merge by the fingerprint, naming the first that differs (`test_c32_a_shard_by_model_whose_fault_settings_differ_is_refused_at_merge_naming_it`, `test_c32_three_shards_by_model_with_three_fault_settings_are_refused_naming_the_first_that_differs`, with `test_c32_three_shards_by_model_of_federations_with_the_same_fault_settings_pass_the_fingerprint_check` as the control). The rehearsal with a real outage (`tests/study/test_faults_in_model_shards.py`): the example's `relay-down` is a `provider_outage` on `providers[1]` taken by the Onedata layer's `FaultInjector` (`prepared()`, `inject()`, the restore) over the fake Compose services, and a campaign split by model into three shards, each on a federation of its own (root, journal, services, Compose project), injects and verifies a restore in every shard; each journal holds only its own shard's attempts as owners; the shards share one fingerprint with `[faults]` in it; the merged batch's scores and analysis are one driver's byte for byte; and a shard whose federation renders another `[faults]` bound is refused at the merge, naming it.

**C33. A start the platform holds back costs no attempt, and the campaign holds instead of spinning.** A platform registers a hold kind with `register_infra_kind(..., treatable=False, charged=False)`: an attempt that ends in it is set aside uncharged under the same attempt number, as a `budget_hold` is, however often, so no run is lost to `max_attempts` for it; a hold must be untreatable, and a kind cannot be made charged or free by registering it again. A pre-run check that fails with `Health(..., charge=False)`, a failure an earlier attempt left, closes the gate and holds every run, the one that found it included, charging none, and the outage's record and status say so; the flag rides on a deferral too; a post-run check that fails so sets its attempt aside as overlapped (`outage_discarded`), uncharged, whatever its outcome. A deferred pre-run check is asked again no sooner than `recheck_s`, with no attempt begun. An attempt is live from its 'running' row until the store holds its result (`expcore.campaign.liveness`): its executor and its post-run check run inside it, and the pre-run check and the dispatcher's own do not; a token of `liveness.owner()` names the process as the lease pool names a holder and, inside an attempt, its campaign and row, and reads ended once the store has the result, the campaign is closed, or the process is gone; what `liveness.on_end` was given inside an attempt runs as it ends, last first, one that raises not keeping the others. *Learned:* the preallocated ingest's follow-up to #246 (14 Nov), before a study's outage batch: a start refused by a baseline mark raised `ingest_failure`, charged, inside the attempt, so a run in progress or an instrument dropped without its reset used every run's attempts while the gate stayed open. *Test:* a hold kind's registration and its refusals; a start held four times with `max_attempts` 2 ends done at attempt 1, uncharged, and a charged failure still exhausts the run; a failing check that charges no one closes the gate, begins no attempt and every run then starts at attempt 1, the record and the status saying no attempt was charged; a post-run check that charges no one sets a held start and a finished run aside uncharged, and one that charges still charges, but never over an instrument's refusal of a hold kind, which decides, even one checked after another instrument's charged refusal; the reading keeps the flag and refuses one that is not a boolean; deferred checks come at least `recheck_s` apart with no attempt begun; a token is live while its row runs and ended for every other state, no row, a closed campaign and a gone process, another live process reads live, and an unreadable token or store is unknown; the executor and the post-run check see their attempt's token and the pre-run check none; an attempt's endings run as it ends, after a failure too, last first, one that raises logged.

**C34. A fault on one target is declared with its target, and never scored without it.** A kind that is a fault on one target (`provider_outage`, registered with `register_infra_kind(..., requires_target=True)` and listed by `targeted_kinds`) is declared in `infrastructure_is_treatment` as `{ kind, target }`. The plain name is refused when the schema is built, in `study.toml` and in a schema built in code, naming the file, the condition and the kind, so a schema cut to the kind names alone cannot be built; no targetless form exists, and the plain name of a kind that does not require one means what it did. The core holds the name `provider_outage` to a target whoever registers it, a study's own kind of that name included. `Outcome.is_treatment` scores such a kind only when the failure names the declared target and the fault injector's snapshot shows that target held down in this attempt, whatever rule the record's version has, and never when no target is declared. A record that declares it with no target is refused when it is built and when it is read, and an outage run's admission refuses to take the federation without a target. *Learned:* a study's smoke cut it to one condition by rebuilding the schema with the treatment kind names alone, which dropped the outage condition's target; `Outcome.is_treatment` then counted any provider's outage as the treatment and never asked the injector, and the store compared the record to the cut schema, so nothing caught it. The study fixed its side. *Test:* the plain name refused naming the condition and the kind, through `load_study`, `load_factor_schema` and the cut schema; the table loads; an outage of another provider, or of the declared one with no injection of it, is never the treatment; a targetless outage never is, under either rule; the record, the registry's flag and the admission refuse the targetless form.

**C35. A resume says when the platform's settings outside the fingerprint are not the ones the campaign started with.** A platform's settings that change how it works and not what an agent sees (the reset's QoS scan, its waits, its concurrency) are in no fingerprint, so a resume under other ones is not refused and the later resets and placements run differently. The platform gives the core those settings as a flat mapping after its defaults (`StudyPlugin.reset_settings`; the Onedata layer's `Placement.reset_settings()`), the store records it at the first start, and every resume compares: a changed key is named with its old and new value in one warning in the log, in a `reset_settings_changed` event, and in the report's infrastructure notes (the export's `reset_settings`), and the resume goes on. An unchanged resume says nothing and writes nothing; a store with no record records the settings at its resume with one `reset_settings_unknown_before` event and one warning that the earlier settings are unknown; a platform that supplies none leaves the store, the export and the report as they were. A start that is refused records nothing. The record leaves out the data provider's name, since it ships with the export, and has the range map, which decides which bytes a state places, as its SHA-256 alone (`placement.range_map_sha256`, of a canonical form that does not depend on the order of the rows or on unmerged ranges), never its rows. *Learned:* #258 made `qos_scan = "listing"` the default and pinned that `[placement]` is in neither fingerprint, so a campaign resumed after that change, or after a study edited its `[placement]`, ran its later resets by another scan with nothing in the store or the report to say so. *Test:* the first start records and is quiet; an unchanged resume is quiet and rewrites nothing; a changed `qos_scan` warns with both values, is an event, replaces the record, and is quiet at the next resume; a key that appeared or went reads `absent`; `2` and `2.0` are one value; an old store records at its resume, once; settings that are not flat plain data are refused before the store is opened; a refused start records nothing; the export, the join of shards' exports and the report carry the changes and the unknown starts, and without the key are what they were; the Onedata settings hold every field but the holder, with defaults, and the map's digest: the same for rows in another order and for unmerged ranges, another for any change of a range, file, provider or space, and a changed map across a resume warns with the old and new digest and never with a row, while the same map written afresh is quiet.

**C36. An export says which campaign it is of, and a reader can check it.** `export.json` carries `campaign_fingerprint`, `{sha256, fingerprint}`: the fingerprint exactly as the store (or its snapshot) records it, never one computed again, and the SHA-256 of its canonical JSON, which is the hash merge.json names for a merged batch. A merged batch's export holds the fingerprint every shard shares, once, and the join refuses exports whose fingerprints differ. `read_export` refuses an entry that is malformed or whose hash is not the fingerprint's; `verify_export_fingerprint` refuses an export whose hash is not that of the store, snapshot or merge.json it is read with, and says `fingerprint not recorded` of an old export instead of refusing it; the analysis checks the export it builds against every snapshot it read, and `package-check` names an export of another campaign. The key is optional: a store that records none, and an export from before the key, read as they did. *Learned:* the first real study's report lane could not check a campaign-wide setting (each mount level's declared `io_mode`, the bound on a tool server's calls) from `export.json` without reading `state.sqlite`, and nothing in an export said which campaign it came from, so an export left over from another campaign could be read for this one. *Test:* the export carries the store's own text and its hash, in the unsharded, partial and merged forms, and from a snapshot; a store with none exports none; a damaged row is refused; a hash that is not the fingerprint's, and an entry of another form, are refused on reading; a matching hash verifies, a mismatched one is refused naming both, an old export is read and said not to record one; shards of other campaigns, or of which only some carry one, are not joined; an anonymised package's export holds the fingerprint without the environment's sources and the hash of that.

**C37. A study built in code runs through the one public entry, with its own kinds in scope and its config checked as a loaded one is.** `pipeline.run_built_study(study)` is the way into a campaign for a `StudyConfig` a study built by cutting a loaded one (a smoke, a selection), and `run_study` loads a directory and calls it. It checks the config first (`config.check_study`: each declared kind; the factor schema built again from its own parts, `FactorSchema.rebuilt`; the `[scheduler]` kinds; the campaign's models against the model table; each contrast's cluster against the tasks; the shard budget; the expansion of the plan), refusing with a `ConfigError` that names the key before any store, lease or code identity is touched, and holds the study's own kinds in scope (`errors.study_kinds`) for the whole run, so the executor's thread, the threads it starts and the scheduler's see them. The private `_run_campaign` does neither and is documented as internal. *Learned:* a study that cut its plan to a smoke had no public entry and called `_run_campaign` directly, which skipped the kinds scope, so an `InfraError` of a kind the study declares was refused as unknown in the executor's thread; and nothing checked a cut's config the way the loader checks a study. *Test:* an executor that raises the study's own kind from a thread it starts gets it recorded on the attempt, with no harness stop; the same study through `run_study` and through `run_built_study` gives the same summary, fingerprint, scores and analysis bytes, and each resumes the other's store; a cut that names a model the table lacks, drops a task a condition names, leaves a task without its contrast's cluster attribute, names an unknown scheduler kind, declares a kind a study may not, has a plan that does not expand, or edits the schema to an unknown or targetless treatment kind or edits a part of it after it was built (swaps its conditions, deletes a condition from a part) is refused before `out/` exists; a smoke copied from the study directory runs under the real directory's code identity.

**C38. A campaign run in batches by factor levels stays one campaign, and its batches never pass the totals.** `run --select FACTOR=L1,L2 --shard-label L` runs the plan's runs whose declared factors have those levels as a selection shard of the same campaign (rule `expcore-shard/4`, recorded in the store, the snapshot, the export and merge.json), so the study, its factor schema and its fingerprint stay whole; a pair may cross selections, and the merged analysis pairs runs by task, model and seed as one driver's. Refused at start: an unknown factor or level, a selection of no run or of every run, `--select` with `--shard` or `[shards] per_model`, and a resume under another selection, label or mode. The mode is declared, never assumed: concurrent selections take parts of each cap and ceiling proportional to their planned runs (`max_usd` to their reserved bound), rounded down; sequential ones (`--sequential`) take the full caps, the first the whole budget and each later one the totals less what the earlier ones actually spent, read from their stores (`--after`, stopped and of the same campaign). In every mode a part below one run's reservation of a model the selection runs is refused, naming the model. The merge refuses selections that are not disjoint and covering, concurrent with sequential, a sequential chain whose records of the earlier selections their stores contradict or that one started without `--after`, overlapping windows of sequential selections, and a total spend over `max_usd` or a `per_model_usd` ceiling. *Learned:* a study had to run two of its four states before the others; cutting the study changed its fingerprint (and once dropped a treatment's target, C34), and running it whole would have attempted and refused the later rows. A proportional split of its $60 left $30 for a model whose run reserves $32.975, so the sequential mode gives the first batch everything and the next what was left. *Test:* concurrent and sequential batches of the example merged give scores and analysis byte for byte one driver's; the refusals at start, at resume and at merge; the budget shares sum to at most the totals; the $60 and $32.975 case refused when split and run sequentially; overlapping windows refused and sequential ones accepted; a later selection's budget is the total less the first's spend.

## P. Provenance

**P01. Unknown code is dirty code, and dirty code does not start a study run.** *Learned:* ICSOC recorded `unknown` silently (`runner.py:373-384`); ICSA read a failed `git status` as a clean tree (`analysis/run.py:30-39`) and fixed it (`icsa/codestamp.py:1-6, 84-111`; f1c242a8, 3 Nov). *Test:* a tree without git or stamp records `unknown`, dirty, and refuses start. **The core is held to the same rule** (`core_check.require_core`, where `require_known` is called: `run`, `check`, `merge`, `analyze`, with the same `--allow-dirty`): a core whose commit is unknown or whose installed source is stale is refused always, and a dirty core unless dirt is allowed, a pilot's choice that its environment then records; a package build refuses a check recorded under a dirty or unknown core, and a package's own check, on the wheels it pins, asks nothing of the core's identity. *Learned:* the core's identity (C06), 16 Nov 2026. *Test:* a change to what a build reads, edited, renamed or new, is dirt, and one elsewhere or a marker at the top is not; with the core dirty, run, check, analyze and merge are refused, and with `--allow-dirty` the pilot runs to its report recorded dirty, its shards merge only with `--allow-dirty`, and its package is refused.

**P02. Code identity includes the data files the code reads, and is re-verified.** *Learned:* WWW `replay_check.py:52-71, 152-283`, `record.py:209-232` (330464f8, 27d28f58; 20 Oct). *Test:* editing a data file under a code path changes the identity.

**P03. Tree hashes ignore modification times and caches.** *Learned:* ICSA `codestamp.py:18, 27-42`. *Test:* touching a file or adding `__pycache__` leaves the hash unchanged.

**P04. Every output manifest carries the hash of its configuration and preregistration.** Test overrides mark the hash. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/analysis/config.py:37-57`, `analysis/run.py:42-63`. *Test:* an override produces a hash ending in `+override`.

**P05. Scoring and inference code are frozen by digest and a known-answer test.** *Learned:* WWW `harness/score/digest.py:3-21`, `harness/analysis/kat.py` (scorer rule versions swap the digest, db8274c8, 21 Oct). *Test:* changing a frozen module fails the digest check.

**P06. One model table.** *Learned:* ICSA keeps it in three shapes with three naming schemes (`config/models.toml`, `icsa/campaign/config.py:16-39`, `config/harness.toml:55-76`, bridged by `config.py:131-141`); ICSOC kept three (`scored_sweep_driver.py:181-183`). *Test:* every model named anywhere in configuration resolves to exactly one table entry.

**P07. A replication package is not a code source.** *Learned:* this inventory: ICSA's anonymised build changed behaviour (see [`inventory.md`](inventory.md#icsa-replication-package-versus-the-code-that-ran)). *Test:* applying the private study-token map to the source blob named in a module's provenance header reproduces the module's import commit, minus the header; every pushed commit passes the study-token check ([`architecture.md`](architecture.md#provenance-of-extracted-code)).

## S. Statistics and cause coding

**S01. Float noise is snapped before rank tests.** *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/analysis/contrasts.py:49-54, 190-201` (a dry run moved p from 1.12e-5 to 6.09e-6; ruling #14). *Test:* differences equal in arithmetic tie regardless of summation order.

**S02. Holm keeps the declared family.** A missing or barred test counts as p = 1 and stays in the family. *Learned:* ICSA `contrasts.py:228-246`; WWW `harness/analysis/prereg.py:6-7`. *Test:* a family with a NaN keeps its size m.

**S03. Degenerate contrasts are defined.** All-zero differences give p = 1; dropped zero differences and dropped clusters are counted. *Learned:* ICSA `contrasts.py:204-220, 255-258`. *Test:* each degenerate input returns the documented values.

**S04. The cluster bootstrap resamples clusters once for all models.** *Learned:* ICSA `contrasts.py:168-187`. *Test:* the resampled cluster ids are identical across models within a replicate.

**S05. An undefined resample is NaN and never supports the hypothesis.** *Learned:* WWW `harness/analysis/stats.py:7-13, 43`. *Test:* a replicate with an empty arm yields NaN, not 0.

**S06. Multiplicity levels are exact at the boundary.** *Learned:* WWW `harness/analysis/multiplicity.py:86-88`. *Test:* a p-value equal to its level is rejected exactly as declared.

**S07. Kappa reports when it is undefined.** NaN when expected agreement is 1; a bootstrap interval is NaN when fewer than half the replicates are valid; blank, duplicate or missing ids raise. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/causes/kappa.py:13-65`. *Test:* identical single-code ratings give NaN.

**S08. Blind coding sheets cannot leak the condition.** Shuffle rows under opaque ids, keep the key apart, and rename copied files. *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/causes/sampling.py:92-162`. *Test:* no condition or run key appears in the sheet or its file names.

**S09. The power simulator is independent of the pipeline it checks.** *Learned:* ICSA `icsa/analysis/sim.py:3-4, 72-81`. *Test:* an import-graph test, and refusal of parameters that would clip probabilities.

**S10. A treatment attempt never silently leaves its own contrast.** A treatment attempt is scored as its condition's outcome, so a `None` outcome drops its pair from the contrast it was kept for. Count those pairs per contrast; refuse a contrast in the Holm family that has any, unless its preregistration declares `treatment_outcomes = "excluded"`, which leaves every pair with a treatment attempt out whatever its outcome; `"scored"` is a claim that is checked, so such a contrast is refused too, past a preregistered `max_unscored_treatment` (0 unless set), which cannot excuse every treatment attempt of the contrast; a treatment attempt left `None` counts whatever became of its partner; and `"excluded"` may not leave a confirmatory contrast with no pair; report the declaration and the count beside the contrast's result. An exploratory contrast reports and is not refused. *Learned:* the core's own end-to-end test of its example (issue #39): the example's scorer and the guide's both returned `None` for every outcome of a treatment attempt, so the manipulated condition's treatment runs left its confirmatory contrast unseen. *Test:* the drop is counted apart from other `None`s; a confirmatory contrast is refused naming the contrast, the count and the fix; either declaration lets it through; an exploratory contrast reports and goes on; an excluded contrast's row names it with its count.

**S11. A contrast with no usable pair is untestable, never a null result.** A contrast that used no pair, whatever the cause (every partner exhausted or unfinished, an ordinary run scored `None`), concludes `untestable`, never `not_detected` or any word of a tested contrast. It has no estimate, p-value or minimum detectable effect, whatever its estimator returned for no data; in the Holm family it stays a member as p = 1, so the family keeps its size and it never rejects. Its row gives the causes of its missing pairs with their counts, and the word is in the terminal summary and `analysis.json`; a result whose word disagrees with its pairs is refused. *Learned:* the core's adversarial review of #65 (issue #66): a contrast left with no pair concluded `not_detected`, the word of a contrast that was tested and found nothing. *Test:* each cause gives `untestable` with no minimum detectable effect under power settings; Holm adjusts the other member as with p = 1, also against an estimator that returns a number for no data; an exploratory contrast is untestable too; the row gives the causes; a disagreeing result is refused; the example with one arm exhausted shows it through the command line.

**S12. A preregistration makes no confirmatory claim only by saying so.** `exploratory_only = true`, with no `holm_family`, makes every contrast exploratory in `analysis.json`, the report and the terminal summary, and the report's summary says that no confirmatory claim is made. An empty `holm_family` alone is refused, since it is more likely a family never filled in than a choice, and the refusal names `exploratory_only`; a family beside the flag is refused too. Nothing that applies only to a member of the family refuses a contrast, the report shows no empty family table and no Holm wording, and the power study has no row at alpha over the family size. The flag is in the file, so in the preregistration's hash. *Learned:* the first real study on the core (issue #104): a pilot had to name a family of one, which the report then showed as confirmatory. *Test:* the flag loads with no family; an empty family alone, neither, and both are refused, naming the key; every contrast is exploratory and no treatment refusal applies; the report and the summary say no confirmatory claim is made and show no family table; the example runs through `check`, `analyze` and `report` with it.

**S13. A ratio outcome sums every run, a zero sum is declared, never a number, and its cells are never independent units.** A contrast of an outcome per unit of another (`estimator = "ratio"`, a `numerator` and a `denominator`, such as tokens per correct result) compares a ratio of sums, not a mean of per-run ratios: in each cell each arm's numerator and denominator are summed over its used pairs, every task and seed together, and a run whose denominator is 0 still adds its numerator. The cell's value is ln((N_A / D_A) / (N_B / D_B)), computed exactly up to the logarithm, so equal ratios give 0. The cell is declared, `estimation_unit`: the cluster alone (the default) pools the models of each value of the attribute, and `estimation_unit = ["model", attribute]` makes each model's part of a value a cell. The cluster, one task attribute, stays the independent unit either way: a value's log ratio is the mean of its cells', each model weighing the same, and the kernel's signed-rank test, its cluster bootstrap (which draws values with all their cells) and the minimum detectable effect count values, not cells. The cells of one value share its response to the treatment, so declaring them as the cluster (`cluster = ["model", attribute]`) is refused, naming that reason and the declaration that estimates per cell and tests over values. A cell with a zero sum in either arm has no log ratio: the preregistration must declare `zero_denominator`, `"exclude"` (the cell leaves the contrast, counted with its pairs under `zero_denominator` or `zero_numerator`; a value drops only when every cell of it is lost) or `"continuity"` with a positive `continuity_correction` added to all four sums of every cell; nothing is set to 0 or an infinity. A pair lost because a run's numerator is None while its denominator is 0 is counted under `numerator_none_at_zero`, and a confirmatory contrast with any is refused. Each contrast names its own numerator column, and may give each arm its own (`numerator_a`, `numerator_b`, `denominator_a`, `denominator_b`, each defaulting to the shared key), so an adverse sensitivity estimate with arm A at an upper bound and arm B at a lower one is a declared contrast of its own; each run is read in its own arm's columns only, found from the run itself, a failed run's None numerator refuses a confirmatory contrast only in the column its arm reads, and a shared key no arm would read, or an arm with no column, is refused. `analysis.json` and the report name both units and count the cells used and lost by cause; the report shows the outcome as `ln(numerator/denominator)`, a "Ratio contrasts" section with the units, the cells, the rule and exp of the estimate and interval, each model's estimate over its own cells as an exploratory breakdown, and the three causes in the pairs table; a report without a ratio contrast is unchanged. A contrast of one outcome already estimates per (model, value) cell and tests over values, and its record names the same two units. *Learned:* the first study's main-study preregistration (issue #138): its confirmatory cost hypothesis, tokens per correct result, could not be expressed, and a scorer that set tokens to None on failed runs made the comparison silently conditional on success in both arms; its power analysis estimated per (model, instance) cell and inferred over instances, which the pooled ratio does not do (PR #233's review); and a first answer to that review made each (model, instance) cell an independent unit of the test and the bootstrap, which claims an n the data do not have (the coordinator's review of PR #233). *Test:* hand-computed log ratios, the failed run's numerator counted, each zero case excluded with its cause or corrected, exact zero for equal ratios, the estimator against scipy's signed-rank test and the kernel's bootstrap, a confirmatory contrast refused for a failed run's None numerator and an exploratory one counting it, every key of the form refused when missing or misplaced, the report's columns only when a ratio contrast is there; a hand-computed nested example (two groups of two models each, each group the equal-weight mean of its cells), a group keeping its other cell when one is lost and a group with every cell lost dropped and counted, perfectly correlated cells of a group giving the inference of one cell per group (estimate, p-value, intervals, minimum detectable effect, clusters), the cells declared as the cluster refused on a confirmatory and an exploratory contrast, the default unit declared giving the default's record exactly, both units in the record, a difference contrast's group as its unit however many models run it, and two contrasts on two numerator columns each with its own result and refusal; a hand-computed per-arm example (two models, three groups: arm A on its upper-bound column and arm B on its lower-bound one gives cells ln 6r, groups the equal-weight means of their models' cells and the estimate ln 12, against ln 4 with the lower-bound column for both arms), each arm's sums read from its own column with the other column poisoned, a run of arm B without arm A's column paired and a run of arm A without its own refused, a failed run's None in its own arm's column refusing a confirmatory contrast in either arm and a None in the other arm's column leaving it its known answer, every inconsistent declaration refused naming the key, a per-arm key that restates the shared column giving the one-column record byte for byte, the per-arm columns named in `analysis.json` and the report and absent from both for a one-column contrast.

**S14. A run lost to None is counted in the arm that lost it, and a one-arm rate is descriptive.** Each contrast's result gives, for each arm, its planned runs of the contrast's planned pairs (keys both arms plan, analysed or absent), the runs finished and measured, and the runs lost by cause (`treatment_outcome_none`, `outcome_none`, `exhausted`, `unfinished`, `excluded`), each run in exactly one place; the report shows each arm's finish rate beside the contrasts, `n/a` (never 0%) for an arm with none planned. A preregistration may declare one-arm estimates in `[descriptives.<name>]` over one condition or a set: a rate (`outcome`), the mean over the arm's runs that have it, aggregated per task and model, cluster and model, then over models and clusters with equal weight, its interval the kernel's cluster bootstrap at its own seed; or a ratio of totals (`numerator`, `denominator`), every run's sums together, a failed run's numerator counted, each replicate the drawn clusters' total ratio and NaN, counted, where the drawn denominators sum to 0. It has no test, no conclusion and no place in a Holm family, and the report and the terminal say it is descriptive. *Learned:* the first study's main-study preregistration (issue #139): descriptive hypotheses (how often agents query, how rarely they wait) had no one-arm estimate, and a None outcome was counted per pair, never per arm that lost the run. *Test:* hand-computed rates and ratios, the shared draws, undefined replicates counted, every denominator 0 and no run left, the arm counts by cause for both contrast forms and with a run outside every pair, the loader's checks, the report's sections, and the example's report regenerated with each arm's runs.

**S15. Equivalence claims made together are decided together, and only when reachable.** A preregistration may declare `equivalence_family`: members of the Holm family, each with an `equivalence_margin` and an estimator that registers a TOST p-value; it is refused beside `exploratory_only`, and a member outside the Holm family, without a margin or without that hook is refused naming it. A member's TOST p is its estimator's two one-sided tests' p-value; for the Wilcoxon cluster bootstrap, Schuirmann's two one-sided t-tests of the mean cluster difference on n - 1 degrees of freedom, NaN with fewer than two clusters or no spread, never certainty. Holm adjusts the family, a NaN or untestable member counting as p = 1, and a member is `equivalent` only when Holm rejects, with the test's dual interval at 1 - 2 times its Holm level reported beside the TOST and Holm TOST p. A margin outside the family keeps the 1 - 2 alpha interval rule. Before the campaign, a simulation gives, for each member, the chance of concluding equivalent at a true 0 and at half the margin, from the declared SDs or a pilot's, at the target clusters and at alpha over the family size, judging each study by the member's estimator's own test, and says whether the claim is reachable at the target power; the power module imports nothing of the pipeline (S09). A sensitivity analysis the report shows shows the flat equivalence family's table too. *Learned:* the first study's main-study preregistration (issue #140): its "no difference" hypotheses, one per primary outcome and state, would each have read its own 1 - 2 alpha interval, so the chance of a false equivalence grew with their number, and nothing said whether the clusters planned could ever show one; and the core's own review: a percentile-bootstrap TOST called one cluster inside the margin equivalent, and erred at the margin four times in ten with one cluster and one in six with two. *Test:* hand-computed and scipy-checked t-tests, one cluster and no spread giving no p-value, the exact duality with the interval, the Wilcoxon estimator's hook, two members each below alpha and neither equivalent under Holm, the step-down levels and adjusted intervals, one cluster never equivalent, an untestable member, the loader's refusals, the simulation's error rate at the margin for 2, 3 and 30 clusters, its power against the closed form, its agreement with the kernel's test, a member judged by its estimator's own test, and the report's table, and the egress rerun's table of the flat equivalence family.

**S16. A smallest effect of interest is judged on the scale of the estimate, after Holm, and only an estimate of the claimed sign meets it.** A contrast may preregister its smallest effect of interest in the form its estimator reads: `smallest_effect = d`, a positive difference on the outcome's scale, for a contrast of one outcome; `smallest_ratio = r`, a positive ratio other than 1 (2 for a factor of two), for a ratio contrast, whose estimate, interval and minimum detectable effect are natural-log ratios, so r is compared as |ln r| and r and 1 / r are one size; and an optional `direction`, `"higher"` or `"lower"`, the claimed sign of A - B. The key of the other form, a non-positive or non-finite value, a ratio of 1, a direction alone or unknown, and a ratio below 1 beside `"higher"` are refused naming the key, by the loader and by a spec built past it. The decision follows the study's rule, significant and the point estimate at or beyond the threshold: `meets` needs the claimed sign, so a significant effect in the opposite direction is `opposite_direction` whatever its size; without a direction it is `meets_either_direction`, never a bare `meets`, and the report and the terminal write the estimate's sign beside it; else `falls_short`, `not_significant` or `untestable`. A member of the Holm family is judged by Holm's decision, never its raw p-value. Beside it, the reported interval includes the smallest effect or excludes it, beyond or short of it, and the design detects it when the contrast's minimum detectable effect, on the same scale, is at most it (unknown, never no, without power settings or where it cannot be found; a ratio contrast's also as a ratio, exp of the log-scale figure). Comparisons are on the snapping grid. An equivalence margin is another declaration and neither is derived from the other. `analysis.json` gives a declaring contrast `smallest_effect`, and a study that declares none keeps the bytes of its `analysis.json` and its report. *Learned:* story P1 (issue #81): a past study's rule, Holm-significant with a point estimate of at least 10 points on proportions or a factor of 2 on cost, could not be preregistered, and the equivalence margin as a stand-in changed the decision rule; and its first draft's review: "meets" as |estimate| at least the threshold let a significant effect of the wrong sign confirm a directional hypothesis. *Test:* hand-derived minimum detectable effects of six clusters (the exact signed-rank test rejects only when all six share a sign, so power 0.8 needs Phi(delta / sd) = 0.8^(1/6)) above and below a declared difference, and a ratio contrast with `smallest_ratio = 2` whose log-scale figure and estimate are judged against ln 2 where the wrong scale would flip each verdict; an opposite-sign effect with and without a direction; Holm's decision over the raw p-value; every refusal; the interval and decision tables; the hash covering the declaration; the report's section; and byte fixtures of an undeclared analysis's `analysis.json` and report.

**S17. Each preregistered Holm family is adjusted on its own, never merged with another.** A preregistration may declare several named families in `[holm_families]` in place of one flat `holm_family`; Holm runs over each family apart at alpha, so a member's level, adjusted p-value and decision are its own family's and no family's size or p-values move another's. A member's minimum detectable effect is judged at alpha over its own family's size, and an equivalence claim (S15) is adjusted with the other equivalence claims of its own Holm family only. A contrast is in one family at most, and a family of one is a stand-alone confirmatory test; both forms at once, the table beside `exploratory_only`, an empty table or family, a blank name, an unknown or repeated contrast and a contrast in two families are refused, naming the file and the family. `analysis.json` gives each named family its members and its steps (raw and adjusted p-value, level and verdict) and the report a table and the steps per family, refusing a result whose families and decisions disagree; a study with one flat family keeps its `analysis.json` and report byte for byte. The families are in the preregistration file, so in the hash every output carries. A sensitivity analysis the report shows (the rerun without the runs an egress or mount guard leaves out) is shown the same way: a table and the steps for each named family, never one merged family, the same refusal of families that disagree with the decisions, and each family's members and steps beside the rerun's decisions in `analysis.json`. *Learned:* re-expressing an already-run study on the core (issue #76, from story P1): it registered ten families of three contrasts, and the core took one flat family only, so merging them would have made Holm's first step alpha / 30 where the study registered alpha / 3, a test more conservative than the one preregistered, while keeping one family and leaving the others out would have labelled confirmatory claims exploratory in the report. *Test:* two hand-worked families of three and two (alpha/3, alpha/2, alpha/1 and alpha/2, alpha/1) against one flat family of five that rejects other contrasts, a family that stops at its second step, a family of one, an untestable member in its own family, equivalence claims adjusted within their own families, the feasibility and power levels, each refusal, the hash, the report's tables and its refusal of families that disagree with the decisions, a run through the command line, and a flat family's analysis.json pinned byte for byte, and for the egress, mount-guard and both-guards reruns a table and the steps per family, the terminal lines naming each member's family, a refused disagreement, the families in `analysis.json`, and a flat family's rerun pinned in its text, its terminal lines and its keys.

**S18. A declared equivalence family is adjusted over its own claims, wherever their significance tests are.** A preregistration may declare named families of equivalence claims in `[equivalence_families]` in place of the flat `equivalence_family`; Holm runs over each family's TOST p-values apart at alpha, whatever Holm families the claims' significance tests are in, so twelve claims registered as one family face alpha / 12 first, alpha / 11 next, and so on, and no Holm family adjusts or holds an equivalence decision. Each member must be in a Holm family (its significance test is confirmatory) with an `equivalence_margin` and an estimator that registers a TOST p-value, and a claim is in one equivalence family at most; the table beside `equivalence_family` (naming both) or `exploratory_only`, an empty table or family, a blank name, an unknown or repeated contrast, a member in two families or outside every Holm family are refused, naming the file and the family. The feasibility simulation judges each claim at alpha over its own equivalence family's size. `analysis.json` adds `equivalence_families` (each family's name, members and steps: raw and adjusted TOST p, level and verdict) and the report a table and the TOST steps per family, refusing a result whose families and equivalence decisions disagree; without the table, the flat `equivalence_family` is adjusted within each Holm family as before, and its `analysis.json` and report keep their bytes. The table is in the preregistration file, so in the hash every output carries. A sensitivity analysis shows and writes its declared equivalence families the same way. *Learned:* a main study's preregistration, before its analysis freeze: it registered one equivalence hypothesis as a single Holm-adjusted family of twelve claims (alpha / 12 at the first step, the figure its feasibility rests on) whose contrasts sit in four Holm families of three significance tests, and the core split the flat equivalence family by Holm family (S17), adjusting four groups of three at alpha / 3: a more liberal test than the one preregistered. *Test:* hand-worked steps of twelve claims across four Holm families (alpha / 12 to alpha / 1, Holm stopping at the fifth step) against the same claims without the table (alpha / 3 within each Holm family, nine equivalent where the declared family has four), two equivalence families under one flat Holm family against one flat equivalence family of the five, an untestable claim keeping its family's size, the feasibility levels, each refusal, a preregistration built in code, the hash, the report's table, steps and refusals, the terminal line, and analysis.json and the report of both existing layouts pinned byte for byte from the code before the table existed, and the egress rerun's claims, TOST steps and `analysis.json` entries.

**S19. A preregistration declares the sensitivity reruns it expects, and an analysis runs exactly those.** Which sensitivity reruns `analyze` runs (the egress rerun, the mount guard's, the process-visibility check's, and the one without every run any guard leaves out) depends on which guard instruments the records carry, so a guard listed on the wrong runs or on none gave a different set than the one a study meant to preregister, and the preregistration had no key to say. `[sensitivity] reruns = [...]` names the reruns the study expects (`egress`, `mount_guard`, `process_visibility`, `any_guard`, each at most once, in any order) and is read by the loader as the other tables are (`expcore.stats.reruns`): refused when it is not a table, holds a key but `reruns`, lacks it, is not an array of the four names or names one twice, with the file, `[sensitivity]` and the key, and the closest name for a misspelt one. `analyse_study` then refuses unless the reruns it would run are exactly the declared ones, in either direction, naming each declared rerun it cannot run with why (no analysed record carries the guard's instrument; for `any_guard`, fewer than two guards checked the batch, and which did) and each rerun it would run that is not declared; a rerun counts as run when its guard checked the batch, whether or not it left a run out, so an all-clean batch is the rerun that was declared. **A preregistration with no table reads as `reruns = []`:** a batch no guard checked runs no rerun and passes with no table, its `analysis.json` as it was, and a batch a guard checked is refused, naming the reruns it would run and the table that is missing, so no rerun goes unpreregistered; `reruns = []` declares that none is expected. The declaration is in the file, so in its hash, and `analysis.json` carries `sensitivity_reruns` only when the table is there. *Learned:* the first study's question whether the form had keys for these reruns: it had none, and an unknown top-level key was refused at load; and the review of the first version of this change, which asked that an absent table read as `reruns = []`. *Test:* the table loads in the order given, the field is None for an absent table and empty for `reruns = []`, each malformed declaration is refused with the file, table and key, an override is checked and shares nothing with the original, the hash changes with the declaration; matching reruns are accepted whatever their order, a declared rerun nothing supports is named with why, an undeclared rerun is named, both directions are named with the two lists, `any_guard` says which guards checked the batch, a declaration of nothing refuses a batch that would run one, and a guard's rerun counts as run when it left no run out; through the pipeline an analysis that runs exactly the declared reruns goes through and `analysis.json` names them, a declared rerun no record supports and an undeclared one are each refused naming it, a guarded batch with no table is refused naming the reruns to declare and the missing table, and a batch no guard checked with no table passes and writes no `sensitivity_reruns`.

## O. Platform (Onedata)

**O01. Transfer status is not evidence of placement.** Only the block map (`GET /data/{id}/distribution`) is; Onedata 25.0 reports `completed` for a replication that placed zero bytes and an eviction that removed none. An eviction that skipped files without a failure shows only `filesProcessed` above `filesEvicted` with `filesFailed` 0, and op-worker skips for four reasons: the provider holds nothing of the file, the file is open, the provider knows of no other copy yet (the eviction that removes none, right after a placement), and the file is cancelled per file because a QoS requirement still covers it there (a requirement whose deletion has not reached that provider through dbsync; op-worker f118ed9, `replica_deletion_master.erl:364-371`, `replica_eviction_worker.erl:51-59, 88-96`). The counters therefore cannot decide, and the reset's eviction stage does not fail on a gap alone: it keeps the block maps as the judge and the scheduling again, records the last attempt's `files_processed`, `files_evicted` and `files_failed` per provider (unknown is None, never zero), and when files still hold bytes after the last attempt, names the provider, the counts and the likely cause. A replication that completed short is scheduled again: the target runs it from its own dbsync copy of the other providers' file locations, which lags a write while the block map read through any provider already shows it, so one scheduled right after a write can copy only part of the file and report `completed`. While the target's own block map is short after a job ended, the replication is scheduled again after a pause of 5, 10, then 15 s, at most five times, within `settle_timeout_s` from the first job's end, and every try's id is recorded (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-14-federation-facts-replication-lag.md`). *Learned:* ICSOC onedata-issues 0003 and 0006; ICSA `icsa/scripted/placement.py:1-5`, `provision.py:4-5, 219-220`, `mplus.py:9, 180-184` (34b0ec84, 1 Nov); the op-worker source read for the QoS requirement deletion study (the cancelled eviction, 13 Nov). *Test:* `replicate()` returns success only when the block map covers the range, against a fake that reports `completed` with zero blocks. A replication whose target's copy of the source's map shows the prefix alone copies it, reports completed and is scheduled again after a pause, the second placing the rest, with both tries and their ids recorded; one whose copy never catches up is refused as `NotPlaced` after its five tries and the pauses between them, within the settle bound; one that places the file at once is scheduled once.
