a deadline holds, the retry cut to what remains and none made when a pause and a second do not fit; against a real socket a connection that carried an answer and then hangs is closed and left out of the pool, the retry opens another and the line says `reused` with the requests it had served and then `new`, also when the pool holds a second idle connection, which is not given to the retry; a table of every call of the layer that reaches a GET names its choice, found by a scanner that takes any get with a keyword or a path, the client's gated and space reads on any receiver, a wait imported by name or bound by partial, a method got by getattr under a constant name, the client's own calls, the provisioner's helpers and the layer's functions that retry by default (`place_ranges`, `evict_ids`, `evict_tree`), fails on a call it cannot classify, and fails a measuring module that names a policy or calls such a helper (a probe's resolve with no wait=False); the health gate's reads and the probes' listing and block-map reads are one attempt while a listing, a size read, the visibility wait and a reset's reads are retried and counted, the reset's record, a failed reset's included, and the ingest's snapshot holding the count.

**O30. The data gate starts on the deployment it is given, and reaches the federation by the names its certificates hold.** The gate runs as root with every capability dropped but `NET_BIND_SERVICE`, so every file it reads must be root's or readable by anyone: each TLS key it serves a name with is made owner-only and given to root before it starts, as its token file is, and a key that cannot be given to root fails the start before any container exists; its certificates, their directory and its configuration hold no secret and are readable by anyone, whatever the driver's umask. A federation the host reaches at addresses no container can use, such as a Compose project's services published on the host's loopback, is reached on its own Docker network, declared in `[sandbox.federation]` with the zone's and the local provider's names there: the gate starts on that network, is then connected to the sandbox's internal one, and serves and dials those names on port 443, checking each certificate as `[tls]` says; the agent resolves the names to the gate and never joins that network. Without the table the gate is on Docker's default bridge and serves and dials the hosts and ports of the platform file's URLs, as before. *Learned:* #203, from the first federation study's day-0: every start of the gate container on the Compose federation exited before "gate ready", because `make_ca` writes each key owner-only for the driver and the gate, without `CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE`, cannot read it (a `PermissionError` from `load_cert_chain`, reproduced byte for byte from the core's own gate); and the platform file's URLs, the loopback ports the host reaches, would have had the gate dial `127.0.0.1` inside its own container, keyed by the one host both URLs share. No live test had started the gate container: the offline tests run the gate in-process as the test user, and the only live sandbox runs were on the mount path. *Test:* each key the gate serves is 0400 and given to root through the privilege command, the certificates, their directory and `gate.json` are readable by anyone, and a key that cannot be given to root refuses the start with nothing left; with the table the gate's container starts on the federation's network and joins the sandbox's, its hosts are the zone's and the local provider's names there on port 443 and its certificates are issued for them, the agent resolves those names to the gate, its zone host is the zone's name there, and it is on the sandbox's network alone; a rest level needs the table's `zone`, and a table no rest level reads refuses it; without the table the gate's network, hosts and the agent's zone host are unchanged; the names the live test's derived platform gives the gate are names the certificates the Compose federation issues hold; a gated live test (`tests/live/test_rest_path.py`) starts a REST sandbox on the Compose federation with the federation's own platform file and a `[sandbox]` table, reads a file through the gate and gets it by hash.

**O31. One campaign interleaves conditions of different tool-server rights, each run on a slot whose user holds its condition's.** A slot's tool-server user holds exactly one condition's rights, since its token is minted only for a user whose rights equal the condition's; so each slot declares those rights (`[slots.<name>.toolserver] rights`, required, by a condition's rules), each set of declared rights is a lease group of the slots that declare it, conditions with the same rights share a group, and a run with a tool server leases only from its condition's group. Runs of different groups hold a slot each at once, and a run waits only for a slot of its own group. A run with a data path and no tool server leases from every slot. A condition in the plan whose rights no slot declares is refused when the campaign is built, naming the condition by its position and the rights, never at a run's mint; a slot of other rights handed to a run is refused before anything is journalled or minted. A condition's tool allow-list and its write rights must agree, or the platform file is refused at load, naming the condition by its position and the tool or the right: a write tool whose right the condition lacks (replication, or adding or removing a QoS requirement: the agent would see it fail on every call), a file-writing tool (a tool-server user is never granted `space_write_data`), and a write right no tool of the allow-list needs (a token wider than its tools). Which privilege each tool needs is one table, `toolrights.TOOL_NEEDS`, read at the onedata-mcp commit `docs/inventory.md` pins, with the server's own mutating tools as its write set; only the rows the repository records are enforced, the view rows being documented until a live run records them, and a tool it does not know is not judged. Splitting the campaign by condition is not the fix: it confounds the condition with time (endpoint drift, the platform's state) and breaks the randomised, interleaved order a paired comparison relies on. *Learned:* #205, from the first federation study's live run path: the one lease group of all slots let a run of a read-only condition (two rights) draw the slot of a writing one (six rights), and its tool-server token's mint failed on the rights mismatch. #182, asked for by the first federation study: a condition whose allow-list held a write tool and whose rights granted no write right got the read-only token, and every write failed as though it were the agent's mistake. *Test:* a campaign whose plan interleaves a read-only and a writing condition leases every run a slot whose tool-server user declares and holds exactly its condition's rights, and every mint succeeds; a read-only and a writing run are in flight at once, each on its group's slot; with the read-only group's slot held another read-only run gets none while the writing group's is free, and a writing run takes that one; a run without a tool server takes from every slot; a plan condition no slot serves refuses the campaign when it is built, naming `conditions[i]` and the rights and no slot, and its group cannot be asked for; a lease of the writing group handed to a read-only run mints and journals nothing; a campaign of one rights set leases every slot, as the one group did; a slot without rights, without the baseline, with an unknown right or a right twice is refused at load by its position; a condition whose allow-list holds a replication or QoS write tool without its right, or a file-writing tool, or whose rights hold a write right no tool of its allow-list needs, is refused at load naming `conditions[i]` and the tool or the right and never the condition's name, while an agreeing read-only condition and an agreeing writing one load, a view tool without its view right loads, and the first federation study's two conditions (a basic reader and a QoS writer) load exactly as declared; the table holds every tool of the pinned server, its write rows are the server's own list of mutating tools, and its pin is the inventory's (`tests/onedata/test_toolrights.py`).

**O32. A reader of a run's transfers reads the run's window of the ended listing, newest first, never the space's whole history, and fails closed when the order it rests on does not hold.** Ended transfers are never removed and never expire, so every run and reset adds to a space's ended listing for the life of the space. The transfer log (the probes' `space_transfers` and `on_demand`) and the reset's history stage read the waiting and ongoing listings to their end, and the ended one newest first, each job's record in its turn, down to the first job whose record says it ended before the window's start less the clock-skew allowance and a margin (`transferorder.STOP_MARGIN_S`, 10 s); a job before that whose record has not ended is kept, as it may have run into the window. Their bounds (`MAX_LISTED`, `LISTED_MAX`) count the jobs read down to there, so a long history never makes a run's reading unknown or a reset fail, and a window that truly holds more is unknown, never cut. A job's times are stamped on the platform's global clock, to which the providers synchronise theirs, and neither a provider's Date header nor Onezone's gives it (each is a node's system clock, and a multi-node Onezone's answering node can differ from the global clock by a bias), so both place the window on the global clock itself, read from Onezone's public current-time endpoint (`GET /provider/public/get_current_time`, no credential, answered `timeMillis`): its offset is the global time less the midpoint of this host's clock around the read, and half the round trip widens the band. The margin decides where reading stops, never what counts. A job in the band at the window's start, from the least offset the readings allow less the allowance to the greatest plus it (the allowance covering a provider's synchronisation error to the global clock), may lie on either side: the probes flag it (`transfer_near_window_edge`, its target's bound unknown) and the reset counts it in `history.uncertain`, never in `ended`, which is exact only when `uncertain` is 0. Every finish time read must be no later than the one before it, through the end of the page that holds the stop and across every record of the next page, which the walk reads for that check alone; one that is later makes the reading unknown, naming the assumption, rather than ending the walk early and missing a job, and so does the global clock more than the margin behind this host's, or, for the reset, a reading of it that fails or cannot be read (for the probes it leaves every bound unknown, `clock_offset_unknown`). The residual: a job of the window two or more pages past the stop is not seen, since catching it would take reading the whole history; there the walk rests on the order holding by construction of the listing's keys, and the page past the stop guards that assumption where its failure would cost a job. A job cancelled while still scheduled, before it was enqueued, gets no finish time, so it is listed after every job that has one and the walk stops at it (when its record says it ended): neither it nor what follows is read as part of the window or counted against a bound, however many such cancellations the history holds, so the reset's `history.ended` counts the jobs that ended with a finish time in the window and never holds it, the probes' `records` and `listed` never hold it, and it moved no bytes; the reset's own cancels are counted in `transfers.cancelled`. A job with a finish time listed after one with none breaks the order and makes the reading unknown, as any order violation does; a record whose `finishTime` is 0 but that does not say it ended may lag its listing entry and is kept. No page token is built by hand to jump to a time. A record says it ended by its state read per kind (`transferrecord.state_of`): a replication's is its replication half's, an eviction's its eviction half's, and a migration's that of the half it is in, final only once both are; the half a job does not do reads `skipped` on 25.0 and contradicts nothing, and status fields that contradict each other read `conflicting`, never final. The walk, the window, the probes' records and the waits for a transfer's end (`consistency.transfer_state`) all read it so. *Learned:* #207, from the first federation study re-wiring its reset: with about 1,500 files read and reset per run, the probes' listing passed `MAX_LISTED` within two runs, leaving `on_demand_lower_bound` unknown for most runs of a campaign, and the reset's history would fail past `LISTED_MAX`. The order, the paging and the absence of expiry and of a time filter were read from op-worker `f118ed9` (`transfer_links.erl:56-95,160-164`; `transfer.erl:325-345`; the space-transfers handler's parameters), and Oneprovider's REST description states none of them. The Codex delta review of #207 found the check past the stop reading only the next page's first record (cutoff 985, pages `[100]` and `[90, 1010]` returned an empty window) and the reset's history counting from the least offset alone (offsets 0 and -8 counted a job one provider stamped at 995, before a window at 1000). Who stamps a finish time, and on which clock, was read from op-worker `f118ed9` (`transfer_changes.erl:21-28,75-89,121-135`; `replication_status.erl:222-231`; `replica_eviction_status.erl:223-229`), ctool `8ae7baf` (`global_clock.erl:8-46`; op-worker's `gs_channel_service.erl:41-46`) and cowboy 2.9.0 (`cowboy_clock.erl:61,74,97`). The second delta review found the band built from the providers' Date offsets, which do not bound the clock that stamps: with both providers 8 s ahead and Onezone agreeing with this host, a job at 1001 was placed before a window at 1000, neither ended nor uncertain, so `uncertain` 0 claimed an exact `ended` that missed it, and the probes left a known bound without its bytes; it also found `ended` omitting, without a word, a job cancelled while scheduled in the window. Onezone's clock was read from ctool `8ae7baf` (`global_clock.erl:9-10,28-32,86-87,259-262`) and oz-worker `14b5bda` (`provider_routes.erl:192-198`, `provider_logic_plugin.erl:304-305`, `provider_logic.erl:548-554`: the providers synchronise to Onezone's `get_current_time`, its global clock). The third delta review found Onezone's Date header, a node's system clock, standing for the global clock: on a multi-node Onezone a node 8 s ahead with a global-clock bias of -8 s dates its answers +8 while the providers stamp on a clock that agrees with this host, so a job at 1001 was again placed earlier with no uncertainty. It also found a job given no finish time kept by the walk and counted against the bound: a window with one recent finish after a long tail of such cancellations raised (`listed_max` 2: one finish and two cancels). The global clock is now read itself from Onezone's public `get_current_time` (oz-worker `14b5bda`: `provider_routes.erl:192-198`, `provider_logic_plugin.erl:304-305,491-492`, `provider_rest_translator.erl:128-129`, `provider_logic.erl:548-554`), which no live run has made yet (`client.UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS`, with its live contract test), and a job given no finish time ends the walk. The first federation study's run at core `39bba713` found every ended replication record, `transferStatus` and `replicationStatus` `completed` beside `evictionStatus` `skipped` (all 1,080 answers of its 20 replications), read `conflicting`, since the state was the set of the status fields and any disagreement conflicting: the probes listed 20 jobs ended and conflicting and 23 carried over, so the placement and transfer bounds were unknown for every run whose agent replicated, though the bytes reconciled exactly, and the study's scripted persona, polling `job_of(...).state` for a final state, never saw an end and ran 54 rounds more, to its token cap. The core's fakes answered one half alone, so no test met the real shape. The rule was read from op-worker `f118ed9` (`transfer.erl:157-166,437-455,474-495`, `replication_status.erl:198-282`, `replica_eviction_status.erl:202-269`, `transfer_rest_translator.erl:78-88,119-124`). The Codex review found a record of `type` replication that named an evicting provider beside `replicationStatus` and `transferStatus` `completed` and `evictionStatus` `skipped` read completed, though op-worker derives the type from the provider ids (`transfer.erl:397-403`) and would call it a migration, final only once its eviction is: provider ids that name another kind than the `type` now read conflicting. *Test:* the fake lists newest first by finish or schedule time and resumes after a full page's token; 3000 ended jobs of earlier runs and the run's one leave the probes' reading known, holding the run's job alone, with at most the page that holds the stop and the next page's records read past the window; with a bound of two, two jobs in the window are read past fifty older ones and three are unknown; an older job listed ahead of the run's, or the run's two jobs out of order, fail closed naming the order, and so does an older job alone on the page that holds the stop with a newer one first on the next page, in the walk and in the probes with one job a page, or second on the next page behind an older one (cutoff 985, pages `[100]` and `[90, 1010]`); five old waiting and ongoing jobs are all read and carried over; a job at the cutoff is read and one a second before it is where the walk stops; a provider clock behind by more than the margin fails closed; the reset's history passes a bound its whole history exceeds, cancels the old running jobs, counts its window from its own start or the run's, says older jobs exist, and fails at its history stage on an order violation; it counts a job read in the margin below its window as an older one, not the window's; with both providers' Date headers 8 s ahead or behind, or Onezone's own, and the global clock agreeing with this host, the band is 995 to 1005 and a job at 1001 is uncertain on the reset and flagged near the edge on the probes, never earlier, and one at 1006 is ended, the Date offsets only recorded; the band is uncertain at both ends and a second past either is older or ended; a global clock 3 s behind this host's widens the band so that a job at 996 is uncertain, and half the round trip of the read widens it further (a read of 2 s: 993 to 1005); the reset fails at its history stage, and the probes' reading is unknown, on a global clock 30 s (12 s) behind, and a reading of the global clock that fails, or holds no `timeMillis` number, fails the reset and leaves every probe bound unknown, while an unreadable Date of Onezone's, a provider's clock that far behind, or one without a Date, places and fails nothing; the public read is sent with no credential, and no other path is; a job the reset cancels while still scheduled is in `transfers.cancelled`, keeps finish time 0, and is in none of `history.ended`, `uncertain` and `earlier`; with a bound of two, one finish followed by two jobs given no finish time is read on the reset (`ended` 1) and on the probes (the finish alone), a long tail of them on a walk costs the stop's page and the page past it, a job with a finish time listed after one with none fails closed on the stop's page and the page past it, and a record with `finishTime` 0 that does not say it ended is kept; and a job listed ended before the window whose record still says active is still carried over; the live replication answers, ended and enqueued, and an eviction's and a migration's derived from op-worker read completed, enqueued, completed and active in every reader of a transfer's state, every phase of each kind reads its half's state and is final exactly when op-worker's summary is, contradictory fields and provider ids that name another kind read conflicting, a missing half never reads final, a poll of `job_of` and `wait_transfers` stop at the first final answer, an earlier job in the live shape is never carried over, a replication-only and an eviction-only run each leave a known bound with the bytes reconciling, and the fakes' records carry the real answers' status fields, the unused half `skipped`.

**O33. The reset removes the run's own QoS requirements, found without a read per file where it can be, and shows their files settled and quiet after the eviction.** The 25.0 build has no listing of a space's requirements, and a QoS replication creates no transfer document, so neither the transfer listing nor a cancel reaches one. The reset finds requirements by `[placement] qos_scan`: one recursive listing of the space root on every provider that supports the space (the default since the 14 Nov 2026 live slot) (a requirement is visible at once only on the provider it was added through, so the data provider's listing alone misses one added just before the reset) with `effQosInheritancePath` and `include_directories`, its pages ended by a null `nextPageToken` and refused when a path was inaccessible, the entries united, then a summary per entry a requirement is on, read on the provider that marks it, and each requirement's record, or, with `qos_scan = "summaries"`, a summary per leaf on every provider; the slot showed the listing takes `include_directories` and found a requirement added through the mirror (3.89 s, by hand), and `qos_scan` is in no fingerprint. A requirement in the run-start baseline is kept. Every other is read on every provider until it is `fulfilled` or `impossible` there (a provider dbsync has not reached yet is waited for) within `[placement] qos_fulfil_timeout_s` (120 s), or the reset fails with nothing deleted; it is then deleted, and a deletion barrier holds until every provider's summary of its entry no longer lists it and its record answers that it is not there, within 60 s, before any file is settled or evicted; a second scan shows them gone. A deletion cancels the replication only on the provider that processes it, and best-effort: the replicating provider goes on until dbsync brings it the deletion (about 8 s, 15 s more on a gap), and an eviction it processes before then is cancelled for every file the requirement still holds there. `pending` does not prove that bytes are moving; no REST answer shows bytes in flight. The files the removed ones applied to must then keep their block maps unchanged for 3 s within 60 s on every provider, and after the eviction and the occupancy (read once every eviction's change can show in it, O46), the block maps of the eviction plan's files (each on the providers it was evicted from) and of those files are read once, at one instant T, and must hold no more than the eviction (nothing) or the settle (what it saw) left; the occupancy is then read at T plus the space root's longest occupancy lag for the deepest file (10 s for the file location's flush, 5 s per level above its parent, from the source) plus a margin of 5 s, and no provider other than the data provider may hold more of the space than at the occupancy stage. **What the stage proves, exactly: no replica of the plan's files, and no occupancy growth, had landed by T.** The occupancy read then shows every placement that landed by T; **a landing after T is not certified**, since it may not show yet and no map is read again. The block maps decide for the plan's files, and a zero occupancy is never taken alone as nothing having landed. **The quiet check does not wait for a requirement to be found.** **The residual:** a fulfilment whose cancel missed may still be replicating one file, with no requirement left and no transfer document to show it: one of a requirement the agent added and removed during the run, or of one the reset removed after a reconciliation restarted between its fulfilled status and its deletion reaching the replicating provider. It is at most one file's size per missed cancel, and rare, since it needs a missed cancel; it may land after T, and is then caught by the next start's reset. `run(qos_rights=)` answers *could any run since the last verified reset have set a QoS requirement*: a study's reset runs at the start of each run and clears what earlier runs left, so it is never the rights of the run about to start, and a read-only run that follows a writing run would skip the check exactly when it is needed. `True` or `None` runs the check (fail safe, the default); `False` is for a caller that knows no run since the last verified reset had `space_manage_qos`, and `Condition.manages_qos`, which describes one condition, is combined over every such run. The check covers the occupancy read, which is made after the wait whenever the stage runs (an empty plan included), and the block maps of the eviction plan's own files, the ones the reset evicts from a provider other than the data provider, listed by the eviction and never by a second walk; it records why it ran (`requirements`, `rights` or `default`), the size and source of those files (`held_files`, `held_source` `"eviction plan"`) and its seconds. No block map of the rest of the space is read, so a replication that lands before T on a file the plan does not cover is caught by the occupancy alone. Only `qos_rights=False` with no requirement removed skips it. It costs the wait (20 s for files one directory below the root, 15 s directly under it, 5 s more per further level) and one block-map read per evicted (file, provider) pair of the plan, once: bounded by the eviction plan, never by the size of the space. `qos_settle` stays keyed on the affected files. *Learned:* #209, from the first real study: the per-file summaries cost about 52,000 reads, 9 to 43 minutes, per reset of a large tree, and a replication an agent's requirement started, which creates no transfer document, could still be running when the reset began and land after the eviction; another study measured a directory's physical size 9.5 s behind a placement and 0.05 s behind an eviction (the eviction's figure since contradicted live, O46); and a deletion cancels a requirement's replication only on the provider that processes it, best-effort, while the replicating provider learns of it by dbsync and cancels an eviction it processes first. Read from op-worker `f118ed9` (`qos_traverse.erl:88-93`, `replica_synchronizer.erl:224-231`, `qos_logic.erl:84-95`, `qos_status.erl:107-137`, `replica_deletion_master.erl:351-371`, `qos_rest_routes.erl:31-101`, `qos_traverse.erl:298-305,332-338`, `file_qos.erl:262-291`, `basic_file_operations_rest_routes.erl:59-70`, `file_rest_translator.erl:92-97`, `fslogic_cache.erl:524-530,706-715`, `dir_stats_collector.erl:168`, `rel/files/app.config:511`). *Test:* an agent's requirements on a file and a directory are removed and the baseline's kept, by either scan; a requirement still `pending` is waited on and deleted only once fulfilled on both providers, one that never settles fails the reset at the 120 s bound and is not deleted, `impossible` is settled, and one a provider has not received yet is waited for there; the barrier waits for the slowest provider, past the settings' `settle_timeout_s`, and a deletion that never reaches a provider fails the reset at 60 s; a reconciliation still replicating on a provider the deletion has not reached lands its last file before the eviction and none after; an add-then-remove whose cancel missed lands after T, the reset passes, and the next start's reset fails at its occupancy stage, the limit pinned; the fake's addition and removal reach the other provider late, its requirement is `pending` until its replication has landed, its replication lands until the removal arrives, and a missed cancel lands one file; the listing scan of 2,500 more files reads three pages on each of the two providers, two summaries on each and two records where the summaries scan reads one per leaf per provider; a requirement only beta has received, which alpha, the data provider, has not, is found and removed by either scan, where a listing of alpha alone finds none; one directory two providers mark holds the requirements each shows, its summary read on each; a page without its end marker, or with an inaccessible path, fails the qos stage and removes nothing; the first page sends no token and the pages follow to the null one; a quiet reset records both stages and their seconds; with no requirement removed and `qos_rights=False` neither stage reads or waits; a caller that says `True`, or does not say, runs the quiet check with no requirement left and records `rights` or `default`, the plan's file count and its source `eviction plan`, and its seconds; in a space of 1,500 more files the quiet stage reads the evicted files' block maps alone (six reads, once, at T, against 1,506 before the eviction), for a plan of a directory and for a plan of file paths; an agent's requirement added and removed before the reset, its replication landing after the eviction was verified and before T, fails the quiet stage on an evicted file's block map without waiting, and one landing there on a file the plan does not cover, on the occupancy, which lags the landing by 15 s at depth 2 in the fake and is read at T + 15 s + the margin, for an empty plan too; a file a removed requirement applied to that the plan does not evict is held to the map the settle ended on; the occupancy is read exactly the quiet wait after the last block map; bytes that land as the wait ends, after T, are not caught, and the test says so; the fake's directory size shows a placement 10 + 5 x (L - 1) s after it, and an eviction as late (O46); maps that change once settle and maps that keep changing fail qos_settle at its bound, which counts the waits and never a long reading of many files; the wait is 10, 15 and 20 s plus the 5 s margin at depths 1 to 3 and grows with the deepest file; the setting is one of the two and `listing` by default, in no fingerprint, and a reset on the default settings finds requirements by the listing; the fake's listing marks requirements on an entry and its ancestors; and gated live tests check the listing's `include_directories`, a requirement found through another provider, and the root's occupancy lag against the source's bound. **One listing for a planner and the scan (O51):** a planner's reading (`SpaceReset.plan_scan`, `resetplan.py`) makes the recursive listing once, each provider's listing read at once, and a reset given it (`run(planned=)`) takes its scan as the qos stage's first, refusing one of another space or of other providers before any stage, and makes the second scan itself; a requirement added between the planner's scan and the reset is missed, as one added just after the reset's own first scan would be, and the record gives `scan: "given"` and `scan_age_s`.

**O34. A cell's deadline holds even when a process of the cell outlives its kill, and such a cell is never the agent's outcome.** At a cell's cap the harness kills every process of the cell, taken from the agent container's cgroup (its init and the init's `sleep` excepted), so a worker that left the cell's process group or session goes too. A process left after `[sandbox] kill_grace_s` (5 s by default, 1 to 60) is recorded with its state, and the sandbox aborts the mount's FUSE connection from the oneclient sidecar, which holds the mount and SYS_ADMIN (fusectl mounted in the sidecar's own namespace when it is not there, then the connection's `abort` written; nothing runs in the agent's container), then waits the grace again; an abort that fails, cannot be made (no FUSE mount) or frees nothing orders the sandbox's stop, whose ordered teardown has its own last resorts (O15) and runs once the outcome is recorded, never inside the classification or the cell's time. Each grace is counted by the clock, every command given only the time left, and the whole escalation lasts at most `deadline.margin_s` past the cap (two graces and 20 s); when the margin runs out the escalation stops waiting. A listing or kill of the escalation that raises (its `HostOps` call failed, not the cell) is recorded as the step `raised`, with what was under way and the error's class, and the sandbox's quiesce kills what is left: every process shown gone is the clean kill it would have been, `killed`, unless the mount was aborted before the raise; a process left in whatever state, or none it could kill or list, is `survived_kill`, and the stop is ordered. An outcome never gets less severe than a step already taken (`killed` < `mount_aborted` < `sandbox_stopped` < `survived_kill`): once the mount was aborted, or the stop reached, the outcome is at least that whatever a later fallback finds, so a raise after a successful abort is `mount_aborted` with the run ended, never the agent's `time_limit` with later cells allowed on the dead mount. Every wait for the outcome, the cell's own `exec` included, lasts at most the margin and 30 s (`SETTLE_SLACK_S`), so an escalation that stalls, the quiesce of one that raised among them, never holds a cell, its worker thread or `asyncio.run`'s shutdown; an outcome not decided by then is published for the cell as `survived_kill`, and that stays the run's end: an outcome the escalation decides later is kept among the cell's deadlines and never replaces it. Only the init's own `sleep`, identified by its pid and start time when the container started, is spared by the kill: a process of the agent's can rename itself `sleep` and be reparented to the init. A lazy unmount is no abort: it leaves the agent's copy of the mount and the reads waiting on it. Processes that cannot be listed are never taken for gone. Each deadline is recorded with the wall-clock time of the cap and each step's seconds since it, and ends `killed`, `mount_aborted`, `sandbox_stopped`, or `survived_kill` when the margin ran out first, the escalation raised and its quiesce did not show the cell gone, or the outcome was not decided in time. `killed` is the agent's own time limit, as before (O17); any other outcome makes the cell `kill_survived`, whatever the counters say (after an abort or a stop they describe the harness's doing), and its attempt infrastructure of the platform kind `cell_unkillable`, which is not treatable, so no condition declares it, an outage's included, and it comes before the gate's refusals. The run is then over: every later cell raises that kind, so none reads a dead mount as an empty directory, and so does the collection of a run that was stopped; after an abort alone the outputs are collected as evidence. *Learned:* the outage rerun of 12 Nov (#178, `deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-provider-outage-rerun.md`): with the data provider stopped, a read through the mount of a file only that provider held had read 0 bytes at its 60 s bound, was killed, and had not ended 10 s later; a process waiting in the kernel on a FUSE request takes its kill only when the request returns. The sandbox's kill at the cap then waited 10 s for it and went on, the cell's `docker exec` waited for the read, and the first real study's wiring had to bound the cell itself with a thread, a grace period and a stop (#190). oneclient 25.0 lists `--provider-timeout` and `--storage-timeout`, 120 s each by default (`2026-11-11-federation-facts-mount.md`); whether either ends such a read with an errno is unmeasured, and enabling one changes the mount's behaviour, which is the study's to decide. A review after the first merge (#212) found two holes: the cell's own wait for the outcome had no bound, so a stalled escalation held its `exec` and the worker thread under it for ever, and a kill whose `HostOps` call raised fell back on the quiesce, ignored what it found and published nothing, so the cell was the agent's `time_limit` and later cells ran; the fix for the second made a third: after a successful abort, a kill that raised while waiting for a newly found process and a quiesce that found nothing left were `killed` again, the agent's `time_limit`, with `ended` unset. *Test:* with a fake process tracker, processes that end at the kill are `killed` with nothing aborted; one that ignores SIGTERM and SIGKILL is recorded as survived after the whole grace, with its state, and the abort that releases it makes `mount_aborted`; an abort that fails, one with no mount to abort and one that frees nothing each order the stop; a listing that takes 4 s cannot stretch a 5 s grace, and an abort that takes all the time it is given ends the escalation at its margin as `survived_kill`; the sandbox's stop runs after the run's `ended` is published and the cell returns without waiting for its teardown; a renamed `sleep` under the init, and a process that took the init's `sleep`'s pid, are killed, and a start whose init's `sleep` cannot be identified fails; an unreadable listing escalates; a process forked during the grace is killed; through the sandbox on the fake host, a normal cell killed at its cap is still the agent's time limit and the run goes on, the kill reaches a grandchild and never the init or its `sleep`, a wedged cell has the abort run in the sidecar alone, as root, with the connection of the run's mount from the host's table, its attempt `cell_unkillable` and the next cell refused with that kind; a failed abort stops the sandbox, and a later cell and the collection raise that kind; no data path stops the sandbox; the code tool returns the cell's own result, a timeout, and the next call raises the kind (O72); through `launch`, the agent loop and the code tool, whose watch cuts the call at the agent's cap, a cell that outlives its kill ends the run as `cell_unkillable`, not the loop's `time_cap`, and so does one whose deadline decides before the loop cuts, whose call the loop records as that cell's own timeout (the loop's cut and the cell's own timer fall on the same moment of the real clock and are a few milliseconds apart, so each test holds its order with events, never the clock, #226); `collect` settles a cell the loop cut before it decides, so a cell that outlives its kill is never collected as quiet; the grace is read within its bounds and recorded; a kill that raises is `raised`, with its step and error, and `killed` only when the fallback shows every process gone, `survived_kill` with the stop ordered when one is left, none can be listed, or the fallback raises or is missing; the outcome is never less severe than a step reached: after a successful abort, a kill that raises for a process found while waiting and a fallback that finds nothing is `mount_aborted` with no stop, a process left or none listed `survived_kill` with the stop ordered, and through the sandbox it is `kill_survived`, `cell_unkillable`, the run ended and the next cell refused; for every pair of a reached and a decided outcome the more severe is recorded, a stop outcome has its stop ordered, and a fallback that finds nothing after the abort or the stop is never `killed`; a timer whose kill never decides answers its cell's `end` within the seconds it is given; through the sandbox, a cell whose escalation never decides returns after one bounded wait of the margin and 30 s, by a fake clock, as `kill_survived`, `cell_unkillable`, with the next cell refused; one whose escalation decides an abort only after that wait keeps `survived_kill` as the run's end, with the late outcome among its deadlines, and its collection is refused; a kill whose `HostOps` call raises, with a quiesce that cannot kill, leaves a process in D or cannot list, is `cell_unkillable` with its step and error, ends the run, refuses the next cell and stops the sandbox, and with a quiesce that kills every process is the agent's `time_limit` and the run goes on; a gated live test stops a provider through the fault injector, reads a file only it holds through the mount with a 20 s cap, and checks the survived step, the abort, the hard bound, the kind, the restore and the teardown.

**O35. Every provider link a run can fetch through, between any two providers that share a space, is shown to transfer before any run, after a restore, and again while the campaign runs, or nothing runs.** A pair is a reading provider and a source, two different providers that support a declared space in common, and every ordered pair is checked: the agent's local provider's, and the others' too, since replication moves data between any two supporters (n·(n-1) pairs, 6 for three providers, 20 for five). For each, the check writes one probe file of its own under 1 MiB through the source (a file per pair, never one per source, so that a copy a first reader holds never serves a second reader's fetch) into a probe space no agent can see (`[link_check]`, never a study space and never under a study prefix, refused at load otherwise, and supported by the local provider and every source, or the untested pair is named at load), makes it cold (the reading provider's block map empty of it, and no other provider of the probe space holding any: the whole block map is read, a holder `[link_check]` names is evicted and confirmed, any other holder or a holding that cannot be read fails the pair, named), and reads it whole through the reading provider once, within `timeout_s` (30 s by default), checking its bytes. A pair that does not pass fails closed, named by position with the remedy (restart the reading provider's rtransfer link, `rtransfer_config:restart_link()`, or its container alone; never a Compose `down`): a provider waits on its synchronizer with no timeout and restarts a stuck job only after 1,800 s, so the read is never tried again in the same check, and a probe whose fetch failed is retired for good: **a file id whose fetch ever failed is never read again**, whatever its name or generation and whether the file still exists. The retirement is recorded first in the probe space (an empty marker file per retired id, named for digests of the pair's key and of the id, written through the reading provider; a marker is never read or dropped by another pair), then the next generation is written through the source under a new name and file id and the failed file deleted, best-effort; probe selection leaves out every id a marker names, a pair with no probe left and none writable fails closed ("no fresh probe for providers[j] to providers[i]: creation failed"), a retired file that stays is deleted by a later passing check, and a marker is dropped once its file is gone, so the next check never reads a file a stuck job may still be waiting on. The probe is evicted from its reading provider after a passing check, and the next check confirms it gone before it reads. It runs at the health gate's first call, never kept as a refusal, and again at every call once `every_s` (600 s) passed or the last check failed, so a stuck link holds every run until a re-check after its remedy passes, and one that sticks mid-campaign closes the gate, sets the run that found it aside as infrastructure and holds the whole campaign the same way; after a fault injector's restore, for every ordered pair (not the restored provider's alone), and the restore stays unverified (`links_stuck`) until each passes, but only once a session gate passed (Onezone shows every provider online and each peer's block map of a probe file made before the injection holds an answer, not an error, for the restored provider, within `gate_timeout_s`, else `sessions_not_ready`), so the check never fetches through a peer whose session to the restored provider is still renewing; a declared heal (`[faults] heal`, off by default), whose primary mode `all_links` restarts every provider's link in quick succession with nothing fetched in between and no transfer active, settles with no fetch and no run, checks every pair afresh and falls back to a stuck reader's container, never restarts the container of the source of a failing pair or of the restored provider, never issues a second link restart to a provider (a timed-out one may still be running; the heal is journalled before it acts, so no later clear or process heals the same restore again), acts only while the federation is held exclusively, makes at most one of each restart per provider per restore within 2 passes and `heal_timeout_s` (derived when undeclared, from the very least the load check holds a declared value to plus 10 %, to the minute), and stops closed on any pair a step breaks (`heal_broke_pair`) or a transfer still active (`transfers_active`), so a restore is verified only with every pair passing; and since the tool server's stop after the restore examines the run's token, the restore always runs within a token margin the study declares and that outlives it and its gate (`[tokens] margin_s`, never implied, at least the restore's declared bounds, 300 s of slack and the session drain a clear that takes the federation itself waits out before it restores, a missing or shorter one refused at load naming the least, its derivation and the line to write), and a heal is skipped and recorded while a step of the run that uses its tokens (the tool server's launch, its stop's re-verification included, or the sandbox's) still runs, unless the study declared the long margin (that margin, twice `heal_timeout_s` and one `session_drain_s`), a stuck pair then leaving the restore unverified for a start with no such step to heal, while every step that uses a token (the tool server's checks, the sandbox's mint and start) is refused, naming it, without the run's admission held, and a failed restore is tried again only once no such step runs, else left open for the next start's recovery with the run set aside, so one restore falls inside the token's window, as the margin counts; in the fault gate's health check, which, in a check that holds no run's admission and whose own all-pairs check of that call found a stuck pair, heals it the same way (`[faults] heal`, its bounds and settle) under an exclusive admission of its own, waiting at most `health_wait_s` for every shared holder (a campaign run, a tool outside the campaign) and for the drain record and failing closed, uncharged (`federation_busy`), when either outlasts the wait, acts only on the report its own call made and only when the heal's fresh matrix still shows a failed fetch, never restarts the container of a provider that was ever the source of a stuck pair, writes each settle to the federation's drain record, runs in the background while every check answers the uncharged deferral (so a heal longer than `health_timeout_s` charges no run, and one still running past `heal_timeout_s` plus `heal_settle_s` closes the gate, uncharged), declares that bound after a session drain (`session_drain_s` plus `heal_timeout_s` plus `heal_settle_s`, and how many of the core's waits around them it includes: two rechecks and two checks, or one of each with no drain) as its check's longest deferral, so a campaign whose `max_defer_s` is shorter is refused at its start and the heal never becomes a blocking hold that stops it (C11), opens only once its own link check, made afresh under that admission, passes, and heals once per stuck episode, recorded durably (`gate-heals.jsonl`), until a link check passes; at a run's admission, which, holding the lock, compares the federation's link epoch (`link-epoch.json`, raised before any heal, the gate's or a restore's, and whenever the gate's link check finds a pair failing) with the one the run's pre-run check noted, after every link check under way has ended and raised it, and refuses the run, uncharged (`federation_busy`), when it moved or its record is missing or made anew (a raise that cannot be written removes it or empties it in place, and when it can do neither the gate keeps the lock the admission waits for until the raise is recorded), when the check noted none, when a heal is on record since the last passing link check, or when a check under way outlasts the admission's bound, so no run whose check a heal or a failing pair outdated is admitted onto the stuck pair, and a link report older than the epoch is no answer to the gate's next check; and last in `deploy/compose-federation`'s `up`, which is not ready until it passes. A federation with pairs and no `[link_check]` admits no run. The link-check record that `up` writes (`link-check.json`, the report of the check, in one format and one atomic writer, `expcore_onedata.linkrecord`) follows the latest check: every all-pairs check of a restore, of a heal (its passing re-check included) and of the health gate's routine pre-run check writes it in the federation's root, where `up` writes it, whichever platform file the check ran through (the root is the parent of the `[faults] journal` the file names, so a file derived in another directory writes the root's record and nothing beside itself; a journal whose parent holds no `platform.toml` is refused at load, and a check without `[faults]` declares no root and writes none), pass or fail, so that after a heal the operator's pre-check, which wants a check under an hour old, finds the heal's own re-check and not the one from before the outage (which would make it re-run a whole `up`); one check at a time makes it (the check and its write under a leaf lock beside it, a file opened for writing because NFS grants an exclusive `flock` only on a descriptor open for writing, and opened read-only only when the process cannot write it), so a check that finishes late never replaces a later-started check's record; a check that cannot answer, or whose record cannot be written, removes the older record (or empties it in place, dated to the epoch, when the directory refuses the removal) so that a stale pass never stands, and says so beyond a log line (a check whose record was not written, its lock not taken or the write refused, answers with a report that says `lock_not_taken` or `write_failed`, and the run whose restore, heal or routine check it was has the optional fact `link_record: "not_written"` with `link_record_reason` in its record: the fault injector's snapshot, at its top, as a `link_record` step and in a `recovered` step, the heal's matrices, the try's note and `PlatformHealth.link_record` for a routine check, and the day-0 outage's restore facts; the fact is absent, never null, when the record was written, and is never in the record file), and the record is advisory, taking none of the federation's locks and never touching the link epoch or the journal. A run refused at its admission because of a heal that ended with a pair still failing is refused with the failed heal's reason first, the gate's own (`a failed heal: operator action needed (stuck pairs: ...)`, by label), so its `run_unstarted` event says an operator is needed; it is the same uncharged hold, and a heal that mended its pairs, one under way or cut short, a failed heal the gate's link check has since seen mended and a record that names no pair by a label leave the refusal's words as they were. *Learned:* the first real study's federation, where one provider's link to a peer stuck with nothing logged and the peer reachable by TCP and TLS, while every other pair transferred; every read of that peer's files through the stuck provider hung to its cell's bound, and the only symptom was an agent's hung read, which the study would have scored; the stuck link cleared about 1,800 s later, the synchronizer's inactivity restart (#221). The live slot of 14 Nov 2026 then showed the gap in the first check, which tested only the local provider's pairs: after a provider's outage and restore, a mirror's link to the local provider stayed stuck (every read through the mirror of a file only the local provider held timed out at 60 s, the local provider's rtransfer link having crashed during the outage and the mirror's not reconnecting), a pair that neither the restore's check (the restored provider's pairs) nor `up`'s (the local provider's) tested. Another study's federation then showed the same pattern for 46 minutes and more: every pair with the crashed provider as source failing, but the one whose reader started after the crash, and a pair untested at the first check failing later; and op-worker's source shows that a peer's session renewal toward a restored provider backs off to 180 s, that a fetch through a peer whose session is not up raises in the rtransfer callback, and that `rtransfer_worker_sup` allows 10 restarts of the link's supervisor in 3,600 s under a top supervisor that allows none, so an 11th within an hour may take the node down (inference, not measured). The first default of `heal_timeout_s`, a fixed 4,200 s, was refused at load by the loader's own check for a five-provider federation, which needs 4,290 s: a default the check refuses for a common size is wrong, so it is derived from what the check asks. With the executor's documented order a run's tokens are already in play when its injector's start recovery runs, so that recovery's heal is skipped (no heal while a token-dependent step runs) and the pairs it would have healed wait; the health check runs before any attempt launches anything, so it heals them. A live heal then ended with a passing re-check of every pair, and the federation's link-check record was still the one `up` wrote before the outage, since neither the heal's re-check nor a restore's link check wrote it: the next pre-check, which wants a check under an hour old, had to run a whole `up`. **O36. The mount's I/O mode is declared and never left to oneclient's own detection, which hangs the first read of a second space on one storage.** `[sandbox.mount] io_mode` is required, `"proxy"` or `"direct"`, and `"direct"` loads only with its storage mapping and its check file (O40). A mount table without a mode, with another word, or whose `extra_args` carry a force-I/O option (or an abbreviation of one), the TLS flag, `--config` or an `ONECLIENT_` assignment is refused at load, and the sidecar's oneclient is always started with exactly one of `--force-proxy-io` and `--force-direct-io`, so a mount of the core never reaches oneclient's detection; `MountSettings` raises for the same where it is built, and the command raises again, so settings built or changed in code cannot give two force options or a second configuration. The sidecar also passes `--config /dev/null` and removes `ONECLIENT_OVERRIDE` and `ONECLIENT_CONFIG` from the environment (with `ONECLIENT_INSECURE`), so its effective options are its argv and the token. The run's metadata records the effective mode (`mount.io_mode`, never None), the options the sandbox passed (`mount.options`), the study's extra arguments by the names of the options oneclient has (`<value>` for any other word that starts with a dash, which may be a value) and the SHA-256 of the list (`mount.extra_args`), never a value (O12), and the path and SHA-256 of the oneclient configuration file the image carries (`mount.image_config`, `unread` when it could not be hashed), which is never parsed. Direct I/O's path, `performForcedDirectIOStorageDetection`, takes the mutex once, in a block that ends before anything else is called. The I/O mode is in the campaign fingerprint for each mount level (`platform.data_paths.<level>.io_mode`), so a resume under the other mode is refused, and so is the resume of a store whose mount levels recorded none; `extra_args` is not, but the platform file's SHA-256 changes with the table. *Learned:* the first federation study's run of 13 Nov: the first space a oneclient process read worked and the first read of any other space hung, with nothing logged. Read from oneclient 25.0 (tag `bb8a63e3`), `src/cache/helpersCache.h`: without `--force-proxy-io`, `get()` (371-459) posts `performAutoIOStorageDetection` (463-615) for each (storage, space). The first space finds the access type unset and returns a proxy helper, leaving `m_accessType[storage]` at `PROXY`. For a second space on that storage `accessUnset` is false, and the code takes `std::lock_guard<std::mutex> guard(m_accessTypeMutex)` (596) and, the value being `PROXY`, returns `performAutoIOStorageDetection(..., true)` (598) while holding it. The recursive call locks the same plain `std::mutex` (declared at 265) again on the same thread (478): a self-deadlock, so the helper's promise is never fulfilled, no `SynchronizeBlock` is sent and the read hangs. With `--force-proxy-io`, `get()` sets `forceProxyIO` first (427) and that branch is never entered. The study's lead then ruled that oneclient's behaviour is declared up front and nothing is left to its detection; the audit of the client's other options (`src/options/options.h` 40-68, `options.cc`) found the storage's access type the only value decided at run time, the rest being constants or switches that are off. *Test:* a mount table without `io_mode` is refused naming both choices, the reason and the source; `proxy` loads as written, `direct` without its storage mapping is refused naming it (O40), and `auto`, `detect` and the empty word are refused; an extra argument that is either force option, with a value or abbreviated, the config-file option in any spelling, or an `ONECLIENT_` assignment is refused, and so is the same built in code (`MountSettings`, and the command for settings changed past their check); the default document's argv holds `--force-proxy-io` once and never the other option, and a declared `direct` holds `--force-direct-io` alone; settings built with no mode or an unknown one raise at the command, and the sandbox's options are the command's own; the command carries `-u ONECLIENT_OVERRIDE -u ONECLIENT_CONFIG` beside `-u ONECLIENT_INSECURE`, `--config /dev/null` once, and leaves the token's file and variable alone; the metadata records `proxy` for the default document, the options as passed, the extra arguments by the names of oneclient's own options and a hash, with `["--log-dir", "--private/path"]` recorded as `--log-dir` and `<value>`, and the hash of the image's configuration file, or `unread`, as the sidecar's shell prints it (a real shell run against a stand-in for oneclient); the fingerprint holds the mode of each mount level, a resume under the other mode is refused, and so is the resume of a store that recorded none, naming the field; oneclient's start lines are read and compared with the argv, a differing or missing line being named; the cell that reads a file of each space reports `read` for a file whose size and digest match, `mismatch`, a missing file as ENOENT and a read that never returns as `blocked` while the other spaces are still read; and a gated live test mounts a federation of two or more spaces in one sandbox and reads a small file from each through one oneclient process within a bound, with the file's bytes, then checks the recorded mode, that oneclient's logged options are exactly the argv's (or its defaults) and that the token's is the only variable of its environment it reads an option from, the recorded hash of the image's configuration file, and the teardown.

**O37. A REST read says what the platform answered, and never takes a whole file, a mismatched range or an empty answer for the bytes asked for.** The reads of a file's content (`OnezoneClient.read_range`, `read_content`, and the provisioner's `fetch` and `read_file`) report, through `answers`, one `ReadOutcome` per accepted answer: the platform's status, its `Content-Range` as sent and the bytes of its body, so a study tells a whole answer from a partial one, and a range clipped at the file's end (a 206 with a shorter range) from a full one, without inferring it from the request. An answer is accepted only as a 206 whose `Content-Range` is `bytes first-last/total`, starts where it was asked, ends inside the file it names and holds exactly the bytes it says; a 200, a missing or malformed range, another start, a range that ends past the end of the file or before it starts, and a body that is short for the header are each a `PlatformError`, and report nothing. The data gate's line for each answer of a file's content carries `content_range` (null on a 200) beside its status and bytes. *Learned:* the first real study's Day-0 wiring (#198): every read recorded as a 200 with no range, since the reader returned bytes alone. *Test:* a whole read (200), a ranged read (206 with its range) and a range clipped at the end, through the client, the streamed read, the provisioner and a real gate.

**O38. A platform built without slots and a journal takes no lease, mints as the fixed data-path user and never as a slot's, and refuses what needs a slot, naming what is missing.** `SandboxPlatform` took `slots` and the token journal even for a run that leased nothing, so a study that wanted a one-off check invented both. They are optional, together: a lease-less platform gives no run a lease group, mints a data-path token as the fixed `[principals.data_path]` user (a run with no data path mints nothing), journals and revokes nothing, and never touches a slot's users or tokens. `lease_keys`, `condition_group`, `probe`, a tool-server condition (at the plan's `lease_group` and at `launch`) and a `launch` with a lease are each a `ConfigError` that says the platform was built without `slots` and `journal`, raised before anything is minted or created; one of the two without the other is a `ValueError` naming the one missing. A platform built with both is unchanged. *Learned:* the first real study's Day-0 wiring (#199). *Test:* a lease-less platform builds, launches a data-path run as the fixed user, and refuses each call that needs a slot; one with slots and a journal leases as before (`test_provider.py`, `test_lease_groups.py`).

**O39. A harness step ended by the sandbox's own bound says so, and a step that exits 137 by itself is not a timeout.** `exec(code, timeout_s, record=False)` starts no timer, so its only limit is the container's `timeout -s KILL`, `CAP_MARGIN_S` after `timeout_s`, and a step killed there returned exit 137 (the code `os.kill(os.getpid(), 9)` gives too) with `timed_out` false: a caller could not tell a hung read from a crash, and a study's probe mislabelled its hung reads as crashed. The result now says `timed_out` and names the bound (`ExecResult.bound`: `cell_timer`, `container_timeout` or `host_call`, None exactly when not timed out), and the harness step's record carries both. The backstop leaves nothing but its exit code, so the time decides: exit 137 or 124 after the whole of the limit is the bound's kill, and the same codes sooner, or any other code after it, are the cell's own. A cell of the agent's (`record=True`) is unchanged (O17, O34). *Learned:* #220, the first study's reading of the sandbox's cells. *Test:* a step the container's timeout killed after its limit is `timed_out` with `container_timeout`, in the result and in `harness_steps`; a step that exits 137 or 124 at once is neither; a step that ran its whole limit and exited 0 is neither; a cell of the agent's names `cell_timer` and keeps its kill record.

**O40. A direct mount reads the storage it declares, from the sidecar alone, and is handed out only once its start shows direct I/O in effect.** `io_mode = "direct"` loads only with `[sandbox.mount.storages]` (each storage id the sidecar reads directly, and the host directory that holds it) and `direct_check_file` (a file the local provider holds in full), and a proxy mount is refused with either. Each storage is bound read-only into the sidecar alone, at a path of its position, and named to oneclient by `--override <id>:mountPoint:<that path>`; the agent's container never gets one, nor does a read-only input from one, and no storage may be, hold or lie under the work root. The start refuses the sandbox unless the agent read the whole check file, held in full by the local provider and on a mapped storage, and the sidecar's log says forced direct, no forced proxy, and holds no line of detection or fallback; the collection reads the log again and sets aside a run in which a read went through the provider since the start, or whose log cannot be read. The record keeps counts and the check's facts, never an id or a path (O12). *Learned:* the PI's direction of 13 Nov (full experiments on HPC, the providers' storage on a shared filesystem, oneclient in direct I/O), and oneclient 25.0's own code (tag `bb8a63e3`). Forced direct I/O still falls back to the provider per file: a direct read refused with EPERM or EACCES logs an ERROR line, puts the file in the force-proxy cache and is read again through `performAutoIOStorageDetection` with proxy forced (`fsLogic.cc`, `readInternal`, lines 1176-1203), so a read that returned every byte is no evidence of direct I/O; forced detection marks the storage's access type DIRECT before it tries anything (`helpersCache.h`, lines 626-629), so that attribute is none either; `checkPosixMountpointOverride`'s answer is ignored on the forced path (line 680), so a wrong host directory gives ENOENT, retried with delays; and the POSIX helper reads as the uid and gid the provider sends (`posixHelper.cc`, `UserCtxSetter`), so the storage's modes decide whether a direct read is refused. glog holds INFO lines up to 30 s unless log buffering is off, so the sidecar passes `--disable-log-buffering` with direct I/O (and with every mount, O54). one without either, with an empty mapping, a storage id an override cannot carry, a host path that is not one plain absolute directory, two nested storages, one under or around the work root, or a check file that is not a path in a space, is refused by position without echoing a value, and so are a proxy mount with either key and the same built in code; `--override` and `-r` in any spelling are refused as extra arguments in either mode, and `--disable-log-buffering` in either mode too (O54); the command holds `--disable-log-buffering` and one override per storage, at its position's path, after the sandbox's options and before the study's, the recorded options hold no id or path, each storage is a read-only `--mount` bind into the sidecar, a proxy mount has none, and the agent's arguments name no storage; a direct start runs the probe in the agent and then reads the log in the sidecar, records the check without an id or path, and is refused, with nothing left, on a fallback line, start lines that do not say forced direct, an unreadable log, a partial read, a file not held in full (which the probe never reads), one of an unmapped storage, or a failed stat; a direct run whose log stays clean is collected and records it, one whose log gained a fallback line or cannot be read at collection is set aside as `output_collection`, and the log's lines can be read at any time, None when unreadable; a proxy start runs no check; a storage directory not on the host refuses the start first; a read-only input from, in or around a storage is refused; the probe, run on stand-ins, reads a held file whole, reads nothing of one not held in full, and kills a read that does not return by its deadline; each marker line is named; the harness's passes alternate which mode goes first, compare read by read so equal totals with traded bytes differ and the same reads with another final placement differ, compare every file of the measurement even when both passes failed, treat a direct pass whose reads went through the provider as incomplete, treat a failed, unsettled or unended replay as incomplete and never the same, answer yes only when every file compared and none differed, derive a direct platform that loads with the local provider's storage mapped, and say in their help what they compare and change.

**O41. A space provisioned by storage import holds exactly its dataset, is checked like an uploaded copy, and is never written where its storage is read-only.** A space whose data provider holds it on an imported storage is declared so (`[space] import = { readonly = ... }`, or in a `[[spaces]]` entry), checked at load and in the fingerprint only when declared. Its provisioning is shown, never assumed: the data provider's panel must show an imported storage, read-only as declared, in the auto mode with continuous scans off (a scan during a run with change detection on takes the mirrors' blocks); the scan is waited for, bounded, until a later scan than the one counted before has completed with no failed file, and `failed` or `aborted` fails at once; the scan's state is a report, and the evidence is the listing, which must hold exactly the manifest's paths, kinds and sizes and nothing more on consecutive reads on every provider named, and the block maps, on which every file must be whole on the data provider, with every file's SHA-256 read back when asked. An uploaded copy is checked by the same code. In a space held on a read-only imported storage the provisioner refuses, unsent, every creation, write and delete of a file or a directory through any provider and every replication onto the data provider; `docs/storage-import.md`): an imported file is made with a random id and one block `[0, size)` on the importing provider; a read-only storage refuses every write with EROFS, a delete there leaves the file on the storage with its sync record gone, so a re-scan imports it again under a new id;
