The reset reads the occupancy only once every eviction's change can show in it, so the quiet check is never held to a figure that still counts evicted bytes, and judges it by each file's block map, never by the directory-statistics counter alone.** The occupancy stage reads the space root's physical size, and the quiet stage, after its wait, holds the occupancy to that figure. An eviction's change reaches that size as late as a placement's: the 14 Nov live slot saw a mirror's root size drop 20.03 s after an eviction at depth 3 was scheduled, the job's own run included, where a placement at that depth showed after 15.91 s (bound 20 s) and one at depth 2 after 10.76 s (bound 15 s). Read right after the eviction, the occupancy stage's figure still held the evicted bytes: a reset that allows the mirror nothing failed after a good eviction, and one that allows it what it held passed, at its quiet stage, a growth up to the evicted bytes' size, a false certification. An earlier run's eviction that ended just before the reset did the same with an empty plan, and an earlier run's replication that had not shown yet passed the occupancy stage with the quiet check skipped. So the occupancy stage first waits `resetqos.occupancy_wait_s` from the eviction stage's end, 10 + 5 x (L - 1) s for the deepest regular file of the space, which the QoS scan saw, plus the 5 s margin, on every reset, an empty plan and `qos_rights=False` included; it then requires no transfer running and reads. It keeps the reading only when no transfer ran or ended since the wait began: the first page of each provider's ended listing, newest first by finish time, read at the eviction stage's end and again after the reading, holds no job the earlier one lacked, and no transfer is waiting or ongoing. Otherwise a job no stage sent changed the space after the instant the wait counts from, and the stage lets it end and waits again from then, up to `reset.OCCUPANCY_ROUNDS` (3) waits, and then fails. A listing shows a job up to `transfer_settle_s` late (O07), so a look that must see every job ended by a reading is made that long after it: the occupancy stage's own when the quiet stage is skipped; otherwise the quiet stage's, after its own occupancy read and against the same first pages, which fails the reset when a transfer ran or ended since the occupancy stage's wait began, since an eviction in that window can hide a growth in its reading and a job listed late made the occupancy stage's figure wrong. The reset's own jobs, the evictions it sent and the jobs it cancelled, all seen to end before the wait, never count as new, however late they are listed. **The conservative reading:** the slot cannot say how much of the 20.03 s was the eviction job's own run, so the reset applies the placement's lag to an eviction and counts it from the eviction stage's end, at or after every eviction job's finish and its block-map verification, never from the scheduling; every eviction and transfer of an earlier run had ended before the reset began, and none runs at that instant. The record gives the stage's `waited_s` (every wait together), `depth`, `rounds` and `listings_after_s`, and every reset costs the wait, and `transfer_settle_s` more for the late look: 20 s for files one directory below the root, 5 s more per further level. *Learned:* the 14 Nov live slot (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-14-federation-facts-slot.md`, item 4; op-worker f118ed9). The source suggests an eviction's change skips the file location's flush (`fslogic_location_cache.erl:345-354`) and another study measured 0.05 s; both readings are set aside for the measure. *Test:* with the plan evicting `study` from beta and beta allowed what it held, a replication that lands after the occupancy stage and before T fails the quiet stage on the occupancy, the stage's figure 0; an earlier run's eviction just before a reset with an empty plan hides no such growth; an earlier run's replication four directories down that ended just before the reset fails the occupancy stage with the quiet check skipped; a reset that allows beta nothing passes its occupancy stage after evicting it, recording the wait; the occupancy is read no earlier than the last eviction's finish time plus the wait for the deepest file of the space, a deeper file making it longer with no QoS right; the listings are read after the wait and before the occupancy; an eviction that ends during the wait makes the stage wait again, its figure then without the evicted bytes, and a landing at T no larger fails the quiet stage; a replication that ends during the wait is in the figure after the second wait and fails the occupancy stage with the quiet check skipped; an eviction that ends at T, beside a landing of its size, fails the quiet stage on the transfer; a job ending in every wait fails the occupancy stage after three; a replication listed 25 s late, within a `transfer_settle_s` of 30 s, is still waited for with the quiet check skipped, and an eviction listed as late during the quiet stage fails it; the reset's own evictions listed as late count as no new job, with the quiet check run or skipped; the wait is 15, 20, 25 and 30 s at depths 1 to 4; the slot's measures fit the waits; the fake's directory size shows an eviction, of a replica old or new, the same lag after it as a placement; and a gated live test checks that the root's size drops within the wait counted from an eviction's verified end, printing the seconds from its scheduling and from its end. **The verdict comes from the files** (`occupancy.py`): the space root's physical size is a directory-statistics counter, and on op-worker f118ed9 it credited a mirror 1,048,576 B for more than 23 minutes after the eviction emptied the space's only regular file there (depth 3, 43,096,327 B; the file's distribution showed the mirror physical 0 and blocks `[]`, the mirror's storage 0 B allocated, nothing else held, no transfer ran), an earlier 64 KiB read at offset 8 MiB having placed one 1 MiB block in the file's middle; and a placement of 1,572,864 B showed in it only 9.534 s later (34 polls, the Day-0 facts of 13 Nov), the counter reading 0 meanwhile, as the source's flush path explains (a placement's change waits `blocks_flush_delay`, 10 s, then climbs one directory per 5 s). The reset failed at its occupancy stage after a good eviction, with no figure. So the occupancy stage and the quiet stage read every regular file's block map (the `REG` files and `LNK` hard links any provider's listing showed the QoS scan, in either scan) at each provider other than the data provider, whatever the counter says, and judge the sum per provider, the quiet stage against the occupancy stage's sum; a provider the caller declares never holds the space's bytes (`run(never_holds=)`) is skipped only while its counter reads an explicit 0; a provider no declaration fits that supports the space fails the stage; a hard link is counted on its own, and a refusal with bytes in one says so; each provider whose counter differs from its files' sum gives an `occupancy_counter_disagrees` event with both figures; a refusal states the bytes held, the figure allowed and the counter per provider, and the files by position; the reads are bounded (`occupancy.READS_MAX`, `occupancy.READ_BOUND_S`) and fail closed; and an unknown block map or counter fails the stage, never counted as 0. `tests/onedata/placement/test_reset_occupancy_files.py`: a counter 1 MiB high with every file empty passes, with the event, the quiet check run or skipped, under either scan; a file holding 1,024 bytes from its middle with the counter at 0 fails, the message stating the bytes, the figure allowed, the counter and `files[2]`, and no path or id; files holding more than allowed fail whatever the counter says; a counter at 0 skips no provider that can hold, and skips one declared never to hold, which is still read when its counter is not 0; naming the data provider never to hold is refused; with the counter stale, a landing after the occupancy stage fails the quiet stage on the files' sums; a reading past its budget fails closed with the figures; a file's distribution with a failed storage entry, null blocks or the provider left out, or a counter that is null, fails the stage rather than read as 0; a file one provider's listing missed is still read there and fails the reset; an undeclared provider with a counter of 0 fails it; a provider declared never to hold that the counter leaves out is read; bytes in a hard link are counted and named; the time bound is looked at after the last batch; and one budget covers every call of a reading. **What the wait guards now:** the files' block maps show a placement and an eviction at once, so the verdict needs no wait; the wait from the eviction stage's end still guards the two uses of the counter, the skip of a provider declared never to hold while its counter reads an explicit 0 (which under-counts until the lag has passed) and the `occupancy_counter_disagrees` events (a counter read before the lag disagrees with every eviction). Polling the occupancy instead of the fixed wait is left for later: each poll would be a whole per-file reading. **Several spaces (O51):** a reset of several spaces takes the wait once, to the latest instant any space may read at, and each space's reading then comes no earlier than its own; the quiet stage's wait from each space's T and the listings' late look are each taken once likewise. Every wait is a lower bound, so one wait to the latest instant keeps every space's, and a job that ran or ended in the longer window still makes the stage wait again or fail. **The shortcut (O51):** with `[placement] reset_shortcut` and a planner's reading made inside the reset lock's exclusive section, the two stages read only the files that may have changed since that reading and carry the rest as empty (`files_carried`), judged by the same sums; any doubt about the premise gives the full reads. **A connection failure costs no reset, an answer is never retried** (O29): a live reset read 8,290 block maps at one provider and then one `GET` failed to connect (another study, at 9310fe9, host load near 19), the map was unknown and the reset failed closed, 1,238 s lost. Every read of a reading (the occupancy and quiet stages' block maps and counter, the shortcut's narrowed reads, the settle's, the quiet stage's maps at T, the QoS scans' root, pages, `summaries` mode's directory listings, summaries and records, and the planner's) now goes under a policy made for that reading (`Provisioner.reading_retry`), and each stage's own transfer reads (listings, records, the waits for its jobs, the eviction stage's included, Onezone's clock) under one made for the stage (`SpaceReset.stage_reads`, tallied as `transfer_read_retry`): a timeout or a connect, read or write error or remote protocol error is tried again, four attempts 2, 5 and 15 s apart, each retry on a fresh connection, the occupancy's within what is left of `READ_BOUND_S`. A 404 or a POSIX absence still counts 0 at once, and any other error answer, a provider's failed storage entry, an unreadable map and an error answer cut in its body fail at once; a read still failing after its fourth attempt is unread and the reading fails closed, as before. A 200 whose body broke off is no answer and is asked again; the retry's whole answer decides. Each reading records its tally (`read_retry`; the quiet stage's maps `maps_read_retry`): its reads, retries, failed attempts by error type, the reads that failed for good and the answers that were no plain success (error statuses, 404s among them, and unreadable maps), per provider, a failed stage's included, so the next failure shows whether it was load or the provider. **The gate, the space's name, the eviction's listings and visibility wait, and the root go under the reset's policy too** (O09): the mutation gate's reads before every cancel, eviction and QoS deletion (one gate per mutation, thousands in a large eviction), the visibility wait before each eviction (`Provisioner.await_visible`: the file's size on the data provider and the wait's reads at the evicting provider, two reads per file, thousands too), `verify_space` (the driver's, before any stage, under a policy of its own tallied as the record's `verify_read_retry`; the eviction's and the planner's under theirs), the eviction stage's path listings (`resolve_many`, `walk`) and the space root's first, kept read, each under the policy of the stage it is made in (the qos stage's `read_retry`, the transfers and eviction stages' `transfer_read_retry`, the occupancy's `read_retry`). The listings, the visibility reads and the root each hold one of their provider's slots through their retries (the visibility wait's pauses outside it); the gate's reads, made inside the mutation's call, hold none. The gate stays fail-closed: each attempt decides from its own answer, nothing an earlier attempt saw is reused, and after the fourth failed read the mutation is refused unsent (`ScopeUnread`). **A mutation is sent once, whatever the policy:** a cancel, an eviction or a QoS deletion whose answer is lost after it went past the gate (a transport failure, `client.sent_unanswered`) may or may not have been applied, and a second eviction would be a second job on the transfer listing, so none is ever sent again; the job's record, the block maps once no transfer runs, or the deletion barrier decide, failing closed at their bounds, and the stage records how many were lost (`unanswered`). No read a reset makes is left on timeouts only; the one-attempt polls (the requirements' fulfilment and deletion checks, the eviction's block maps) take a failed read for no match, the poll being the retry. *Test (`tests/onedata/placement/test_reset_transport_retry.py`):* a `ConnectError` on one read is retried and the reading passes, and one on every attempt fails closed with the tally, in each path (the occupancy stage, the occupancy's counter, the quiet stage's maps, the settle, the qos stage's listing, `summaries` mode's directory listing, the planner's reading, the shortcut's narrowed reads, the transfers stage's listing, the eviction stage's wait for its job, the history's reading of the global clock); a 404 counts 0 with one request, tallied as an answer; a 500, a POSIX `eacces`, a failed storage entry and a 500 cut in its body each fail after one request, no pause, the tally saying which; a 200 cut in its body is asked again. *Test (`tests/onedata/placement/test_reset_gate_retry.py`):* in each of the four paths, the gate before a cancel, an eviction and a QoS deletion, the eviction's `resolve_many` and `walk`, the driver's and the eviction's `verify_space`, and the root's first read in the eviction and the occupancy stages, one `ConnectError` is retried and the reset passes, and one on every attempt fails closed with the tally; a gate whose reads never connect is read four times and sends nothing after; a retried gate read whose answer names another space refuses the eviction; an eviction of a file and of a tree, a cancel and a QoS deletion that the provider applied and whose answers were lost are not sent again, the reset passes and one eviction job is listed; an eviction and a deletion that never arrived are not sent again and the stage fails closed; a visibility read before an eviction (the size on the data provider, the wait's at the evicting provider) that fails to connect once is retried and the eviction is sent once, one that never connects fails the stage after four attempts with nothing sent, a 500 to it fails at once after one request, and each is made holding its provider's slot; an instrument's read of the root or of a path refuses a policy; and a measuring module that hands a helper a reset's policy breaches (`tests/onedata/test_read_retry_sites.py`).

**O47. A large HTTP body is freed when its exchange is used, never at the next full collection, and no 3xx answer is taken for a result.** httpx binds every answer to a stream that points back at it, a reference cycle that holds the answer, its request and both bodies until the cyclic collector runs. A batch of large uploads or reads, or a re-ingest, then grew by everything it moved, and a host short of memory failed at whatever moment the collector happened to wait for, so the failures were intermittent: the first study's memory audit, run with the collector off, found eight files of 8 MiB uploaded and read back through the public fake leaving 128 MiB alive, and one 16 MiB upload leaving 16 MiB of request and 16 MiB of answer (#259). The client releases every answer the moment it is read (`client._release`: closed, its stream replaced by an empty one), however the read ends, a body cut short included (`httpx.Client.request` leaves such an answer in its cycle, so the request is sent and read in `client._exchange`, and an answer to the exchange's own request that httpx fails on inside its send, a redirect it cannot parse or a hook that raises, is found in the frames the failure passed through, released and counted), and it follows no redirect, whatever the HTTP client it was given does (httpx follows one inside its send, out of reach of the release when it fails halfway, and takes the `X-Auth-Token` header along to another host), and takes no 3xx answer for a result (the client accepted any status below 400, so a bodyless 3xx decoded to `{}`, an empty success and a false pass for a write as much as for a read): every 3xx, 300 to 399, is a `PlatformError` that names the status and whether a `Location` came, never its value, raised after the answer is released and never retried by the client's read retry or a probe's (its body is not read, so a body that stalls raises no timeout to retry; a poll that counts every failed read as one that did not match, `converge` and `converge_each`, reads it again within its own bound as it does any error and ends as not converged, never as a pass, and a wait that waits out only absence raises it at once), on every path that sends or receives a body: its requests (`get`, `get_dated`, `mutate`, `read_range`, on its own pool or a retry's one-shot client), the ranges of `read_content` and the head `content_answer` reads. It keeps no answer, request or body, and it raises a transport failure and a decoder's refusal with their handlers over, so a `PlatformError` holds no httpx error, no request and no text it could not read as context. The fakes' request logs kept every body of a batch as well, which no client could free: `Request.body` is kept up to `BODY_KEPT_BYTES` (64 KiB), and `size` and `sha256` say what any body was. **The fallback:** after `collect_after_bytes` (128 MiB; `None` for none) of bodies sent and received, a JSON request's and a failed exchange's included, a full collection runs at the start of the next exchange, never in the one that spent the budget, whose own request and answer are still alive when it ends (an exchange is counted once its answer is released, so another thread's collection at that moment finds no cycle of it); at most one per budget of bytes moved. It is the net under the fix, for a cycle that arises some other way, and not the fix. **What it does not reach:** a caller that keeps a `PlatformError` keeps the frames it passed through, with the arguments of each (the core's own records keep messages and fields only); an HTTP client the caller injected whose hooks, authentication flow or transport keep a reference of their own to an answer or a request; and a cycle inside httpx or httpcore that no answer the client holds reaches. The fallback collection frees the unreachable cycles among those, never a reference a caller's own hook or transport keeps alive; the guarantee is for the client the layer builds, the fakes, and the answers Onedata 25.0 gives. *Learned:* the first study's memory audit, issue #259 (14 Nov 2026). *Test:* with the collector off, eight files of four MiB uploaded and read back, read in ranges, read whole and read as heads leave no request or answer alive and no byte allocated beyond the fake's own storage, which fails with either half of the fix removed; so do writes whose answer's body was cut, writes httpx failed on inside its send, and a client that follows redirects is not asked to; a 3xx of every status (300, 301, 302, 303, 304, 307, 308), bare or with a body, with or without a `Location`, answered to a read (`get`, `get_dated`, under a policy of three attempts), a mutation (write of content, creation, delete), the gate's own read, an upload, a ranged read, a streamed read and the head of a content read raises a `PlatformError` naming the status with no host or token in it, is sent once, counts no retry, follows nothing to another host and leaves no request or answer alive, and one whose body stalls is refused unread, with its status and no retry, while a 2xx is not refused; the same over a real socket and connection pool; a failed write, a head, a streamed range, a retried read that ran out of attempts and an answer that is no JSON raise an error with no context or cause; the fakes' log keeps size and SHA-256 for a body over the cap and the body whole at it; the fallback collects once the bytes of writes, of JSON requests, of reads, of streamed ranges, of answers cut short or of answers whose close failed reach the budget, before the next exchange and not inside the one that spent it, not below it, never with `None`, counts exactly under threads, and refuses a budget that is no positive whole number.

**O48. A tool outside a campaign holds the federation's shared admission, so no outage can overlap it, and the hold is recorded.** A campaign attempt holds the federation's lock shared and an outage run holds it exclusively (O11), but a study's smoke driver, a warm-up or a `verify` is no attempt and held none, so it could read and write through a provider that an outage was taking down or restoring. `expcore_onedata.tool_admission.shared_admission` (`shared_admission_async` for an event loop) takes the same lock, shared, through the same turnstile: an outage run waits for it and it waits for an outage run and its session drain, as a campaign run is admitted, within a bound (`[faults] admission_timeout_s`, or the caller's `timeout_s`, 0 to try once), past which it is `FederationBusy`, the uncharged `federation_busy`. Under the lock it is refused, naming why, while the fault journal holds an injection open or cannot be read, while the record of the holds cannot be read, and, for the ingest journals it is given, while a preallocated baseline is not whole or is held open by a live run. It records the reason, the process (boot id, pid and start time, as the lease pool names a holder), the start and the end in `shared-holds.jsonl` beside the fault journal, ending the record when the lock goes, a worker's retained share included, and on an exception. A dead tool's lock goes with its process and its record is ended as `holder_gone`, by the next hold or by any exclusive admission, which proves no tool holds the federation. An exclusive wait that runs out says that tools hold the federation and since when, never their reasons (O12). *Learned:* #266: a study's smoke drivers, `verify` and warm-ups could run beside an exclusive outage. *Test:* an exclusive admission is refused while a tool holds the federation and granted when it ends; a tool waits for an outage run in progress, and starts after it; with a bound it is refused as a busy federation, of kind `federation_busy`, and records nothing; it waits for an outage run's session drain and is refused as `federation_busy` within a bound shorter than the drain; two tools hold it together; an open injection, an unreadable fault journal, an unreadable record, an unhealthy ingest baseline and one a live run holds open each refuse it, naming why, and leave no hold; the record names the reason, the process and both times; an exception in the body and a worker's retained share end the hold as they should; a torn last line is ignored; a tool killed inside its hold never blocks an outage run, and its record is ended as `holder_gone` by the next tool and by an exclusive admission; a nested hold reuses the outer one, refused by an open injection and held back by a drain recorded since the outer hold began, and one inside an exclusive admission is refused; a platform without `[faults]` admits the tool with no admission; a reason or a bound that is not valid is refused before anything is taken: the reason is a short code holding no configured name (`shortcode.checked_code`, O12: a sentence, a path, capitals or past 64 characters, and a provider's, host's, space's or zone's name in any spelling, each refused naming the argument and never repeating the value; a valid code recorded as given; the same without `[faults]`); the async form holds and refuses alike.

**O49. A mount run's record carries the declared and the effective I/O mode, and a run that did not run in the declared mode, or whose log cannot say, is refused and never scored as the declared mode.** The declared mode (`[sandbox.mount] io_mode`, O36) was only in the sandbox's metadata beside the campaign, and oneclient's own start lines never reached the record, so a study could not show from a run's record that the run ran in the mode it declares. `expcore_onedata.sandbox.iomode.MountIoMode(sandbox)`, the platform's fourth instrument (namespace `mount_io_mode`), puts `{"version": 1, "mount": true, "declared", "effective", "agree", "forced_direct", "forced_proxy", "lines", "problems"}` in the record: the sandbox's collection reads oneclient's INFO log once (`DockerSandbox.mount_io_mode`, kept in the metadata as `mount.effective`, before a direct mount's own check that may refuse the collection, so a refused collection keeps it) and the instrument records it. `effective` is `direct` or `proxy` when the start lines `Forced direct IO` and `Forced proxy IO` force it, `1` and `0` or `0` and `1`, and no line shows a read that did not follow it: after forced direct, oneclient's own detection, a read refused for permissions and made again through the provider, a switch to the provider's helper, a storage not direct on the provider or one it could not reach itself (the lines of `directio.MARKERS`); after forced proxy, a detection that did not force proxy (`forced proxy io mode: 0`). It is `auto` when neither is forced, `mixed` when the log contradicts itself (both forced, or a start line logged again with another value), `unknown` when it cannot be read or a start line is missing or, anywhere in the log, is not `0` or `1`: every logged occurrence of a start line counts, never the first alone, so a sidecar started again with other options, or a line the log was given, cannot hide behind an earlier one. `agree` is true only when the declared and the effective mode are the same word, `proxy` or `direct`. **The policy:** a mount run whose snapshot is not agreement is refused by the instrument's `check` with an `InfraError` of the kind `io_mode_integrity`, registered `treatable=False` in `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, so no condition can declare it: `finish_instruments` writes `instrument_refusal` naming both modes, the attempt is set aside as charged infrastructure and run again, and it is never scored as the declared mode. An unparsable, unreadable or not yet collected log is `unknown` and refused (fail closed); a `finish` that never ran or failed is refused too. A run with no mount records `{"version": 1, "mount": false}` and is never refused. `MountIoMode(sandbox, refuse=False)` records without refusing, and `agrees(snapshot)` recomputes the answer from the two modes. Nothing of the log is kept, only the two values, the names of the lines found and the module's own sentences. The record is additive: the instrument's namespace is new, no schema field changes, and the fixtures of version 3 replay as they did. *Learned:* the first real study's analysis, issue #247 (the PI's determinism rule, #227: the mode is declared, never autodetected). Read from oneclient 25.0 (`bb8a63e3`, `helpersCache.h:467`, `fsLogic.cc:1184`), with the start lines as the 11 Nov live facts show them (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-11-federation-facts-mount.md`: forced proxy I/O 1, the start-up settings alone in the log). **Not run live:** that a forced proxy mount logs no detection line with `forced proxy io mode: 0` (read from the source, and the 11 Nov log holds none), and that a proxy mount's start lines are in the file when the collection reads it (oneclient buffers INFO lines up to 30 s without `--disable-log-buffering`, which every mount passes, O54): a run whose start lines were not written reads `unknown` and is refused. *Test:* the pure reading of invented logs (forced proxy and forced direct agree; the other forced mode, neither forced and both forced do not; each marker line after forced direct is `mixed`, a detection that forces proxy after forced proxy is not, one that does not is; an empty, garbage, absent or half-parsable log, an unreadable one and a start value that is not 0 or 1 are `unknown` and never agree, and the reading repeats no character of such a value; a start line logged again with another value, in either order, is `mixed`, one logged again with a value that is no flag is `unknown`, and the same lines again are the same mode; a declared mode that is no mode never agrees; `agrees` recomputes and reads any snapshot but a matching one as no; nothing of the log, a path or a host reaches the snapshot); over the sandbox on the in-memory host, a proxy run and a direct run with matching lines record agreement, pass the check and keep the reading in the metadata; a run whose log shows the other mode is refused with `instrument_refusal` naming both modes, the attempt set aside and the snapshot kept; an empty, garbage or unreadable log and a `finish` before the collection are refused as unknown; a failed `finish` is refused by its check; a REST run and a run with no data path record `mount: false` and are not refused; `refuse=False` records `agree` false and does not refuse; the kind is registered untreatable; a direct run whose collection is refused for a fallback keeps its `mixed` reading; the version-3 fixtures hold no such field and replay byte for byte, and the record of a refused run writes, loads and replays to its own bytes.

**O50. A check of the probe space leaves out exactly the files the link check writes, and tells a pair's current probe from its leftovers.** The link check leaves files in its probe space for good: a probe per pair, a higher generation after each failed fetch, and a retirement marker per retired file id, which only a later passing check deletes. A study's Day-0 or reset check of that space saw those leftovers and could not tell a current probe from a superseded one, nor the check's files from a stray file of a near-miss name that a prefix test would have hidden. `linkentries.link_check_entries(listing)` sorts a listing of the probe space into `own` (every entry of the two name shapes, matched whole in lower-case hex: `expcore-link-probe-<16 hex>` with `-g<N>` after it for any generation from 1, never `-g0` or a leading zero, and `expcore-link-retired-<16 hex>-<16 hex>`), `current` (per pair, by the stem its names share, the highest generation among its probes that no marker of the stem retires by the digest of the file id, the choice `ProbeFiles.provision` makes), `superseded` (every other probe: the lower generations, the retired files, and a name with ten or more digits of generation, which is the check's own but which `provision` does not read and `purge` does not delete), `markers` (those whose file is gone too) and `other` (everything else, near-miss names included, for the study to judge). `is_link_check_entry(name)` says whether a name is of either shape. It takes the `Entry` objects the core's listings return (a list of them, or the dict `Provisioner.children` returns) or `(name, file_id)` pairs, needs no provider id, and refuses an item of another shape, an entry with no file id and a name listed with two file ids. *Test:* generation 0 alone; generation 3 above 0; a retired file with its marker, so the next generation is current, and a retired highest generation, so the highest one not retired is; a pair whose every probe is retired has no current one; a marker whose file is gone; a marker of another pair, or for another file id under a name written again, retires nothing; near-miss names (15 or 17 hex digits, upper case, `-g0`, `-g01`, a non-ASCII digit, a trailing newline or space, a suffix) are `other`; a generation past the nine digits `generation_of` reads is the check's own and never current, and one of thousands of digits raises nothing; an empty listing; `own` and `other` partition the listing in its order; the three input shapes agree.

**O51. A reset waits for what can still change, once: a space with nothing to cancel settles its transfers on two observations, several spaces share each wait and work at once, and the eviction waits for no job the reset saw end.** A live reset of four spaces took about 16 minutes against 197 s planned (the first real study's, under a host load of 11 to 19): the transfers stage took 90 s per space with nothing to cancel, four clean observations 30 s apart being a 90 s floor; the occupancy and quiet waits came once per space (100 s and 173 s); and the eviction took 44 to 62 s for one to four files in one attempt of two polls, recording no wait. **The transfer settle:** a space whose every observation shows nothing waiting or ongoing, and that cancelled nothing, settles on its second observation, one `transfer_settle_s` after the first, as the transfer log settles on two alike (`transferlog.py`): the listing shows a job at most `transfer_settle_s` after it is scheduled (the setting's meaning, O07), so a job scheduled before the first observation and still waiting or ongoing at the second is listed then; a job scheduled in the space after the first is the caller's to rule out, as for every stage (a run acting on the space during its reset breaks every verdict), neither two observations nor four cover one scheduled after the last, and the later stages' looks at the listings still catch one scheduled after the settle; anything seen or cancelled keeps the full `transfer_settle_polls` in a row, within twice that many (`fast` in the record). **Several spaces** (`resetspaces.SpacesReset`; `SpaceReset.run` is it with one space): every space's stages in order, stage by stage; the transfer settle's waits, the occupancy wait, the quiet wait and the listings' late look each taken once for every space, to the latest instant any space needs, on the calling thread; every other stage's work for at most `[placement] reset_concurrency` (4) spaces at once, each under its own `[placement] concurrency`; a failure in one space fails the reset once the stage ends, every space's record kept (`halted_before` where it stopped). Every shared wait is a lower bound, so a longer wait can make a reset fail where it would have passed, never pass where it would have failed. **The eviction's waits:** a job of the reset's own whose record has reported it ended on the provider it ran on (an eviction it sent, a job it cancelled) no longer holds the read of the block maps up, whatever a listing still shows (a listing lags the record); any other listed job still does; and the stage records every wait it takes by why (`waits`: `transfers_ending`, `block_maps`, `listed_running`, `retry_pause`). **One listing for a planner** (`SpaceReset.plan_scan`, O33): the recursive listing once, every provider's at once, each regular file's block map read at every provider that can hold the space's bytes, never skipped on the counter's word, and its scan taken by the reset as the qos stage's first. **One full reset on request:** `run(..., full_reason=...)` (`ResetPlan.full_reason`) gives the full reads inside the exclusive section, its grant held throughout, the record saying `full`, the core's own why (`requested` where the shortcut would have been given) and the caller's reason beside it, a short code holding no name the configuration gives, since the record is published (O12); any other reason is refused before anything is sent, and a reset given none records its reads as before. *Learned:* the first real study's live reset of four spaces, 14 Nov 2026. *Test:* a space with nothing to cancel settles on two observations one settle apart; a job the first observation misses is cancelled at the second and the full polls follow; a job seen at the first keeps them; three spaces take one settle wait between them, and a busy one takes its full polls while the others settle on two; one occupancy wait, and one quiet wait and look, cover three spaces, each space's occupancy read no earlier than its own eviction's end plus the wait; `reset_concurrency` 2 runs two spaces' qos stages at once and never three; a missing path in one space fails its eviction stage, the others end theirs and read no occupancy, and the failure holds every record; two resets of one space are refused; the setting defaults to 4 and refuses less than one; the eviction stage records its waits by reason, waits for no own eviction still listed ongoing, and still waits for a job it did not send; the planner lists each provider once, reads every file at the provider that can hold, skips only one declared never to hold, and a reset given its reading lists the space no second time, removes what it found with a second scan of its own, and refuses a reading of other providers. **The shortcut** (`resetshortcut.py`; the exclusivity it rests on is O11's reset lock): the occupancy and quiet stages read every file at every provider that can hold, twice, about 106 s a pass for the first study's large space. Declared (`[placement] reset_shortcut = true`, refused without `[faults]`) and given a planner's reading made inside `SpaceReset.exclusive()` (or `SpacesReset.exclusive()`), whose hold still stands, they re-read at each provider the reading read only the files it showed held there or has no figure for, every file of a job the ended listings show finished since the reading's start (walked newest first to it, less the clock-skew allowance and the margin, the global clock placed by Onezone's readings) or that the transfers stage cancelled, the reset's own evictions excepted, the files the reset evicted, every file a QoS requirement of the planner's scan applies to (a QoS replication leaves no transfer record) and every hard link (it shows its target's blocks), and carry the rest as empty. The walk comes at least `transfer_settle_s` after the transfers stage's last observation, by when every job from before the section is on a listing (O07). Any doubt about the premise gives the full reads, never a failure, and always under the reset lock's exclusive hold (O66): the setting off, no reading, a reading outside a live section, a section that gave none (`holder_gone`, `holders_unread`, `shares_retained`), its hold lapsed before the occupancy stage, a job since the reading on no listed file (a directory, the root), or a listing, record or clock that cannot be read; a hold that lapses after narrowed reads fails the stage. The record's `occupancy_reads` gives the mode, why, the grant (wait, holders), the reading's start and size, and what was re-read. *Test (`tests/onedata/placement/test_reset_shortcut.py`):* inside the section, with one file held and a job replicating another after the reading, the two stages read those two files and none of the other four (`files_read` 2, `files_carried` 4); off by default, every file is read twice; a reading used after its section ended and a job since the reading on a directory each give the full reads; a tool holding the federation past the bound refuses the reset, naming the holder and no reason, nothing read (O66); a requirement's file is read again though no transfer touched it; a reset of several spaces takes one section; every hard link, and every file the reading has no figure for, is read again; and the setting is refused without `[faults]`, as a value that is no flag and as a negative wait, and defaults to off and 60 s. **One bound per provider, and connections reused:** four spaces at once, each reading 16 block maps at once, put 64 reads in flight at a provider they share, beyond httpx's default of 20 kept-alive connections, so the pool closed the extra ones as they went idle and connected again, each new connection a TCP and TLS handshake and a socket left in TIME_WAIT on this host. `[placement] provider_concurrency` (16 by default, a single space's own level) bounds the reads of a reset's readings (block maps; the QoS scans' root, pages, summaries and records) in flight at one provider, every space and planner reading of the process together (`readslots.py`), a read holding its slot through its retries and pauses, and each reading records the bound and the seconds its reads waited for a slot (`read_retry`); the client's own pool keeps alive every connection it may open (100), so the reads reuse their connections. The planner's reading retries a connection failure as the reset's readings do (O46) and records its tally (`Holdings.facts()["read_retry"]`). *Test (`tests/onedata/placement/test_reset_transport_retry.py`, `test_rangemap.py`, `tests/onedata/test_read_retry.py`):* two readings at once, eight workers between them, never have more than the bound (1 or 2) in flight at beta, and do overlap with the default (a latch that opens only once three reads are in flight together, no timing); two QoS scans at once keep a bound of one in their root and listing reads; the setting defaults to 16 and refuses less than one or a fraction; the client's pool keeps 100 connections alive.

**O52. A run whose network could not be restricted is checked for egress, and a missing or damaged egress log is never read as clean.** On an HPC batch node an unprivileged user gets no network namespace, so the agent's network cannot be blocked; the study's PI ruled that egress there is acceptable if detected, with flagged runs reported and left out of a sensitivity analysis. The agent's cell loads a shim (`deploy/hpc-federation/bundle/egresslog.c`) that sends one record per connect, its result, a non-blocking connect's outcome and each send to a new address, with no payload, to a collector outside the cell (`egress-collector.py`) over a Unix datagram socket by a compiled-in path; a connect whose record cannot be delivered is refused. The core reads the log (`expcore.egress`): it is intact only when present, parseable, numbered without a gap, of this run's nonce, closed by its end line, free of malformed, unwritten or dropped records, and opened by the launcher's canary connect, which proves the shim was loaded. Anything else is unknown with its reasons and no counts. Each destination is the data gate, loopback or outside by the run's recorded declaration, and the agent's code cells are scanned for URLs off the platform, downloads and network calls. Once one analysed record carries the instrument, every analysed run is judged, one without a readable snapshot as unknown, and the preregistered analysis is run again without the flagged runs; the report, `analysis.json` and the terminal show both. *Learned:* the standing HPC run of #179 (14 Nov 2026): from the agent's cell the internet was reachable (a TCP connect to a public host opened in 21 ms), and its driver printed the gate log's token check as a literal, so a summary that is not derived from evidence passed what was never checked. An inherited log descriptor was rejected: Python's subprocess closes it in every child, the agent can truncate it, and a reused descriptor number would write into the agent's own file. *Test:* the shim, built with gcc and run against a test program, logs a connect to a loopback listener and a non-blocking connect to TEST-NET-1 (192.0.2.1:9, never routed) with their results, logs a UDP send once per new address, writes no payload, and refuses a connect with no collector listening; the collector numbers its lines from the nonce to its end line, keeps a malformed datagram as its size and hash, and refuses an existing log or an open socket directory; the core reads the real collector's log as intact and classifies it; a missing, empty, cut, unclosed, reordered, foreign, forged, dropped or canary-less log is unknown with no counts; a run with no snapshot, a failed instrument or a self-contradicting snapshot is flagged as unknown; an outside connection or a scan hit flags; the sensitivity analysis drops exactly the flagged runs' pairs, deterministically; the report lists the flagged runs and the rerun, and a batch without the instrument renders byte for byte as before; and through the pipeline, a study run with the instrument, checked in a fresh process and analysed, carries the verdicts in its analysis, `analysis.json` and report. The HPC run's own summary (`deploy/hpc-federation/run/evidence.py`) is derived from the steps' saved evidence files by one function: an item whose file is missing, empty or unparsable fails, and so do a token that the step's own `grep -c -F -f` finds in the gate's or the agent's logs, a scanned log changed or removed after its scan, a write the gate let through, an egress log that is not intact as the core reads it, a node check whose `ps` or `ss` failed, and an evidence directory of another run (the first step needs an empty one and writes the run's identity, a step's directory is made once, and a repeated cell's earlier attempt is moved aside). A collector that stops reading makes a connect fail with EACCES after the shim's bounded wait instead of hanging it, a snapshot that contradicts its own canary, record count or completeness is not read, and an IPv4-mapped IPv6 record is read as the inet6 record it is.

**O53. A federation's Couchbase on HPC never runs without this launch's administrator.** The rootless bundle of `deploy/hpc-federation` lets onepanel create Couchbase's administrator, as the image intends, with a per-launch random password. The image's `couchbase-cli` 4.5.1 accepts a password of at most 24 characters (`MAX_LEN_PASSWORD`, `node.py:26`); over it, `cluster-init` prints an error and exits 0 without creating the administrator (`node.py:434-437`), and onepanel, which reads only the exit status, goes on to `bucket-create`. Couchbase 4.5 lets any REST caller act as its administrator until one exists, so on a node without network namespaces it served the node's loopback with none. The bundle generates 24 random alphanumerics (`gen_alnum`), and `cb_cred_ok` holds every generated, rotated and overlaid pair to `couchbase-cli`'s limits before onepanel sees it. A guard (`hpcop-cb-init.py --watch`) fails closed right after onepanel's Couchbase setup. `GET /settings/web` must answer 200 to this launch's administrator and name it, and 401 to an anonymous request. On a bucket while there is still no administrator once it has asked again, an administrator that is not this launch's, an anonymous request that is answered, a Couchbase that stops or stays without one past the guard's deadline, or an error of the guard itself, it writes a `FAILED` verdict and sends SIGTERM to the start script, whose trap stops every component, and the batch script fails the start. From the host, `cb_guard` repeats both requests before and after the password is rotated. *Learned:* the third HPC live run of #179 (13 Nov 2026): a 48-character password, Couchbase with no administrator for about 3 s, its whole life before the start failed. Confirmed fixed live in runs 4 to 7. *Test:* against a loopback stand-in with Couchbase 4.5's access rules, the guard passes when this launch's administrator answers and an anonymous request gets 401, and fails, with its cause, on a bucket without an administrator (the incident replayed), an anonymous administrator, someone else's administrator, a Couchbase that stops before it has one, no administrator by the deadline and an unreadable launch secret; with Couchbase's answers scripted, an administrator and a bucket created between the guard's first two questions pass, since it asks again before it fails, and a Couchbase still unprovisioned after the bucket listing fails; a failed verdict signals the start script that started the guard; a generated password is 24 alphanumerics, `CB_PASSWORD_LEN` is 24, `node-init.sh` checks the pair before it writes a secret, and `cb_cred_ok` refuses 25 characters, the incident's 48 hex digits, 23 characters, a character outside `[A-Za-z0-9]` and a user outside Couchbase's rules.

**O54. Every mount's oneclient writes each log line as it is logged, so the collection finds a proxy mount's start lines as it finds a direct mount's.** `MountIoMode` (O49) reads oneclient's start lines from its INFO log when the sandbox collects, and refuses as `unknown` a run whose lines are not there. oneclient's glog holds INFO lines in a buffer for up to 30 s unless `--disable-log-buffering` is passed, and only a direct mount passed it (O40, for its start check): a proxy run collected within 30 s of its start could find none of its start lines, and was set aside as charged `io_mode_integrity` infrastructure and run again, in whichever mode it ran. **The fix:** `containers.sidecar_options` passes the switch for every mode, once, after `--config /dev/null` and before `--insecure`. A study's `extra_args` may not carry it in either mode, at load and at the command (`oneclient_args.extra_arg_problem`, which holds it now that `mode_arg_problem`, the direct-only form of the check, is gone), since oneclient refuses a switch given twice. **What it does not change:** it sets when INFO lines are written and nothing else. The sidecar's docker arguments (capabilities, device, AppArmor scope, network, binds) hold nothing new, and a proxy mount's capabilities, devices, security options and network are a direct mount's; none of the start lines the core compares (`oneclient_start.expected_settings`) names it; the campaign fingerprint holds the I/O mode word and no argument (`data_path_record`); the run record's `mount_io_mode` snapshot holds modes, start values, names of lines and problems, never an argument; the record's schema and the fixtures of version 3 are unchanged. The one recorded difference is the sandbox metadata's `mount.options` of a proxy run, which now ends with `--disable-log-buffering` as a direct run's always did (additive: an item of a list the metadata keeps, no field). *Learned:* O49's own note that a proxy run's start lines might not be in the file at collection, which only a direct mount's argv prevented, and the coordinator's request of 15 Nov to join another study's final core pin. **Not run live:** that the start lines of a proxy run are in the file at collection with the switch (read from oneclient's logger, `logging.cc`, `FLAGS_logbuflevel` -1, as O40 reads it; neither mode has shown it live), which the first live proxy run with the instrument shows. *Test:* the proxy mount's command and recorded options carry `--disable-log-buffering` exactly once, after the configuration and before the study's arguments, and so do the direct mount's; the switch is not among docker's own options, and the sidecar's capabilities, devices, security options and network are the direct mount's; the proxy run's metadata records it; the switch and its abbreviations are refused as extra arguments in either mode, at load and at the command, and `--disable-read-events` is not.

**O55. A cell's exec is known to have run its program whole and ended, or it raises naming why: never exit 0 with no output.** A live Day-0 read cell, a program that prints a JSON line on every branch, came back from `DockerSandbox.exec` as exit 0 with nothing on stderr (stdout was not kept), where the same cell in the same kind of outage at an earlier core answered `EAGAIN`. Nothing in the core made that result up: the exec path passed through `docker exec`'s exit code and bytes unchanged, and trusted an exit 0. Two things below it give exactly that. `python -` given no program runs an empty one and exits 0 with nothing printed, so a program lost on its way to the container's stdin reads as a cell that ran and printed nothing. And the docker client reports exit 0 for an exec whose exit the daemon never recorded: at v29.2.1, the host's version, `interactiveExec` returns `getExecExitStatus` once the attach stream ends (docker/cli `cli/command/container/exec.go`, lines 196-201), which fails only on a non-zero `ExitCode` and never reads `Running` (lines 204-218), and the client it vendors takes the daemon's `ExitCode`, a null until the exit is recorded (moby `docker-v29.2.1`, `daemon/inspect.go`, lines 222-223), as 0 (docker/cli `vendor/github.com/moby/moby/client/container_exec.go`, lines 191-194). The daemon records an exit before it closes the exec's streams (`daemon/monitor.go`, lines 215-222), so only a stream that ends some other way is inspected with no exit; it waits at most 2 s for the streams to drain before it closes them (lines 218-222), so a slow copy may also lose the end of an output whose exit was recorded. What was kept of the live run cannot tell the two apart. Both reach every cell, the agent's included: a Day-0 step (`record=False`) and an agent's cell run the same `docker exec` through `CellRunner.run`. **The fix:** a cell runs under a bootstrap (`completion.BOOTSTRAP`), started by `timeout` in place of the interpreter and run isolated (`-I`). It reads the program whole and checks its size and SHA-256 against the ones its arguments carry, and runs nothing when they differ. A whole program runs as before, `<python> -` with the program on its stdin and the exec's streams its own. After that interpreter exits, the bootstrap writes one completion record on each stream, a line that starts with this exec's nonce, with whether the source arrived whole and the exit status, and ends as the program did. The runner takes those lines out of the streams before it counts and caps them (`hostops.run_bounded(..., marker=)`, `marks.py`), so the program's output is its own byte for byte, and it says when the program was not written whole to `docker exec` (`CmdResult.input_error`). `Completion.check` reads the result. An exec that no timeout of the harness and no kill explains, and that is not verified, raises `completion.ExecIncomplete`: an `InfraError` of kind `sandbox` that is also a `SandboxError`, so the code tool ends the attempt as retried infrastructure and the Day-0 check records a gap. Its cause names the source not written whole, the source not arrived whole ("its program did not run"), no record ("the exec's exit was not recorded"), a record on one stream only ("its output was lost"), or another status docker reported. A program that exits 0 and prints nothing, `os._exit(0)` included, is verified and stays a success. The cell's record, a harness step's record and the result carry `exec_check` (`source_whole`, `exit_status`, `nonce_matched`), an incomplete one also `incomplete` with its cause (and `metadata()["exec_incomplete"]`), and the call's record in the run keeps the code tool's `exec_check` as an optional field of version 3 (fixture `07-exec-check.json`). *Learned:* another study's live Day-0 run at core `39bba713` (15 Nov), and the docker client's and daemon's source at v29.2.1. **Not run live:** which mechanism the live run met (neither its step record, docker's events nor the daemon's journal kept it), and the bootstrap under a real `docker exec`; it runs here in the host's Python through the bounded runner, as the container's interpreter would run it. *Test:* the runner takes a marker line out of either stream, split anywhere between reads, after more than the cap, and counts and caps only the command's bytes; another exec's marker, a marker with no line end and one too long are output; an input the command stopped reading is said with the bytes written; the bootstrap, run for real, keeps a program's output and traceback its own, records exit 0 for a silent program and for `os._exit(0)`, the program's status, 137 for a death by SIGKILL, and runs nothing of a program that arrived empty, in part or other; it ignores a module in the working directory; the check names each cause; through the sandbox on the in-memory host, for a cell and a harness step alike, an exec whose program the container's stdin never got, got in part or `docker exec` was not given whole raises, one whose attach stream ended before the record raises the exit not recorded, partial output with no record, a stream cut before its record and another status docker reported raise, a silent exit 0 stays a verified success with its output byte for byte, a cell the harness's timer ended, a host call given up on and a container that stopped under the cell stay what they were; the code tool raises the incomplete exec as `sandbox` infrastructure and hands on a verified cell's check; a Day-0 mount cell whose exit was not recorded is a gap, never a read; the record keeps a call's check as its last field, absent for a tool that gives none, refuses it in version 2 and in any other shape, and replays, passes the replay check and is parsed by the analysis with and without it.

**O56. On an HPC batch node the agent's cell holds no token, every exec is checked whole, the harness keeps its bounds, its egress is logged, and its teardown leaves nothing.** An HPC batch node gives an unprivileged user no network, user or mount namespace of its own (`user.max_user_namespaces` is 0) and no cgroup per container: setuid Apptainer is the only isolation, and the Docker sandbox's internal network, freezer, memory limit and loop disks do not exist there. `expcore_onedata.sandbox.apptainer.ApptainerSandbox` (`[sandbox] backend = "apptainer"`, `[sandbox.apptainer]`) runs the agent's cells there and keeps each guarantee the node allows, recording where it departs in every run's `infrastructure_facts` (`apptainer/facts.py`). **The token:** the core's gate runs as a process of the driver's on 127.0.0.1 at a free high port, reads the token from an owner-only file outside every bind, and the file is removed, verified, once the gate says it is ready; the cell resolves the zone's and the provider's names to the loopback, trusts the run's CA and holds the placeholder (O23). **Every exec checked whole:** each cell is one `apptainer exec --containall --cleanenv --no-home --no-mount hostfs,bind-paths,cwd` of `timeout -s KILL` and the completion bootstrap (O55); an exec Apptainer itself could not start (its exit 255 and `FATAL:` line) raises `ExecIncomplete` saying its program did not run. **The bounds:** the harness's timer kills the cell's processes at the cap through their pidfds, a survivor ending the run as on Docker (O34); a watch finds the run's processes by their PID namespace (a process carrying the run's tag or the cell's nonce names it) and kills them all over `memory_bytes` (`memory_limit`) or when `/work`, `/tmp` or `/dev/shm`, directories on the node's tmpfs, pass their size or inode count (`disk_limit`, a new cause of `killcause`, the agent's own); an OOM kill the step's shared cgroup counted is `host_oom`, infrastructure; whatever of a cell is left when its exec returns is killed and shown gone. **What `/proc` hides:** under `hidepid=invisible` a cell process that makes itself non-dumpable has no `/proc` entry, so the watch's sum neither counts nor kills it (the HPC probes of 16 Nov 2026: one held 768 MiB under a 256 MiB limit while the watch saw a 12.7 MB peak). At every poll the watch reads the anonymous memory of the driver's own cgroup (`memory.stat` `anon`, which charges the hidden process's) before and after its listing, and subtracts the RssAnon of every process of the uid `/proc` shows in that cgroup (the driver, the gate, the collector, the cells' visible processes); memory nothing visible explains that grew, from the last reading with no cell's namespace in the cgroup, past `hidden_memory_margin_bytes` (128 MiB by default, once more for each other run's cell in the cgroup) for two polls in a row, each confirmed by a second listing after the cgroup's second reading, the visible memory being the larger of the two listings' totals, is taken as past the limit, and kills the cell as `memory_limit` (`memory_hidden`). With another run's cell in the cgroup it cannot be told the cell's, the run's memory reading is `unknown`, and the run is ended (O72); with a margin the start's own cells (the canary and the read-only probe) passed, the reading is `unknown`, never a kill. A pid that a visible cell process's `children` files list and `/proc` does not show while a process holds it is a `hidden_process`: recorded, never a kill, and supplementary (a double fork under the cell's hidden init escapes it). **The memory reading** (`infrastructure_facts.memory`, with its reasons and figures) is `over` after a memory kill that reached a process, `unknown` with each reason that held (a hidden process, memory not told the cell's, a cgroup, `/proc` or `children` reading that failed, a kill that reached no process), and `within` only when the check held throughout; `ApptainerSandbox.process_visibility()` is the source of `expcore.visibility.ProcessVisibility` (namespace `process_visibility`), and the report's `## Process visibility (detected, not prevented)` flags a run with a hidden process or hidden memory, reads one with an unknown memory reading as unknown (its limit condition unknown, never within its limit), and reruns the analysis without them. Not compared: a hidden process's shared memory (a memfd, a shared anonymous mapping), which the cgroup counts with the run's tmpfs files, and hidden memory under the margin; the step's limit bounds them. **No name server:** every cell binds a `resolv.conf` naming none and an `nsswitch.conf` whose hosts are files alone, read-only, so the C library resolves the names of `/etc/hosts` and no other, and every run records `dns: none`; a completion cause names `apptainer exec`. **Egress:** the bundle's collector starts first, the shim is preloaded in every cell through a bound `/etc/ld.so.preload`, and a canary connect from inside a cell must open the log and be seen by the watch, or the start fails; the collection stops the collector and keeps its log for `expcore.egress.EgressLog`. **Teardown:** a run's record (`work_root/_runs/<sid>.json`) is written before anything exists; the teardown removes the token file first, then the cells' processes, the gate and the collector (each shown gone), then the run's directory, and drops the record last; a later start reaps a dead driver's run whole, and the lease's probe holds a key while any record, run directory or tagged process of it is left. Every pinned file is copied into the run's directory and its copy hashed; the SIF is hashed once per identity and its identity checked before every exec (O13). A mount run is refused at its start unless the sandbox is given the mount's helper (hook (c), O60) and the platform declares `[sandbox.mount]`, and a run's `infrastructure_facts` lists `mount_refused` only when its sandbox refuses a mount, never for a mount run; the shipped helper refuses `io_mode = "direct"`, so HPC mounts are proxy-only until the direct-I/O comparison passes. **The tool server:** the run's tool server is a process of the driver's on the node, as on Docker one on the host, outside every cell and in the node's PID namespace; the sandbox names where it runs (`tool_server_process`), its `HOME` and working directory a 0700 directory of the run's, its `TMPDIR` in it, removed once it stops and by a teardown that keeps the run as evidence before it keeps it, and it carries the run's tag, so a dead driver's reaper kills it and the lease's probe holds the key while it lives; the launcher names its whole environment (`env -u` of what the MCP client passes on from the driver), so nothing of the driver's reaches it but `PATH` and no value is an argument; a Docker run's launch is unchanged. *Learned:* the HPC probes and the standing allocation of 12 and 14 Nov 2026 (no namespaces, `hidepid=invisible`, tmpfs charged to the job, the core's gate on a batch node's loopback serving a cell's read), the ICSA replication package's HPC sandbox notes, and the bundle's egress shim. **Not run live:** everything here runs on a fake node; Apptainer's flags on the site's install, file binds onto paths absent from the image, the cells' processes under `hidepid`, the end of a cell's PID namespace with its exec, and the watch's kill are a live slot's; the cgroup check's default margin and its reading on the site's nodes are untested live (the probes measured the step's `memory.current`, not its `anon`). *Test:* the tool server the real launcher starts on a fake node has exactly the named environment (none of the driver's `USER`, `LOGNAME`, `SHELL` or `TERM`), its `HOME` and working directory the run's 0700 directory, gone once it stops, its `TMPDIR` in it, the run's tag, and no token in any word of its command, and a home set in `extra_env` is refused; a run kept as evidence while the server runs keeps no file of its home; a server left running holds the key and is reaped by its tag; a Docker run's launcher gets the keywords it always got; no exec's argv or environment, no file under any bind and no file left in the run's directory holds the token, and the gate's token file is gone before the first cell; a cell runs as `timeout -s KILL <limit>` and the bootstrap with its program's size and hash, and an exec whose program arrived in part, whose record never came or that Apptainer could not start raises; the harness's timer makes a `time_limit`, a process that outlives its kill a `kill_survived` that ends the run, the watch a `memory_limit` or a `disk_limit`, the step cgroup's OOM a `host_oom`, and an exit 137 alone no kill; a hidden process's memory (the cgroup's `anon` well above the visible RssAnon) kills the cell as `memory_hidden`, whether a visible parent names it or not, and reads `unknown` when another run's cell is in the cgroup, when the start's cells passed the margin, or when the kill reached no process, while a normal run (the cgroup equal to its visible processes and helpers, or the node's own share under the margin, a growth that lasts one poll, or a worker read just as it freed memory it takes again) is untouched and reads `within`, and two visible processes that hold memory in turn never hide a hidden one; a kill that signals no process reads `unknown`, and its cell still counts as hidden memory; another run's cell in the cgroup between cells never becomes the baseline, and a second namespace of the run's own never widens the margin; the margin is a bounded setting; a child `/proc` does not show is a `hidden_process` that makes the reading unknown, and children, a cgroup or a `/proc` listing that cannot be read do too; the instrument's snapshot of no reading or a malformed one is unknown, a run with a hidden process or hidden memory is flagged, one with an unknown reading unknown, and the report's section and rerun appear only for a batch that carries the instrument; every cell binds the name-service files read-only and the run records `dns: none`; a completion cause names `apptainer exec`; a worker a cell left is killed at its end; the output is capped; the egress log opens with the canary and reads intact after the collection, and a collector that died is a death whose log reads unknown; the teardown leaves no process, directory or record, and one that cannot show the gate gone keeps them; a dead driver's run is reaped whole and a live one's never; the probe holds a key while anything of it is left or `/proc` cannot be read; the shard driver (`deploy/hpc-federation/shards.py`) gives each federation's shard one `--exact` step on its sandbox node with the allowlist alone, runs nothing under a core other than the one given, archives a shard's `out/` whole once its dispatcher exits and resumes from it, and its collection refuses a shard without its lock, with another shard's archive or with one its manifest does not name.

A control step's srun client that outlives its bound is killed only once its step has been gone from `squeue -s` for three polls in a row (its status then read from the accounting), and a step still listed at its bound is cancelled by its own step id, raising `TimeoutError`. **The state file** (`hpc-federation.json` in the root, no secret) is written atomically, under a lock, before each action it enables: the allocation's name (with a random part) before `sbatch`, the job id as soon as it is printed, each step's name before its `srun`, each work directory once its log names it, accepted only as the bundle makes one (`<node tmpfs>/hpcop-<oz|op>.XXXXXX`). **Down** reads it, stops the login node's fault relay first (M4's, when the core has it; `up` starts it last, in an environment of PATH, HOME and LC_ALL alone), and, on whatever `up` reached, tears every service down with the bundle's `teardown.sh` on its node (the providers, then the zone; a work directory missing from the state is read from the service's log, and a step whose directory is still unknown is cancelled by its id), waits for every step to leave, checks each node for the recorded directory, cancels the held job by the recorded id, or by its exact allocation name when the id never reached the state (never `scancel -u`), ends the srun clients and removes the root; what it cannot verify keeps the root and its state for a second `down`, the job cancelled all the same. **What down survives:** a recorded id that now names another job (Slurm reuses ids) is not touched; whatever fails before the cancel (a relay that will not stop, a failed `squeue`, a work directory the driver refuses) is reported and the cancel still runs; every srun client of the job on the login node `down` runs on (the one that ran `up`), recorded or not, is ended and verified gone; and `up` and `down` hold the root's lifecycle lock, so no `down` runs between an `up`'s state and its `sbatch`. The read-back runs again once the job runs, a step a signal ended never reads as a success, both ends of a token's pipe are always finished, nothing writes bytecode, and storages are found by name. **What live slot 5 showed (16 Nov 2026).** A step cancel was an outright kill on the site (on a het job `scancel <leader>.<n>` printed "Invalid job id specified" and exited 225 while it did end the step), so a service whose step `down` cancelled at the stop's bound kept its work directory (2 to 2.6 GB of the node's tmpfs) and `down` could not finish: once such a step has left the list, `down` removes the work directory the state recorded for it, only one `checked_workdir` accepts, by `teardown.sh` on its node, and the node's check shows it gone; a cancel's own exit status no longer ends the wait (the step's leaving the list decides), and nothing the state did not record is removed (K1). Node steps wrote bytecode into the core's interpreter under the account's shared home (B1): every Python the driver and the shard driver launch, on a node or the login node, runs with `-I -B` (`-I` ignores `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE`), the fake Slurm fails any command that runs one without both, and the README says to install the venv's interpreter under the site directory. *Learned:* the core's standing HPC run of 14 Nov and live runs 1 to 7 (`srun` cannot start from a compute node; a provider's delivery killed under 100 MB; peers read once per process), and another study's traps on the same site (`--overlap`, the per-job TMPDIR link, the login environment leaking into steps, a finished step's client hanging 25 minutes, a submit read back before trusting it). **The first live slot (16 Nov 2026)** proved the read-back of both components, the leader's listing as `<leader>+<k>`, a `--het-group=1` step landing on the agent's node, the leader's cancel ending component 1 and the allowlist under the site's task prolog, and found eight faults, each now held here: the image's CA file holds its private key before its certificate, so the root keeps its `CERTIFICATE` blocks alone (`hpcca.py`) and refuses an export with none; a step of a heterogeneous job that names no `--het-group` is refused, so every step names its component (0 on a service node) and the fake refuses one that names none; a step of component 1 is listed under that component's own id alone, so the launcher's lookup by name and `down`'s wait ask it too; **O58. An outage and a heal on an HPC federation keep the Compose federation's journal, restore, settle and fail-closed rules, and an unconfirmed stop or start is never read as done.** The HPC federation runs each provider as a setuid Apptainer service, a Slurm step of one held job, and a campaign that runs on a node cannot launch a step. Its driver (`expcore_onedata.fault_hpc.HpcDriver`) has the Compose driver's surface, so the injector, the journal, the restore and its settle, the recovery, the heal and the fault gate are unchanged, and asks for four verbs (`status`, `stop` keeping the work directory and registration, `start`, and `exec` of the heal's two commands only), which a relay on the login node runs for its own federation alone (`expcore_onedata.fault_relay`). Guaranteed: the same journal lines and steps as on Compose for the same outage; a stop journalled but never sent is `never_down` once the whole settle shows the service as it was, and a stop asked for and never seen down is `stop_unsettled`, open; a stop with no answer is waited out; a restore is verified only once the identity changed, the service's own checks passed (`ready`) and its provider serves; `restart_link` is exec'd into each provider's service with no read through a link from the first restart to the settle's end; the container fallback is a service restart, journalled, and one that fails keeps the gate closed until the gate's recovery restores and verifies it; a relayed request with no answer is unknown (withdrawn and not sent, or a command with no answer), never done, **and a verb that ended in that way never runs afterwards unrecorded (unless the relay itself dies mid-verb, before it writes `watch_opened`): each verb runs in a process group of its own and the whole group is ended at the relay's bound, so no `srun` client of it survives to make its step when the Slurm controller catches up (it can stall for about 200 s), and the relay, which stamps each verb's control steps with a tag of its own (`--step-tag`, so `<allocation>-<service>-<word>-<tag>` is that request's alone), starts a watch after a timed-out verb that cancels a step of those exact names in the federation's own job by its exact `<job>.<step>` id, never by user, name or pattern, (the same watch opens for a verb that exits early with a child left in its group, which is ended too, its answer its exit status, and for one that ran 25 s or more, since hpcfed ends its own srun only after its own bound and the controller may still hold that step) and journals it (`late-steps.jsonl`: `watch_opened` before the watch starts, `late_step`, `accounted_late` for a step that came and went between two looks, and `watch_closed`, `none_seen` only when the looks and the accounting were read); the answer stays `timed_out`, and a start's detached service step is the start itself, never cancelled by name (a restart it makes late during a later run's outage is read by the federation window as an unexplained restart, which stops the batch: it fails closed, and the start's `watch_opened` line attributes it); and the answer says where each verb's time went, from one accounting call: the time from its issue to each step's accounted Start and from its answer to each End, null with the reason where it cannot be given, never failing a verb, kept by the driver and journalled with the stop, the start's restore and the heal's link restart, so a controller stall is the site's in the record**; the relay refuses another federation's service, any command outside the allowed set and any request made for an earlier relay; a dead relay refuses an outage run at its admission, uncharged; `settle_timeout_s` at or below the stop's bound is refused at load, and the least token margin counts the relay and the start's stop; every stop's duration and end are journalled, confirmed or not. Over the HPC driver's own launcher and state file: a control step whose end is not known is never a success; a stop is confirmed only once its step is gone and no start's pending step can still be made, and its record never undoes a later start; a start never launches beside a live process or a launch in flight, and one after a stop whose verdict was never recorded brings the provider back; two stops or starts never interleave; the relay never runs one request id twice, no malformed request stops it, and a verb that failed once it may have run is unknown, never `not_run`; the file system adds at most one poll to a relayed verb's time, never its attribute timeout (the relay runs one request at a time, so a verb still waits for the requests issued before it): the client finds its answer by a fresh listing of `answers/` on every poll (`os.scandir`, an opendir that revalidates the directory) and looks the answer's path up only once the listing shows its exact name, so a name the node looked up while the file was missing (a negative entry, kept for `acdirmin`, 30 s, on a compute node's NFS client) never hides an answer the relay has written, a listing that fails is no answer yet, and a file whose name is not exactly the request's id (a draft, another request's answer) or whose content answers another request is never read as this request's; the relay's stop signals only a process that still has its heartbeat's pid and start tick and whose command line holds `-m expcore_onedata.fault_relay serve --root <its root>` in order (never another process, nor another root's relay), through a pidfd where the kernel has one, and on a login node whose kernel has no `pidfd_open` (ENOSYS) by that start tick read just before an `os.kill`, never a pid another process took, a process group or init, and never by working round any other failure of the pidfd; `down` checks the relay again once it has ended the recorded clients, and reports it unverified only if its second stop still shows a heartbeat; a restarted owner of shared imported trees is never ready before they are proven read-only for its launch (O59), by the shared import's own check on its node; a start's identity check never compares, or records, a null (a restart that finds the provider's domain null is waiting for op_worker's Onezone connection, which answers it: both starts wait, inside the start's readiness budget of 1800 s, for a domain that is neither null nor empty, then compare as ever, so a different non-null domain still fails as a changed identity and a domain that stays null fails as that; a field that is not there fails at once; `jsonget` prints nothing and exits 3 for a JSON null, so no script takes the word for an id, a domain or a token), and a restore records how long its start waited as `domain_wait_s`, optional and never a 0 nobody measured;
