Its boot refuses a root that is not one directory under `/tmp` or `/dev/shm`, whose parent is not a tmpfs, that is not the user's, or that goes through a link, before it makes anything, so a step never writes to a shared filesystem or to Slurm's per-job TMPDIR (a link into an inode-quota'd filesystem on some sites). The payload (the commit's history as a git bundle, `node.sh`, its settings, the pytest arguments) is checked by SHA-256 before it is unpacked. **What is tested:** a clone at the commit (only committed work ships), its environment by `uv sync --frozen`, and a **self-check** first: an isolated interpreter (`python -I`) of that venv must import the core from the clone's `src/`, or no test runs (exit 97). **Nothing of the account's home**, which every agent of the account shares (with HOME unset behind `env -i`, uv fell back to the passwd entry's home, found live): HOME, TMPDIR and the XDG directories inside the run directory, uv's cache, Python, Python links and tools under the node root (`--no-bin`), pip's config and git's global config at `/dev/null`, git's system config and Python's user site off, Apptainer's cache and config inside the run directory, for every phase. pytest's environment is built on the node (no `SSH_AUTH_SOCK`, no `SLURM_*`, no runner setting). **What comes back:** the results directory as one archive between a header with its SHA-256 and size and an end marker with pytest's exit status, accepted only when both match (exit 71 otherwise), and the run's exit status is pytest's own; a launch srun reports failed is retried with the same run id only behind the boot's durable, atomic mark of that id on the node, so a retry whose earlier attempt ran refuses (72) instead of repeating it or hiding its result, and no clean removes a mark younger than a day; an srun client that does not return after its step's end marker (seen live by another study) is ended 30 s later and the results kept. **pytest's arguments** are an allowlist (pytest also reads clustered short options and `@file` argument files): test paths relative to the clone and options that can neither write outside the run, move its results, nor run no test; a zero counts as a pass only when the JUnit XML records a test (exit 5). **Ours:** the login node's `alloc.sh` lists, picks, renews and releases only jobs its ledger holds and squeue shows under our name, so another study's or lane's job under the same account is never touched; it cancels in two places, each one id: the job `sbatch` has just printed when the read-back of what Slurm recorded differs from what was asked (an unreadable record counts as different), and a ledger job alive under our name on `release`; nothing cancels by user. The account and partition reach `sbatch` through its input variables, never a command line. **The idle release:** except while a `want` lasts, the loop releases a RUNNING allocation of ours that has run no step for 3 h (sacct's step records, so no short step is missed; an unreadable record, or one without the job's batch step, counts as busy), only while it holds exclusively the login directory's use lock, which every run holds shared around its step, and after one read of the job's live steps, by its id through `release`'s checks, clears the marker and releases our queued successor. **Renewal follows use:** by default the loop renews an allocation while sacct shows a test step of ours within 3 h, and lets an idle one end; `renew want --until` forces renewal (and holds off the idle release) and `renew never` forbids it, `never` winning; unreadable accounting decides nothing that round; an override change and a loop decision never overlap (the override lock, held from reading the overrides to the end of the submit or release). It reads the clock and squeue at every poll, never a timer: it queues the successor with `--begin` at the end less 15 min once the running allocation ends within 3 h, submits one at once when none of ours is alive, and stops at `--until`, a stop file, `--max` submits, a submit that was not as asked, or three failed submits. *Learned:* another study's working setup on the same site (one held job, steps from the login node, `--exact`, the venv on the node's tmpfs, the TMPDIR link, the srun client hang, a session-only timer that never fired and left the node gone 2 h). *Test:* the step's argv, the allowlist whatever the login shell holds, the remote line's quoting and the site values kept off it, a root off a tmpfs refused before anything is made, the results accepted only with their SHA-256 and size, the job picked among ours only; a whole run through a fake ssh, srun and uv on a real tmpfs (pytest's status, summary, `-ra` lines and JUnit back, no login variable seen by the tests, no run directory left), the self-check's refusal, a hanging srun client, the clean of a dead run's read-only tree; the submit's argv and read-back cancel by its own id, the guard, the listing and release of ledger jobs under our name only, every cancel of one id, and each renewal decision from the clock, a busy allocation renewed with no marker, an idle one not, `never` stopping a busy one's renewal and `want` an idle one's end and its idle release, no decision on unreadable accounting; the idle release of an idle ledger job alone, never one that ran a step within 3 h or runs one, never on an unreadable record.

**O65. A mount run on an HPC node runs the oneclient the Compose sidecar runs, from a pinned bundle on the image's own loader with no RPATH of the image and no cache of the node consulted, and the campaign fingerprint and the sandbox's metadata name it.** A batch node has no oneclient of its own, and one run from a SIF mounts inside the SIF's mount namespace, not the node's, where the host mount (O60) needs it; `host_mount_factory` took a binary given in code, pinned by its SHA-256 but absent from the campaign fingerprint, so HPC mount runs on two oneclient builds would have merged as one experiment. `deploy/hpc-federation/oneclient_bundle.py` builds a relocatable bundle from the image the Compose sidecar is pinned to (one never-started scratch container created by id with `--pull never`, its root copied out, that container alone removed; the reference refused unless it names the id): the binary, its whole library closure resolved inside the image (RUNPATH and RPATH with `$ORIGIN`, the image's `ld.so.conf`, every link resolved in the image's tree, a name found nowhere failing the build), the NSS modules glibc opens by name, which no `ldd` lists, and the image's own loader, as a tar whose bytes depend on its content alone. `[sandbox.apptainer]` declares it (`oneclient_bundle`, `oneclient_bundle_sha256`; required by a mount level, so such a platform without it does not load), `record()` holds its SHA-256, so the fingerprint's `sandbox_backend` tells two bundles apart while a platform without one keeps its fingerprint, and `host_mount_factory` refuses a oneclient given in code. Each run unpacks its own copy into a private directory from the bytes it hashes (the file's whole length; a link, a device, a path that climbs or goes deeper, a name twice or another SHA-256 refuses the start with nothing left), runs its `--version` in the environment the mount's oneclient gets, never the driver's (a site's `LD_PRELOAD` would reach the probe alone; a start whose oneclient does not name its version fails before the token is written), and records `oneclient_build` (version, FUSE version, the binary's GNU build id, the pin) in the helper's metadata, which the sandbox's carries (`metadata()["mount"]`); the stop removes the copy. A Compose mount run records the same keys: at the end of the sidecar's start `sandbox.oneclient_build.sidecar_build` runs `oneclient --version` and reads the windows of the binary the GNU build-id note needs (the ELF header, the program-header table, each note segment, each up to a bound) by `docker exec` in the running sidecar, never by `docker cp` (a copy from a running container re-mounts its binds, O05; the window is printed by `tail | head | od`, which seeks, so the binary is never read whole and the host's text output carries it as hex), after the host's copy of the token is gone, and `metadata()["mount"]["oneclient_build"]` holds `version`, `fuse`, `build_id` and `image`, the sidecar image's id (shown pinned at the start, O13), where the Apptainer record has `bundle_sha256`. A reading that fails is None, logged without the tool's words, and never refuses the start (the mount is up already, as with the image's config file, O36); a run without a mount has no `oneclient_build`. oneclient runs as `lib/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2 --inhibit-cache --inhibit-rpath <every file of the bundle> --library-path lib bin/oneclient`: the image's libraries carry the DT_RPATH `/opt/oneclient/lib`, which glibc searches before `--library-path`, and a library opened at run time falls back to the node's `/etc/ld.so.cache`. *Learned:* the HPC mount design's live probes (16 Nov 2026) and the coordinator's conditions (the bundle's SHA-256 a fingerprinted pin, oneclient's version and build id recorded per run). `oneclient --version` prints no build id, so the binary's GNU build-id note stands for it. On a batch node `LD_DEBUG` showed the plain `--library-path` launcher trying `/opt/oneclient/lib` for a library before the bundle: absent there, but a node that had it would have served its own copy; the launcher above tried and initialised nothing outside the bundle at `--version`. A name the bundle lacks is still looked for in the loader's own Debian-layout directories, which a node keeping its libraries under `/usr/lib64` does not have. **Not run live:** the Compose sidecar's reading (`oneclient --version` and the windows of its binary through `docker exec` against the real image, which needs `tail`, `head` and `od` there; `tests/live/test_mount_path.py` asserts it when the live gates are set), a real mount from the bundle, its bind into a cell, a known file's bytes and the effective `io_mode` through it, and the comparison with the Compose sidecar's mount (per file served and placed bytes, `io_mode`; proxy first) that comes before HPC mount runs are scored. *Test:* the build reads an ELF object's loader, needed names, search paths and build id and refuses another machine's; it follows RUNPATH, `$ORIGIN` and absolute links inside the image, an RPATH, the RPATH of the object that loaded one without paths of its own, the image's `ld.so.conf` and its includes, skips a candidate of another machine, adds the NSS modules with their closure, and fails on a name found nowhere and on a loader that is not the x86-64 one; the image is read through one never-started container, created by id with `--pull never`, copied from and removed by its own name, even when the copy fails, a reference naming another image is refused before anything is created and a started container is refused and removed, and the Docker command refuses every verb that would run, pull or remove anything else; two builds of one image whatever its times give one SHA-256, the tar holds two directories and regular files with time 0, owner 0 and fixed modes, its provenance names the image's id and every file's source and SHA-256 and never a host path, a tar already in the output is never overwritten; the self-check flags a library outside the bundle or not found and refuses an object initialised from outside it; on the node, the bundle unpacks as private files, a tar that is not the pinned file, one with bytes appended, one holding a link, a path the bundle does not hold, a file or a directory named twice, and one without the binary are refused with nothing left, as is an unreadable file and a directory that is not empty or holds a ':'; the launcher inhibits the cache and every file's RPATH; the version is read from oneclient's own line and anything else fails the start; the build id is read from the note; the host mount runs oneclient through the launcher, runs the version probe with the mount's environment and none of the driver's, refuses a platform without a bundle and a bundle that is not the pinned file before the token is written, records the oneclient that served the run, fails a start whose oneclient names no version before the token, removes the copy at the stop, and its factory refuses a oneclient given in code; the bundle is a pin of the backend resolved like the others, a mount level without it and a bundle declared by half are refused at load, a platform without it keeps its record, and two bundles give two campaign fingerprints differing by that pin alone; `up` hands the core the site's bundle and its pin, refuses one missing or of another hash naming the variable, and the fingerprint holds its pin and no path; a Compose mount run records the version, the FUSE version, the build id and the image id of its sidecar's oneclient, read by exec in the sidecar with no `docker cp` and no new container, after the host's copy of the token is gone; a reading that fails (an exec that exits non-zero or cannot run, a `--version` that names no version, a window that is not hex or is cut, a file that is missing, not an ELF, or has no note) is None and the start goes on; a run on the REST path or with no data path has no `oneclient_build` and runs nothing for oneclient; the build id is read through any reader as from a file, never from a table or a note segment over its bound or a note cut short; and the script that prints a window of a file runs for real on a file, short at its end and silent for a missing one.

**O66. Every reset, full or shortcut, holds the federation's reset lock exclusively for its whole run, or refuses at its bound: none runs beside another holder.** The exclusivity O11's reset lock gives covered only the shortcut, and only for 60 s. A plain `SpaceReset.run` took no lock; with `[placement] reset_shortcut` off (the default) the section took nothing; a shortcut whose wait ran out (`reset_exclusive_wait_s`) fell back to a full reset and proceeded, "the fallback, never a failure"; a section that found a dead holder let its hold go at once, one entered while a worker of the run held a share never took it, and one with no admission took nothing. So a run's reset could evict a provider's replicas, cancel transfers and read block maps while another run's agent, a tool or a health check acted on the federation, the cross-run interference the design forbids, and the core only recorded it (`why = shortcut_unavailable`). **Now** `SpaceReset.run` and `SpacesReset.run` check every plan, then hold `federation.reset` exclusively (`resetgrant.exclusive`), using the caller's live section of the same federation when there is one, before anything is sent: the run gives up its own share (never its share of `federation.lock`) and waits, polled, within `[faults] admission_timeout_s` and until its admission's `cancel` is set; at the bound, or cancelled, it is refused as `ResetLockBusy`, a `FederationBusy` (`federation_busy`, which costs the run no attempt), naming the holders by pid and status (never a reason, O12), its share taken back and nothing sent. The shortcut's own premise (the setting off, a dead holder's leftovers, a worker's share, a record that cannot be read, a reading outside the section) still gives the full reads, under the hold; another holder present never does. On a platform with `[faults]` a reset in a context with no admission (a script outside a campaign, a thread that did not inherit its run's context) is refused at once, `AdmissionRefused`, so it never waits on its own run's share nor runs beside an outage run's agent, which holds no share of the reset lock; outside a campaign it takes `shared_admission`. One under an admission whose owner has left is refused too, and so is one while the run's sandbox or tool server is up (a step that uses the run's tokens is under way), checked before and once the hold is taken: with the launches refused while the hold stands, nothing of the agent runs during a reset, and a share the run keeps then is a harness step's; a step that begins during the reset fails the stage it began in. The hold keeps a share of the run's admission and lasts as long as its last user: an attempt cancelled while its reset runs in a thread lets neither the admission nor its locks go until the reset ends, and a caller's section that ends while a reset that inherited it still runs leaves the hold to it; a hold that lapses all the same fails the stage it lapsed in, the last included. A worker of the run's own that already holds a share goes on, as it did beside a full reset before (`shares_retained`, no shortcut): waiting for it could keep the run's reset out for good. A reset of several spaces of two federations is refused. `reset_exclusive_wait_s` is accepted and bounds nothing. **What it costs:** shared runs on one federation that each reset no longer overlap their attempts: a run's reset waits for every other shared holder (another run's whole attempt, a tool, a health check) to let go, and a stream of them can hold it to the bound, which refuses, uncharged, and the campaign asks again. *Learned:* a review of `resetgrant.py`, 16 Nov 2026 (the reported 60 s fallback, and the three further paths it found where a reset took no hold). *Test (`tests/onedata/placement/test_reset_hold.py`, real locks):* another campaign run in a process of its own holds its shared admission through `run_admission`, and a full reset (the shortcut off, no section) sends nothing until that process begins letting go, then passes; another run of the process holds its share past `reset_exclusive_wait_s`, and the reset waits for it and then proceeds; a tool's hold, and another process's, past the bound refuse the reset as uncharged `federation_busy`, naming the pid and no reason, nothing sent, the share back; the run's cancel stops the wait long before the bound; a run alone resets at once under its own shared admission (a full reset, a shortcut reset with no section, inside its own section, through `SpacesReset.run`, twice in a row); a full reset, one whose shortcut a dead holder refused and one with a worker's share hold the lock from the first stage to the last; a hold that lapses during a stage fails that stage (the last one included, which used to end the reset `ok`) and begins no later one; an attempt whose owner lets its admission go while its reset runs in a thread keeps both locks until the reset ends, which passes; a caller's section that ends while its nested reset runs in a thread leaves the hold to it; a reset with no admission on a platform with `[faults]` is refused with nothing sent, its message naming the remedy (`shared_admission(config, reason=<code>)`) with an example code that hold accepts, under which the same reset then passes, and one under a tool's `shared_admission` holds the lock; the campaign's own path (`run_admission` entered, the executor under `asyncio.run`, the reset through `asyncio.to_thread`) holds the lock from its first stage to its last, and a reset through `run_in_executor`, which does not carry the run's context, is refused with nothing sent; every way the run's agent comes to act (a sandbox or tool-server launch, a sandbox start's share, a tool reusing the admission) is refused at the reset's first stage and its last and let through once it has completed; a reset while the run's sandbox or tool server is up (`token_step`) is refused with nothing sent, and passes once the step has ended; a step begun during the reset fails its stage; a reset of spaces of two federations is refused.

**O67. An ingest lists each directory once, as its space's sole writer, and ends with the space's listing equal to its manifest.** `Provisioner.upload` takes `listings`, one operation's cache of directory listings: each directory is listed once and every entry made through it is added (a new directory as empty), so n uploads into one directory cost one listing, not n that grow with it. An entry the cache holds is read again by its id before a skip, a resume or a restart is decided, and so is every cached directory on the way before it is descended into, with attributes that answer a removed entry as gone (`name` and `type`; `size` alone answers from a tombstone on 25.0), and one gone, renamed or moved drops the directory's listing, which is read afresh. `expcore_onedata.bulkingest.ingest_files` is the one operation that passes a cache, on two conditions: the running context holds the provisioner's own federation alone for an ingest (`ingest_hold`: `FederationLock` exclusively, purpose `ingest`, the space's label as target; refused on a platform without `[faults]`, under a shared admission, under an exclusive one of another purpose and for another federation, before anything is sent), and it ends with a fresh listing of its root, every level, compared with its manifest: every file there as a regular file of its size, every directory on the way a directory, nothing else, any difference failing the ingest and naming each path. Each source (a path or a tar member's `Slice`) is checked against the manifest's size and SHA-256 before it is sent. *Learned:* the HPC ingest of a dataset with 15,340 files in one directory (16 Nov): without a cache its uploads read about 125,000 listing pages, quadratic in the directory's files. *Test:* the same actions, digests and bytes with the cache as without; a flat directory listed once with the cache and twice per file without; a cached file changed, cut short, removed, replaced by another writer's or renamed behind the cache compared as it is now (restarted, resumed, made again, refused, made again); a cached directory renamed or moved behind the cache never descended into (the path and its file made again, the moved file untouched); a name another writer made after the listing refused loudly with the other file untouched; every refusal of the hold with nothing sent, and the hold keeping every other admission out and naming the ingest; a manifest ingested and matched, and skipped whole when run again; a source not the manifest's refused before it is sent; manifests that cannot be a tree refused; an extra file, an extra directory, a removed file, a file cut short and a file the space already held each failing the final listing by name; a tar's members ingested from the archive.

**O68. A fleet's federations are each brought up in a process of its own, refused past the operator's maximum and the quota before anything is made, given shards of one campaign only when their platform fingerprints agree, and taken down only where the fleet's own up made them.** A campaign's #154 shards run in parallel, one per federation and one federation per held job (`deploy/hpc-federation/fleet.py`; DESIGN.md, section 1, "A fleet"). **How many:** `up N` past `HPC_FLEET_MAX` (the operator's maximum, set by the grant and the scratch inode quota; unset refuses) is refused before the fleet's directory or any job exists, and `add K` counts every federation that may still hold a job; the inode guard (`hpcquota`, when the core has it) is asked once for N times one federation's estimate, since N ups would each read the same usage and pass alone. `HPC_FLEET_PARALLEL` bounds the ups and downs at once. **One process each:** a federation's driver changes process-wide state (the umask its secrets are written under), so each `up` and `down` is `hpcfed.py` in a child of its own, with `-I` and from the fleet's 0700 directory; one that fails leaves the others up, recorded and reported with the job it may hold. **Ownership:** the fleet's directory, each slot and each root are exclusive `mkdir`s that never adopt; the record (`fleet.json`) is written before each action and accepts no root but `<fleet>/fed-k/root`; `down` acts on it alone, and before each `hpcfed.py down` checks that the root is the directory the fleet made (device and inode) and its state file the one that federation's `up` wrote (its command names the root, its job name is the one recorded, its agent's job record is named as that `up` names it, every job id is the one recorded), refusing, untouched, anything else; a root an `up` never marked is removed only while empty. **One campaign:** `plan` gives a shard only to a linked federation of the fleet's whose spaces declare their data (`data_manifest`, C06), and refuses outright when the federations' `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` differ; `step` checks it again, builds each line only from the `shards.toml` the record gives, launches it only into a job Slurm still holds, running, under the name its federation's `up` gave it (ids are reused), and exits 1 on any refusal. **Placement:** the agent's node is a job of its own (`sandbox_placement = "separate"`); a `het` layout is refused, since steps on a heterogeneous job's services failed live. **Moving a shard:** a shard freed by `down --member` goes only to a federation brought up after, of the fleet's fingerprint, whose `platform.toml` leaves `[placement] reset_shortcut` off, so every reset there takes the full reads; its step resumes. **The fault relay:** `up` records the relay's state in the federation's state file (`fault_relay`: `serving` once its heartbeat is seen, `none` for a core without the relay, nothing from an older up or one that failed before the heartbeat), and a federation whose state file does not say `serving` is given no shard of a campaign that injects faults (an outage condition in the study, or a sanctioned `[faults]` in its `platform.toml`) and is refused again by `step`, naming it and the reason; the up log's line is never read, and a campaign that injects none is unaffected. **Each member's state:** `plan`, before it assigns anything, and `status` say plainly up and ready, up with no relay, failed and still HOLDING jobs by count with the command that releases them, or down. *Test:* `tests/deploy/test_hpc_fleet.py`, `test_hpc_fleet_plan.py`, `test_hpc_fleet_relay.py`, over a fake cluster per federation with the core's phase the real one: three federations up, the maximum and the guard refusing with nothing made, a failed `up` beside two that stay up, no more ups at once than the setting, `down` of all and of one, a federation brought up by hand in the fleet's directory and another user's job left untouched, a replaced state, a replaced agent's job record and a replaced root refused, a `het` layout refused and the committed `layouts/pilot-shape-separate.toml` taken (it is the pilot's layout but for that placement, read as TOML), the inode guard the core's own (`hpcquota.guard`) read once for N times one federation's estimate, at the line and one inode over, with an override and an unset command refusing the fleet and no scratch root saying no check was made, a step never launched into a job whose id now names another, gone or not running, differing fingerprints refused, each step on its own job and node, an edited manifest refused, a shard moved and resumed, the relay's state read from the state file whichever way the log disagrees, a member with no relay refused for a campaign with an outage condition or a sanctioned `[faults]` and accepted for a plain one, `step` asking again, a relay that never beats left unrecorded, and a failed member that still holds its jobs shown by count in `plan` and `status`.

**O69. On an HPC node the agent's REST cell sees the names its layout declares, on 443, never a node's name or its port.** On the Apptainer backend the cell's names came from the URLs of `[zone]` and the local provider, which on an HPC federation are `https://<node FQDN>:8443`. The node's FQDN, a site detail, was then in the cell's `ONEDATA_ONEZONE_HOST`, its `/etc/hosts`, `/etc/expcore/gate.json`, the gate's certificates, the run's egress declaration and whatever error text the agent's client printed, while a Docker agent on `[sandbox.federation]` sees the layout's names on 443. So the agent-visible text differed between platforms and federations, and a site's node names would land in transcripts and published traces. `[sandbox.federation]` now takes `zone` and `providers.<local>` on the Apptainer backend: the gate serves and certifies those names on the cell's loopback, where the cell dials them on 443, and dials the URLs' hosts and ports behind them, checking their certificates there as `[tls]` says (`apptainer/gaterun.hosts`). This is name resolution and TLS identity, as on Docker, and no answer of the platform's is rewritten. The table holds no network on this backend, is read only with a rest level, and needs two different names. The gate's own 502 for a failed upstream call names the host it dialled by the name it serves on 443 (`gate._as_served`), since a certificate's name mismatch quotes that host, and its log line keeps the text as it was. A gate whose names are the hosts it dials (Docker's, or an Apptainer gate without the table) is as it was. *Learned:* the HPC live slot 5 (16 Nov 2026), whose providers reported their nodes' FQDNs, and another study's audit of what a cell sees on each backend. **Not changed:** the platform's own answers carry each provider's registered domain (the zone's scope answer, `providers.<id>.domain`; Onedata's no-connection errors). The published client dials the domain the scope answer gives, so the table is usable only on a federation whose local provider registered its declared name; until the HPC providers do, a cell given the table cannot reach its provider. **Not run live:** a cell's REST reads and the tool server's answers on an HPC federation, recorded and checked for any node name or `8443`. *Test:* with the table, the cell's environment, `/etc/hosts` and `gate.json` hold the declared names on the loopback, and no exec's argv, environment or bound file holds a URL's host or, in its environment, hosts or gate file, the URL's port; the gate serves the declared names and dials the URLs' hosts and ports, the run's CA issues leaves for the declared names alone, the metadata says `declared` and the egress declaration names them; without the table the cell is given the URLs' hosts as before, a zone on its provider's host included; names in capitals are served in lower case, as the gate matches them; a network, a table without a rest level, one name for both, another provider's name and a name with a scheme are refused, naming a key and never a name; a real upstream whose certificate names another host fails the call, and the gate's 502 names the served name, never the dialled host or its port, while its log line keeps the dialled host; the dialled host, with its port, in brackets and in any case, is named in one pass, and a gate whose name is its host gives the text unchanged.

**O70. On an HPC batch node the harness keeps a cell's cpus, pids and open-file limit and the gate's memory limit as far as an unprivileged user can, and every run's record says which readings were shown.** The Apptainer backend recorded `cpus`, `pids`, `nofile` and `gate_memory_bytes` as not enforced (`cpu_pids_nofile`), so an agent's cell could use every core of the step, fork without bound and open as many files as the node allows, and a gate that grew without bound was bounded by the step's memory alone. **What is kept now:** `nofile`: every cell command is prefixed by `prlimit --nofile=N:N --`, so `apptainer` and the cell under it start with a hard and soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` of `limits.nofile`, which the cell (it has no `CAP_SYS_RESOURCE`) cannot raise, as Docker's `--ulimit`; the start's canary reads `resource.getrlimit` inside a cell, and a hard limit that is not the declared one fails the start, as does a `prlimit` or `taskset` that cannot run (named, never as a failed connect). `cpus`: the prefix also holds `taskset -c <cores>`, the first `ceil(cpus)` cores of the driver's own affinity (`NodeOps.own_affinity`, the step's cores, all of them when `cpus` is more), the canary reads `os.sched_getaffinity(0)` inside a cell and that is the run's effective set, and at every poll the watch reads the cores of every task of every process of the run (`NodeOps.task_affinity`: `sched_setaffinity` is per thread, so the union of each task's `Cpus_allowed_list`) and records a process outside the effective set (`widened`), never killing it: the agent's own code can widen its affinity, and Docker's `--cpus` is a CFS quota, not a set. `pids`: the watch sums the tasks (`Threads:`; Docker's pids.max counts tasks) of the run's processes at every poll and over `pids` kills the running cell's processes as `pids_limit`, the agent's own (`scope: "cell"`, beside `disk_limit` in `killcause`), where Docker refuses the fork with EAGAIN. `gate_memory_bytes`: the watch reads the gate helper's `RssAnon` and `RssShmem` (a process of the driver's own PID namespace, known by pid and start time) and over the limit SIGKILLs it through its pidfd, in a record of its own (`gate_kills`, never a cell's, so no cell's cause is taken from it); the collection's `deaths()` then records the gate's death with cause `component_defect` and a basis naming the watch, which `infradeath` gives a gate past its own memory limit on Docker, and only a death the watch's own signal caused (by pid and start) is so. **The record:** `infrastructure_facts.limits` (also in `metadata.json`) has `version`, `cpus`, `pids`, `nofile` and `gate_memory`, every key always, a value that could not be read null and its block's `reading` `unknown` with its reasons; a reading is `pinned`, `within` or `set` only when it was shown: a poll that could not list `/proc` during a cell, a process whose task count or cores could not be read, a canary line that did not come back, a step whose cores could not be read, a gate nothing could read, and a run whose memory reading holds `hidden_process` or whose cell was killed for hidden memory (`/proc` hides such a process's tasks and cores) each leave it `unknown`, while a kill that reached a process (`over`) or a process seen outside the effective set (`widened`) outweighs them, and a kill that signalled none is `unknown` with `kill_reached_none`. The facts' `cpu_pids_nofile` is split into what remains a departure from Docker, each with its reason: `cpu_affinity`, `pids_watch` and `gate_memory_watch`; `nofile` is Docker's own rlimit and no departure. *Learned:* the parity review of the Apptainer backend against the Docker sandbox (17 Nov 2026) and the other study's check on the record's shape. *Not run live:* whether Slurm's task/cgroup already confines the step to its cores (`cpuset.cpus.effective` inside the step and inside a cell), that `prlimit` and `taskset` exist on the node and that setuid Apptainer's starter resets neither the cell's `RLIMIT_NOFILE` nor its affinity (the canary proves both at every start), and that `Threads:` and `Cpus_allowed_list` are readable for a cell's processes under `hidepid`. *Test:* the prefix's words and the pin (the first `ceil(cpus)` cores of the step's set, a fractional `cpus` a whole core, `cpus` above the cores held all of them, none without a step set), the canary's line read back whole or not at all and its program reading the limit and the affinity, the task and cores parsers refusing a cut or foreign file (never 0 and never the empty set), every cell command of a fake node's start parsed back with its `prlimit` and `taskset` and the harness's own one-shot reads with neither, the start failing on another hard limit and on a failing `prlimit` naming it, a run over `pids` killed as `pids_limit` with the next cell running and the count and kill in the record, a run at the limit within, a process whose task count was unreadable and a poll that could not list `/proc` leaving the reading unknown, a process outside the effective set recorded and left alive, a canary set wider than the pin and a starter that leaves every core reading widened, a thread that widened itself seen on the real `/proc`, a hidden child making pids and cpus unknown, a gate over its limit killed through its pidfd with its death a `component_defect` of kind `sandbox`, a gate killed from outside staying undetermined, a gate stopped by the collection no unreadable reading, a run with no gate `not_applicable`, and every key of the record present before the run started.

**O71. A write past the Apptainer cell's /work size can be refused with ENOSPC, as on Docker, behind a setting the start proves.** On Docker, `/work` is a loop-mounted ext4 disk of `work_disk_bytes`, so a write past it fails with ENOSPC, the agent's code sees the error, and nothing is killed. On Apptainer, `/work` was a directory on the node's tmpfs, and the watch killed the cell past the size (`disk_limit`, O56). The same agent therefore got an error it could handle on one backend and lost its cell on the other. An unprivileged user on a batch node has no loop mount and no root. Setuid Apptainer can mount an ext3 image for a cell, and its documentation says such an image is bound read/write. So `[sandbox.apptainer] work_disk = "image"` makes `/work` a directory of an ext3 image of `work_disk_bytes`, rounded down to 2 MiB. The image is made sparse on the node's tmpfs by the node's `mkfs.ext3 -d` from a tree holding one directory of the driver's, and bound as `-B <image>:/work:image-src=/work`. The image's root and its `lost+found`, which are root's and 0700, stay outside the cell's `/work`. **The proof:** nothing about the image bind under the site's setuid Apptainer is assumed. Before the agent's first cell, a cell writes zeros into `/work`, at most one chunk past the size. The start goes on only when a write failed with ENOSPC after more than half the size and at most the size. No bound, a bound that came only at `fsync`, another error, or no answer fails the start, so nothing unproven runs. The file the probe leaves must also come out through the collection's own path, byte for byte, or the start fails: a run whose outputs could not be collected is never paid for. The probe fills the image, and ext3 leaves freed blocks allocated in the file. So the image is made again by the same command, and a last cell shows the new one to be a file system of its own, not `/tmp`'s, of more than half the size and at most the size. Until then the record holds `work_disk_unverified`, never `work_disk_image`. **Once per node and driver:** the full fill costs a whole image of time and memory, so it runs at the first start of a driver (a shard step) on a boot of the node with a configuration, and its proof is kept in `<work_root>/_proofs/<key>.json`, 0600 in a 0700 directory of the driver's that no cell binds. A later start with the same key checks the invariants instead (the image's superblock read on the host, the size and mount type of `/work` seen from a cell, and the copy out of the image), and its record names the proof it relied on (`work_disk.proof`, `kind: invariants`, its `id`). The proof is taken only when it is a 0600 regular file of the driver's, with one link, that parses and whose checksum holds: a missing, unreadable or tampered proof, another size or driver or boot, or invariants that differ make the full fill run again, and nothing is ever assumed. **The collection:** the host cannot read an ext3 image without privilege. So `copyout.copy_tree` runs with its copy helper in one-shot execs of the image, which bind the disk at `/work` and a staging directory, bind no preload, and run after the image's identity check (O13). They carry a tag of their own, never the run's, so the watch never kills the harness's copy when the agent's last cell left `/tmp` past its size. The listing is the image's own, so every refusal is the same as Docker's (O19), and nothing is copied before the checks. Each exec mounts the image anew, so the helper is given the listing's device and compares each object with the device the root has in its own exec; the inode, kind and size are unchanged. **What outlives it:** the teardown and a reaper kill every process of the uid whose command line names the image (a cell's `apptainer` given up on, a FUSE server) or that carries the run's copy tag, listing again until a fresh listing shows none, since one may start another while it is killed. They wait for every process once found to be shown gone, whatever a later listing can read of its command line, and fail when one is not. A run kept as evidence drops the run's image, and never an agent's file of that name in a directory run, when the run had a token, since a file's blocks need not be contiguous and a raw image cannot be shown free of the token (O05). **The default stays `"directory"`**, today's behaviour with the watch alone, until a live slot proves the image on a node. The directory mode's record, fingerprint and facts are unchanged. The image mode adds `work_disk` to the backend's record and swaps `disk_watch` for `disk_watch_tmp` (`/tmp` and `/dev/shm`, still the watch's) and `work_disk_image`. *Learned:* the parity review of the Apptainer backend (a disk past its size killed the cell where Docker's write fails). *Test:* every cell binds the image's directory at `/work`; the image is the rounded size, made by `mkfs.ext3 -d`; too small a work disk and a failing mkfs refuse the start and leave nothing; the probe and the check run as the harness's cells and their results are recorded; each wrong answer of the probe or the check refuses the start, and so does a copy out of the image that fails; the probe stops one chunk past the size on a directory with no bound; the first start of a driver on a node fills and keeps its proof owner-only where no cell binds it, and a later start checks the invariants and names the first start's proof; an edited, unparsable, open or linked proof, a missing one, another size, driver or boot, and a mount type that differs make the full fill run again; an unknown boot id or mount type keeps no proof; a failed copy refuses the start on either path; an image whose superblock is not an ext file system refuses it; a cell's write past the size gets ENOSPC with no kill, and the next cell runs; an output comes out of the image through execs of it that carry their own tag, and a `/tmp` left past its size kills none of them; a symbolic link, a FIFO, a hard link out of the output and a sparse file past the disk are refused as on Docker, with nothing copied; the copy compares against the device of its own exec; a holder of the image and a copy left running are killed at the teardown and a holder by a reaper; a holder started while the sweep kills is found by a fresh listing, one whose command line cannot be read again is still waited for, and a zombie is never waited for; one that cannot be killed fails the teardown and keeps the record; a kept run drops the image, and a kept directory run keeps an agent's file of that name; the facts close `/work` only after the proof, and the proof sets the work disk's keys in the run's facts in place, so the node's readings (O87), the federation's window (O86) and the platform's `provider_storage` (O57) recorded before it stay; the directory mode is unchanged; the setting's default, its validation (`work_disk_mkfs` is one program word and never an option, since a file system's type and options shape the image) and its fingerprint rule.

**O72. A cell's processes end with it on both backends, so the code tool's "State does not carry between calls" holds, and a memory kill ends the cell, not the run, unless the cell cannot be isolated.** The code tool's default description tells the model that state does not carry between calls. The Apptainer backend keeps it, since each cell is an `apptainer exec` of a PID namespace of its own and whatever of it is left is killed when the exec returns (O56); Docker's container kept a worker a cell detached into a session of its own running into later cells until the next cap or the collection, so the same agent saw a server survive on one backend and die on the other, and a study's priors measured under Docker were not the HPC runs'. **Docker:** once a cell's `docker exec` has returned, `CellRunner._sweep` kills every process of the agent's container but its init and the init's `sleep` (the quiesce's set, from the cgroup, by pid and start time), in rounds until none is new, and waits for them to be shown gone; `left_after_exec` on the cell's record counts them, and a sweep that killed something or could not list is in `metadata()["quiesced"]` with its cell. A leftover not shown gone (other than a zombie, or a process in D that the kill reached, which returns to no user code), a kill command that failed with a process left, or a listing that fails, ends the run as `survived_kill`, judged on every process left, never only the 16 its record keeps (`left_running`, `left_alive`), as at the cap (O34); after the timer fired the deadline has already killed every process, and a run already ended is the stop's, so neither is swept again. A harness that needs a process of the agent's to outlive a cell starts it with its own `docker exec`, beside the cells. **The memory kill:** another study's sampled runs had 32 memory kills in 20 runs each followed by a later cell, 17 of them exiting 0, so agents recover from one: on Docker a container's OOM kill ends the cell and the run goes on, and on Apptainer the watch kills the running cell's processes (the run's processes are the cell's, since nothing outlives a cell) and the run goes on too, each kill and the cell's `kill` saying `scope: "cell"`. **A cell that cannot be isolated ends the run:** hidden memory (O56) past the margin while another run's cell shares the cgroup cannot be told this cell's, so the watch kills the cell and ends the run (`scope: "run"`, `attributed: false`, its `why`), infrastructure of kind `cell_not_isolated` (registered by `sandbox.kinds`, untreatable, charged as `sandbox` is, so the export and the report count these runs apart), every later exec and the collection refused, and the reading stays `unknown` (`unattributed`), never `over`, the run not counted as hiding, since the memory may be the other run's; every run whose watch sees the growth ends so, and says so as a run-level fact (`infrastructure_facts.run_ended`: `scope`, `attributed: false`, `reason`, `what`, `why`; absent from a run the watch did not end). **A signal reaches only the process that was listed:** the sweep, the collection's quiesce, the cap's kill and the teardown's readers listed the container's processes and then ran `sudo kill` by bare pid, so a pid that ended and was given to another process in between (the gate or the dispatcher, on the agent's node) was signalled instead. A listing now carries each process's start tick and the digest of its command line, and the root half (`killhelper.py`, `sudo PYTHON -I -B -c`) opens a pidfd where the kernel has one and signals through it, else reads the identity just before `os.kill`, as `fault_relay.signal_if_started` does (#339); it signals a pid only while both match, and never pid 1 or below or a process whose identity cannot be read. A process it did not signal is listed again and never marked done, read as gone or counted as signalled: a leftover no signal reached ends the run as `survived_kill`, and the cap's record says how many it did not reach (`unreached`). *Learned:* another study's 412 sampled runs and its tools' texts (4 runs relied on a detached process), and the review of the sweep (the pid-reuse window it named). *Test:* a worker a Docker cell detached, a child and a grandchild in a session of its own among them, is killed when the exec returns and the init's `sleep` spared, with the count on the cell's record and in the quiesce records; a cell that left nothing kills nothing and adds no record; a leftover no kill ends ends the run as `survived_kill` and the next cell is refused as `cell_unkillable`; the tool's description says the promise; an Apptainer cell killed over its memory limit is `scope: cell`, raises nothing in the code tool, and the next cell runs; hidden memory with another run's cell in the cgroup kills the cell and ends the run once, reads `unknown` and counts no hidden memory, and through the sandbox makes the cell's kill infrastructure that the code tool, the next exec and the collection raise as `cell_not_isolated`, with `infrastructure_facts.run_ended` in the run's metadata after the collection was refused, and none for a run whose cell's own memory kill let it go on; the sweep's own wait never lengthens a cell's recorded time; a leftover in D whose kill command failed ends the run, and so do sixteen leftovers in D and a seventeenth running, on both backends; a pid whose start tick or command line changed between the listing and the kill is never signalled by the sweep, the quiesce, the cap's kill or the teardown's readers (the `kill` calls are recorded), and a leftover that execed is signalled as what it is now; a process whose identity cannot be read is not signalled and ends the run, or escalates the deadline, as a survivor; an answer that is not the answer to the request (a pid coerced from a float, an entry out of order, one missing) reaches nobody, so a worker in D never passes as quiet by it; a command that failed after it signalled is judged by a fresh listing; a process declined as it execed and reached after is not counted unreached; the helper signals through a pidfd when it can and else checks the identity just before one `os.kill`, never works a failure other than ENOSYS around, never signals pid 1 or below, and handles a process that is gone; the harness's digest of a command line is the helper's, a carriage return or an undecodable byte included; a cell that ran to its end keeps its own result (exit status, streams, `exec_check`, no kill) when the sweep after it cannot end a leftover, and the run's ending is the sweep's, in the metadata (`run_ended`) and in the error of every later exec and of the record's outcome (`10-sweep-survivor.json` pins both). **A cell that hit its own deadline and outlived its kill is the call that ran, on both backends:** the code tool raised the run's ending (`cell_unkillable`) in place of that cell's result, so the loop recorded a call that ran as `failed`, class `skipped`, with the infrastructure as its detail, which the pilot's audit found. The cell now keeps its own truth, the one a clean kill gives: status `error`, class `timeout`, the output it kept, the exec's check of a killed exec (`source_whole` and `exit_status` unknown, `nonce_matched` false), no detail; its gate refusals and kill are not read into it, since after an abort or a stop nothing read from the run is the cell's evidence. The run's ending stays at run level: the next call is refused, and `launch` (on a body that returns or raises) raises `cell_unkillable` as before (#226), as does the collection, on the Apptainer backend always and on Docker after every outcome but `mount_aborted`, whose outputs are collected as evidence, so an agent whose time is used up at the cell's cap, which is the cell's, ends the loop its own way and the run is still set aside, by `launch`; the detail names the cell's deadline, its outcome and why, and says the cell's own result stands, and the record of an escalated deadline carries its `cell`. No record field is added (`11-deadline-unkillable.json`, schema `expcore-record/3`). *Learned:* the pilot's audit of a cell that ran into its deadline with a process no kill ended. *Test:* through the real loop, the code tool and a Docker sandbox on the fake host, a cell whose cap kill cannot run and whose process stays wedged is a `timeout` call with its kept output and its killed exec's check, never `skipped`; the next call is the one refused, as `skipped` with the infrastructure as its detail and no check; the outcome is infrastructure of kind `cell_unkillable` naming cell 1's deadline and `survived_kill`, never a sweep; the sandbox's metadata keeps the deadline under its cell and the refusal names it; a cell the sandbox's stop ended (`sandbox_stopped`) and one the mount's abort ended (`mount_aborted`, through `launch` and the loop whose watch wakes late) are told alike; a gate refusal logged in that cell changes neither its call nor the run's ending; the Apptainer backend's cell is the same timeout through the code tool and its next call is refused naming the deadline; and the loop's record is the stored fixture, byte for byte (`test_deadline_record.py`, `11-deadline-unkillable.json`).

**O73. On an HPC federation every Onedata service registers and is reached by the name its layout declares, so no node's name, address or public port reaches the agent.** O69 left the providers registered under their nodes' FQDNs, which the zone's scope answer (`providers.<id>.domain`), the no-connection errors (`zoneDomain`, `providerDomain`) and the registered domain a client dials carry through the gate, so a node's name still reached the agent whatever names the gate served, and the `[sandbox.federation]` table could not be used (the cell resolves the declared names only). The names resolve nowhere on a cluster, and an unprivileged user cannot add one to a node's resolver, so the change is made in four places that must land together, in this order. **(1) The address map:** `up` writes, once the nodes' facts are read and before any service is launched, a private map (`secrets/address-map.json`, 0600, the root's own) from each declared name to its node's address and the node's own names, and `platform.toml` names it (`[addresses] file`, `expcore_onedata/addresses.py`). The platform layer's client (`client.MappedTransport`), the bootstrap's (`fedrest.Rest`), the gate (`dial` beside `addr`, `gate._upstream_conn`), the host mount's oneclient (`-H`) and the day-0 reachability check dial the mapped address while the URL, the TLS handshake, the certificate check and `Host` carry the declared name. The map is no part of the fingerprint, a record or a message, and a node's name or address belongs to one declared name only. **(2) The URLs:** every URL the driver builds or records for the zone and the providers is `https://<declared name>:8443` (`hpcdriver.placed_layout`; `hpc_spec` also stops doubling the service's name in its domain). **(3) The registration:** `configure.sh` takes `--declared` and one `--host=NAME=ADDRESS` per service, and the service registers under the declared name (`onezone.domainName`, `oneprovider.domain`, the generated certificate's domain) while the cluster keeps the node's own names. Each service gets a hosts file of ours (the loopback, the node's own names, every service's declared name with its address) bound read-only over `/etc/hosts` with the site's bind paths off, since an unresolved peer crashes a provider's lookup of it; a peer with no declared name, or a name that is no peer, is refused before anything is written, and the driver refuses a service with no address. The service's own readiness calls, the token mint and the registration read-back dial the declared name pinned to the loopback. Onepanel's DNS check resolves through DNS alone and will report a layout's name `unresolvable`: it is advisory, no step of `up` depends on it, and `facts.json` records that. **(4) The tool server and the ordering:** the tool server gets no `ONEDATA_MCP_NODE_NAMES`: once the services register the declared names there is nothing to map, so onedata-mcp runs unset on both platforms and its text is the same on both, which is also why the registration (steps 1 to 3) is the fix, since raw REST answers reach the agent without passing through onedata-mcp (`ctx.serviceDomain`, a URL in a description, `details.providerDomain`). The server still needs to reach the declared names, which resolve nowhere on a node and which a node's name or address cannot replace (the certificate holds the declared name alone): a Python server is started through a launcher of the root's (`deploy/hpc-federation/toolshim/resolve_launch.py`, copied into the root by `up`, named by `[addresses] resolver`; `toolserver.server_argv`, `addresses.launch_argv`): `python -I -B resolve_launch.py <map> -- -m <module> ...` (the interpreter the study declared, every Python of the driver's running isolated) installs a `socket.getaddrinfo` override for exactly the names of the private map, to their addresses, and every other host as before, never consulting the environment, then runs the server with `runpy`: no `PYTHONPATH`, no `sitecustomize`, nothing in the server's environment, nothing shadowed. A `[toolserver]` it cannot start (not a Python interpreter, no `-m <module>` or script) is refused when the platform is loaded. `up` reads back each service's `/configuration` `domain`, fails naming the role and its declared name unless it is the declared one, and records it in `facts.json` (`registered_domain`); a `[sandbox.federation]` table whose names are not those, or declared with no record of them, is refused as `platform.toml` is rendered, naming the table's own names alone. **Fallback, not built:** the same `ONEDATA_MCP_NODE_NAMES` mapping set on both platforms, only if the registration cannot land before the pilot's freeze. *Learned:* the feasibility investigation of 16 Nov 2026 (a registered domain is a free name that every party dialling it must resolve and whose certificate must name it; Compose already registers a network alias), and the live slot 5's providers, which reported their FQDNs. **The count of what still gets through (a follow-up of O73):** the gate scans each non-content answer it relays, and its own answers, in memory, for the exact node names and addresses of the address map's real side (`SandboxSpec.node_names`, from `AddressMap.real_hosts`; the gate's own configuration, `scan_names`, a file of its directory that no cell binds and no collection copies, empty and so absent on Compose), whole, since such an answer is buffered before it goes, so a name split across chunks counts. A name counts when it is whole (not inside a longer name or a longer IPv6 address), in any case, bare, with a port or in brackets. It writes an integer per request, `node_name_hits`, and never a body, a matched name or its context; a file's content is the study's data and is not scanned, and a JSON error at a content route is an answer and is. Once the gate is stopped, `infrastructure_facts.rest_node_name_hits` is `{version, rest_answers, unknown}`; `rest_answers` is `None` with its reasons (the gate's stop, an unreadable or malformed log, an answer in flight, no `teardown_complete`, `gate_not_collected` before the collection) whenever the log is not shown whole, never zero, and the key is absent on a run given no names. The tool server's answers do not pass the gate, so they are not counted. **Not run live, and owed:** a sandbox slot that captures the cell's environment, `/etc/hosts`, `gate.json` and the gate's certificates, the agent's typical REST answers, one forced no-connection error and the three K1 tools' answers, and the transcript, asserts none holds a node's name, address or `8443`, and measures Onepanel's DNS check; also the service cells' own hosts file in a `--writable` sandbox, a registered domain unrelated to `cluster.domainName`, a provider's peer check and a replication between two providers under the declared names, and the tool server's connection through the launcher on a node (its venv, its interpreter, `onedata-mcp` under it). **Limits of the launcher:** it covers Python servers only (onedata-mcp is one), and `-I` applies to the server too; any other server needs a mechanism of its own (a C preload would cover any process, but is a built file pinned in `site.env`). *Test of the count:* a real gate, with a real TLS upstream, counts an answer with a node's FQDN in `ctx.serviceDomain` and in a URL in a description as two, one with only declared names as none, and a name split across the chunks of a chunked answer as one; counts whole names with or without a port, in any case and as an IPv6 address, and no name inside a longer one; scans its own answers; leaves a file's bytes unscanned and scans a JSON error at the same route; scans nothing and records nothing without names; its log holds an integer and no name, address, port or piece of a body; the sandbox gives the gate the names and a cell none (no argument, environment or bound file holds one); the facts hold the sum once the gate is stopped, unknown with each reason before and when the log is not whole, and no key without names. *Test:* the map is 0600, read back, refused when readable by others, through a link or with a host under two names, and holds no name or address in any refusal; a real TLS service on the loopback whose certificate holds the declared name alone is reached by the platform's client, the bootstrap's client and the gate, the handshake asking for the declared name and `Host` carrying it, and refused when the certificate names another; the gate's failure answers and the cell hold no address or port; `configure.sh` fills the registered domain and the certificate's domain from the declared name, writes the hosts file with every peer and refuses each missing or doubled name before writing; the service starts with the bind and its own calls name the declared name; `up` writes the map before the first launch, launches every service with its name and every peer's, records the declared names, and the facts and `platform.toml` hold no node's name or address; a service that registered another name fails `up`; a table of other names, of an older registration's, or with no record is refused without naming a node; a Python server started with exactly the command and environment the platform builds, in a real child process, reaches a real TLS service whose certificate holds the declared name alone, at the mapped address, with the handshake and `Host` the declared name (and cannot without the launcher); its argv carries `-I -B` and its environment holds no `PYTHONPATH`; the launcher resolves exactly the map's names (any case, an IPv6 address) and no other host, runs `-m <module>` and a script with their arguments, and stops the start (exit 78, naming no path, name or address) for a map readable by others, a link, missing, garbled, of another format or empty, or a server it cannot run; a `[toolserver]` it cannot start is refused at load; the HPC tool server's environment has no `ONEDATA_MCP_NODE_NAMES`; and a fake of the pilot's layout with node-shaped names has every command line, environment and file bound into a REST cell searched for each node's names, addresses and `8443` as a token, and the gate's leaf certificates read for their names. **The timing of what the gate relays (a second follow-up of O73):** a provider was seen live to stall about once a minute, a request on a reused keep-alive connection hanging until a client's 60 s timeout, and the gate's log held no time, so no run could measure it. Each request the gate relays now has `duration_ms` (from the gate's reading of the request to the last byte relayed or to the failure) and, of the upstream attempt that waited longest, `upstream_wait_ms`, `upstream_wait_conn` and `upstream_wait_end` (from just before its request went, or from the start of its connect when that fails, to its first answer byte, or to its failure when none came), beside `upstream_conn`. `upstream_conn` is the last attempt's, and the gate retries once on a new connection when a kept one is dropped with no byte, so a stall on a kept connection that ends in a drop would have been filed under `new`: the wait and its connection are kept apart for that. Once the gate is stopped and its log whole, `infrastructure_facts.rest_request_durations` is `{version, requests, by_connection: {new, reused}: {requests, over_10s, over_30s, over_60s}, max_duration_ms, unknown}`, each request counted under the connection it waited longest on, the bands strictly more than 10, 30 and 60 s and nested; it is unknown (every count `None`, with the reasons of `rest_node_name_hits`), never zero, whenever the log is not shown whole, and absent on a run with no gate. A request still being answered at the stop writes no line (the gate's upstream read timeout is four hours, past a client's), so the teardown line of its connection carries `request_age_ms` and the summary is unknown. The fields are whole numbers and fixed words in the log where it already was and as protected (the Apptainer gate's directory is mode 0700 and its file 0600, the Docker gate's log is bound into the gate's own container alone, no cell binds it on either): no body, header value or name, and nothing an agent can read. The gate's timeouts, retry and connection reuse are not changed by this: a later change will, once the stall's mechanism is diagnosed. *Test of the timing:* a real gate and a plain-HTTP upstream on a plan, with a fake clock the upstream advances before it writes (a stall of a minute takes no time and every figure is exact), logs for a request answered after 31 s on a reused connection `duration_ms` 31000, `reused`, the wait and its end, and for a fast one on a new connection 0; a kept connection that hangs 61 s and is dropped, then answered on a new one after 2 s, is a request of 63 s whose wait was 61 s on `reused` (ending in `failure`) and which the summary counts as a reused one over 60 s; a request that fails after two dropped attempts of 7 s logs 14 s until the failure and a 502; a streamed content whose head comes at once and whose body ends 40 s later has a wait of 0 and a duration of 40 s; the gate's own refusal has no timing and is not counted; the teardown line of a request in flight holds its age; the summary counts exact boundaries (a request of exactly 60 s is over 30 s only), by the connection waited on, ignores lines of no relayed request, is zeros with no maximum for a whole log with none, and is unknown for a log unreadable, cut or not shown whole, a gate not stopped, a write that failed, a request in flight, and a timing that is missing, partial, not a whole number (a string, a float, a boolean, negative) or of an unknown connection; the facts carry it unknown before the collection and the sum after, and no key without a gate. **The address map is a slot of the template, not a line the render adds (a third follow-up of O73):** `hpcboot.rendered` put `addresses = { file, resolver }` first whenever the root held an address map, so a render was its template plus a line the template did not have, and a study's conformance check (a render equals its template with only the slots filled) refused it (found by the pilot's first HPC run, 17 Nov 2026). The line is now the template's own: a top-level `addresses = @hpc_addresses@` before the first table, whose slot (`hpcnames.ADDRESSES_SLOT`) the render fills with the table, `{ file = "<map>", resolver = "<launcher>" }` (without `resolver` until the root holds the launcher), the value the driver used to put first; the slot stands for the value, as `@hpc_direct_storages@` does, so a template shows its key. The render adds, moves and removes no line. `up` refuses, naming the slot and before it submits a job, a template whose top-level `addresses` key does not take the slot (`hpcnames.check_addresses_slot` over the unrendered template, which `up` always needs, since it writes the map into every HPC federation's root); the render refuses a template that names the slot when the root holds no map, a map with a template that has no slot, and a rendered file whose top-level `addresses` is not the table, none naming a path. The five shipped HPC templates carry the line. *Test of the slot:* every shipped template has the one line, alike, before its first table; the render of each is the template with its slots replaced, line for line, nothing added or moved; a template with the slot and a map renders the table, with and without the launcher, as TOML the platform layer reads; a slot with no map, a map with no slot, and a slot in a comment, under a table, as another key or beside an `addresses` line of its own are each refused naming the slot; `up` refuses a template without the slot before it submits (no `sbatch`, no state file) and takes the shipped one; the core's phase renders the pilot's template on a root with a map into the template with its slots filled (but for the `[placement]` completion), loads it with the map and the launcher, renders a template without the slot on a root without a map as it always did, and refuses the template that names the slot there. **`verify-data` reaches each owner by its declared name through the root's address map as `up` does (F-B2-1), and no client of the services is built beside the one constructor:** the core's phase and `verify-data` build their REST client in `hpcboot.service_client`, which on a layout of declared names (services made by `hpc_spec`) passes the map the root holds as `addresses` and refuses a root with no map, or one that cannot be used, naming `secrets/address-map.json` and never falling back to a client with none; a layout at loopback ports (the Compose federation's) keeps no map. `verify_ingested` once built its client with no map, so on a federation of declared names every owner read failed to connect (`no answer (ConnectError)`), the ingested space got no verification marker and `shards.py plan` refused it. A scan over `deploy/hpc-federation`, `deploy/compose-federation` and `src` fails on any construction of a client of a service (`Rest`, a factory called `rest`, `OnezoneClient`, `httpx.Client`, `MappedTransport`, an `http.client` connection or a subclass of one) outside the constructor that is not listed with its reason: a client on a node's loopback, in the agent's cell, of a Compose federation, built from the rendered `platform.toml` (whose `[addresses]` `up` writes) or a transport's own (`tests/deploy/test_hpc_fed_verify_data_addresses.py`, `tests/deploy/test_hpc_service_client.py`, `tests/deploy/test_rest_constructors.py`).

**O74. A federation records what it rendered as hashes alone, and a shard step runs only against the render its plan expects.** A campaign's shards are one campaign only when every federation was rendered alike, and nothing said so before the spend: a federation `up` had rendered from an edited template, or with another pin, differed only at the merge, by the campaign fingerprint, after the shard had run; an edit of the template that leaves the rendered platform unchanged (the template's own text) was not seen at all. **The record:** `hpcfed.py up` writes `render-identity.json` in the root (mode 0600, canonical JSON with sorted keys; `hpcidentity.py`, from `hpcboot.write_facts`, so for `core = "login"` and `"step"` alike) with `version`, `template_sha256` (the template as the renderer read it, before it was rendered), `platform_fingerprint_sha256` (the canonical JSON of the rendered file's `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` hashed as the core hashes a fingerprint) and the SHA-256 of each pin the platform holds (`sandbox_sif_sha256`, `egress_shim_sha256`, `egress_collector_sha256`, `oneclient_bundle_sha256`): the values `up` filled in and checked (`hpcpins`), never read again. A pin the template does not name is left out and no entry is empty; a layout with no template writes none. It holds no address, id, job, node, path, user or secret. **The study's artifacts tree:** the pilot's tool server runs from a directory of the study's own (its clone, its venv, its manifests), and two federations whose directories differed by one byte were one render. With the optional site key `HPC_STUDY_ARTIFACTS` the record also holds `study_artifacts_tree_sha256` (`hpcidentity.tree_sha256`): the SHA-256 of a canonical listing, `expcore-artifacts-tree/1` and a NUL and then a record per entry sorted by its relative path's bytes (`F NUL path NUL size NUL sha256 NUL` for a regular file, `D NUL path NUL` for a directory, `L NUL path NUL target NUL` for a link), the venv included, with no mode, owner, time or path of the directory. `up` takes it once, in its preflight on the login node before the root is made or a job submitted, keeps it in the state file and hands it to the core's phase, so a core step on a node (whose environment is an allowlist) has it and the tree is never read again; a layout with no template reads none. A link is recorded by its target string and never followed, so what it points at is pinned only if it is in the tree: *the directory is self-contained* (the coordinator's ruling: its interpreter is installed in it and its venv built on it) and a link whose resolved target leaves it is refused, which left that target unpinned (a change there passed the shard check). A link is resolved as the kernel resolves it, against the tree's own entries alone and never opening anything outside: a component that is a link is replaced by its target (a relative one read from the link's directory, an absolute one only when it begins, by whole components, with the root's real path or the path it was declared by: `/p/artifacts2` is not inside `/p/artifacts`), `..` goes up one and one at the root is an escape, and a path may pass through at most 40 links (Linux's own limit, the link itself the first), so a loop is refused; a link that dangles inside the tree is allowed and recorded like any other (a file that appears where it points moves the digest), and the digest of a tree whose links all stay inside is the one it was before the check; a special file, or a root that does not exist, is a file or is a link, is refused, naming `HPC_STUDY_ARTIFACTS` and a path relative to the directory, never an absolute one; the tree is read below one held descriptor, no link followed, and never written (the pilot makes it read-only first, and its launch check's git calls rewrite `.git/index` in a tree that is not), and a file that changes while it is read is refused. A site with no such directory has no such key (never null), the step compares it like any other key and names it on a difference. **The check:** the manifest's `expect_render_identity` names the expected record (a path of the study, the guard's path rules); `plan` and `lines` read it, refuse one that is not a well-formed record, record its SHA-256, and each line ends in `--expect-render-identity REL --expect-render-identity-sha256 HEX` (with none declared the lines and the plan are what they were). `step` compares first, before the pre-step guard and before anything is made: it reads the expected file below the held study root and shows it is the plan's bytes, reads the federation's record once from its root (no link followed, a root that is this user's and that group and others cannot write to), requires both well formed, and refuses with status 73 on any difference, naming the keys that differ and never a value, hash or path, or when the federation has no record, an unreadable or malformed one, or no root. **The proof in `out/`:** every step writes the record it validated into `out/study-state/render-identity.<step id>.json` at its start, whether or **O75. A run with no mount is not applicable for the mount guard: it stays in the mount guard's rerun and in the rerun of every guard, is counted and shown as not applicable, and is still judged by the other guards; a mount run without its snapshot stays unknown and is left out.** The mount guard (O60) was listed only on the mount runs of a campaign that mixes data paths, since `MountGuard(sandbox, None)` raises for a run with no watcher, so once one analysed record carried it every REST run read `unknown` (`not_recorded`) and was left out of the rerun wholesale: a run that had no mount, and so could not have touched the mount's token, was excluded as one that could not be shown not to. every snapshot of a batch must record the same, else none is used), so no record has to be rewritten. **The factor is the study's, named once:** `[study] data_path_factor = "<name>"` in `study.toml` (`StudyConfig.data_path_factor`; a declared factor, else refused at load, naming the file, `[study]` and the key and listing the declared factors; `data_path` when unset, so a study that sets no key behaves and fingerprints exactly as it did, the key being a setting of study.toml and so only in that file's digest), which `judge(record, condition, data_paths, factor)` and `mount_guard_analysis(..., data_paths, factor)` read by both routes (the record's statement, checked against the campaign's data path, and the campaign's `data_paths` by level) and `SandboxPlatform(..., data_path_factor=)` reads for a run's mode, so a study whose factor is called `path` need not be renamed, which would change every run key and the campaign fingerprint. **A campaign whose platform reads another name is refused at its start:** the plugin hands back the name its platform was built with (`StudyPlugin.data_path_factor`, None: the default), and `check_data_path_factor` refuses, in the study's build before any run and never in analysis, when it is not the study's, naming `study.toml`, `[study]`, the key and both names, whether the platform was built without the name the study declares or with another, or with one the study does not declare. A level the campaign maps to `mount`, a level it does not map, a run without the factor and a campaign that records no data paths leave the run `unknown`; a statement that names another data path than the campaign records for the run's level is `unknown` (`data_path_mismatch`);
