**O76. The rerun of the guards together covers every guard that checked the batch, the process-visibility check included, and leaves a run out only for a guard that applies to it.** The rerun without every run a guard leaves out (O60) combined the egress check and the mount guard alone, so a run the process-visibility check flagged for a hidden process or hidden memory, or whose memory reading was unknown, stayed in it: "the runs every guard shows clean" was not that. `expcore.guards.guards_analysis(rows, absent, prereg, egress, mount, visibility)` now takes the three guards' analyses, each None for a guard that did not check the batch, and returns an analysis when two or more did. A run is left out when any guard that checked the batch says anything but `clean` or `not_applicable`, so a run unknown to one guard and clean to the others is left out, a run not applicable to the mount guard (O75) is left out only for another guard, and a state the code does not know leaves a run out. `GuardedRun` keeps `egress` and `mount` and gains `visibility` (None for a guard that did not check the batch), `left_by` names the guards that leave a run out and `by` the one word for them: a guard's name, `both` when the batch was checked by two guards and both leave the run out, or the names joined by `+`. The counts are `analysed`, `left_out`, one `<guard>_only` for each guard that checked the batch and `both` (two guards) or `several` (three). The report names the section for the guards that checked the batch (`Egress and mount isolation together` as it always was for those two, `Egress, mount isolation and process visibility together` for all three), after every single guard's section; `analysis.json` `guards` gains the guards that checked the batch and each run's `visibility`; the terminal says `both guards:` for two guards and `all guards:` for three. A batch checked by egress and the mount guard alone has the text, the keys and the counts it had, so a study that does not watch for hidden processes sees no change. *Learned:* the coordinator's rule that the combined rerun covers every guard; a study's mixed campaign, whose combined rerun left out every REST run, a run the mount guard did not apply to (O75). *Test:* a run flagged or unknown to the visibility check alone, with egress, with the mount guard and with both is left out, `by` and the counts name each combination, a run not applicable to the mount guard and clean to the others stays in, a batch of egress and the visibility check alone says `both`, a batch of egress and the mount guard alone has its old vocabulary, one guard alone has no combined rerun, verdicts of other runs are refused, and a state the code does not know leaves a run out; the report, `analysis.json` and the terminal name the three guards, and through the pipeline a study checked by all three gets the rerun without every run any of them leaves out.

**O79. The HPC render always carries a complete `[placement]`: the template's declared holder and the core's default for every other setting, so every reader reads one file and none supplies a default of its own.** The platform layer requires `[placement]` (`rangemap.load_placement`) and the pilot's template declared none, so the file `up` rendered was refused by the loader, and a tool that read it had to patch in a table of its own (the ingest tool of live slot B's prep copied the render and appended one, its first provider as holder): defaults supplied separately by each tool are how a setting drifts unseen. The core has no default for the data provider (`PlacementSettings.holder`), so a template declares it: every shipped HPC template does, the pilot's with a comment saying why, and `up` refuses a template that declares no table, no `holder`, a `holder` that is no name or one that is no provider of the file, or declares the table in a spelling the render cannot add to, naming the key and never a value, before anything is submitted (`hpcplacement.check_template`, in the preflight, which runs the render's own completion over the template with each placeholder filled in as a value of its own; the render refuses the same). The render (`hpcboot.write_platform`, in both passes that size the token margin) then completes the table (`hpcplacement.complete`): what the template declared keeps its value, and every other setting is written at the default of `PlacementSettings`' fields, read from the dataclass (never a copy of the numbers, so a field added there is in the next render), each with its own type: a boolean as `true`/`false`, a float as a float; a value of a type the writer cannot spell, or a float TOML cannot hold, fails the render instead of being written as text the loader would read another way. The missing settings are added where the template declares the table, inside one `placement = { ... }` line of the root or after one `[placement]` header (TOML cannot add to an inline table from elsewhere), any other spelling is refused, and the result is read back as TOML: it must be the declared document plus exactly the added settings, or the render fails and writes nothing. A table that declares every setting is returned as it came. `load_placement` still refuses a file without the table, and `shards.py step` copies the federation's file as it is. *Learned:* the prep of live slot B (17 Nov 2026), where that ingest tool carried a default `[placement]` of its own, and the coordinator's rule that no tool supplies a default of its own, so the holder is declared and never implied. `shards.py` on `main` adds none (it copies the file as it is), and no open branch's copy does either, so nothing was removed there. **The fingerprint:** `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` does not read `[placement]` on `main`, so a render with the defaults written out and one with them implied are equal there trivially; once the fingerprint holds the placement bounds a run reads (#358, `placement_record`, which reads them through `load_placement` after their defaults), the two are equal because the record is made after the defaults, and the test pins that. *Test:* every shipped HPC template declares a holder that is one of its providers, and completing each adds exactly the core's defaults beside what it declares and touches nothing else; the render of the pilot's template carries the table, in the file and in the text returned, as the declared holder and then every field of `PlacementSettings` at its default with its type, in the dataclass's order, and nothing else; a dataclass whose defaults changed, or that gained a setting, is in the next render, and a setting with no default is refused naming it; the loader reads the table as the dataclass's defaults, the same `PlacementSettings` and `reset_settings()` as a table naming the holder alone (the loader keeps a literal copy of each default, which this pins to the dataclass); the fingerprints of the two renders are equal; a template that declares more than its holder keeps its values and gets the defaults for the rest with no setting twice, as an inline line or as a table (in a file that holds map rows too), and renders the same either way; a table that declares every setting is returned byte for byte; no table, no holder, a holder that is no name or no provider, a table that is no table, dotted keys and two lines of one spelling are each refused naming the key and never a value, and a template with no holder writes no platform file; `up` refuses each of the first three of those, and a table spelled as dotted keys, in a template before anything is submitted and takes the shipped one, and the preflight reads placeholders that stand in keys as values of their own, not as one value that collides and makes the template look unreadable; each setting reads back as the same value of the same type, and a value the writer cannot spell is refused; a shard step places a platform file with no `[placement]` as it is and `load_placement` refuses the copy naming the key. **Not run live:** `up` and a campaign on the table the render writes (the offline rehearsal of the HPC path renders it and passes).

**O80. A failure an earlier attempt left in the fault journal charges no run that meets it.** An injection the journal holds open (its restore failed, or its attempt ended without clearing it) closes the federation for every run that follows, and the attempt that made it was charged already by its own post-run check. The verdicts that name it (`SettledInjections`, and the fault gate's unsettled check, `fault_gate._unsettled`, which says "the restore of X (how) failed" for a `clear_failed` entry) charged whichever run met it first, so the first run tried after an attempt left one, in a pre-run check that closed the gate or in another attempt's post-run check, was charged an infrastructure attempt for a failure it had no part in. The injector now writes the owner of each `injecting` line, as the baseline marks do (`liveness.owner`: the process and, inside an attempt, its campaign and the store's id of the attempt), and the verdicts charge by it (`expcore_onedata.fault_owner.charge_of`): an entry owned by the attempt the check runs inside is charged to it exactly as before, so a run's own post-run check of its own failed restore stays charged; when every entry is owned by another attempt, or the check judges no attempt (a pre-run check, the dispatcher's own), the check holds uncharged, the gate records `charged: false`, and the detail says the failure was left by another attempt; an entry that records no attempt as its owner (no owner, one that cannot be read, or a process alone: a heal's container restart, a tool's injection outside any attempt) is charged as before and the detail says so, since an owner is never guessed; with several entries the judged attempt's own wins and an entry naming no attempt keeps the charge over those of other attempts. The gate's other answers that name an open injection judge it the same way: the failure of a check's recovery of what an earlier run left open (`an injection of X that an earlier run left open was not cleared`) and a lock whose holder is gone beside an injection it left open. A lock left held by a gone holder with nothing open in the journal names no attempt (the holder record is written at the run's admission, which precedes the attempt's row), so it is charged as before. An unreadable journal still fails closed and charges. `Health.charge` keeps its default of `True`: a study's own marker-based health check (a marker of its own that an attempt leaves) must answer `charge=False` for a marker an earlier attempt left, or compare the marker's owner with `liveness.current()` as `ingesthealth` and `fault_owner` do (`docs/interfaces.md`, the health check). *Learned:* a study's pilot: its own marker-based check, on `Health`'s default, read the first run tried after an attempt left an unhealthy marker as `found` and charged it an infrastructure attempt for that earlier attempt's failure; the core's baseline marks (O45) already charged only their owner, and the fault journal's verdicts had the same gap. *Test:* the `injecting` line names the attempt that made it inside an attempt and the process alone outside; an attempt's own unsettled injection is charged in its own post-run check with the detail it had, and held uncharged, with the reason, in another attempt's check and in a pre-run check; a failed restore (`start_failed`, `stop_unsettled`) is charged to its injecting attempt and to no other; an entry with no owner, an unreadable owner or a process alone is charged as before and says so; with two attempts' entries open each attempt's own check is charged and a check outside both is not; an ownerless entry beside another attempt's keeps the charge; an unreadable journal charges; through the real fault gate, a recovery of an earlier attempt's injection that fails is held uncharged in a pre-run check and in another attempt's, and charged in its own attempt's; through the core's health gate, the run after an attempt's failed injection is held with no attempt begun and an outage record that says `charged: false`, and the attempt's own post-run check is charged; a gone holder's lock beside an open injection is judged by the injection's owner; an attempt's own open injection beside another's failed restore is charged.

**O81. A launch-time or check-time git read never writes the clone it reads.** Every `git` the core runs in a study's tree, the tool server's checkout or its own (the pin check, the code identity and its digests, a package build's reads) takes its environment from `expcore.provenance.gitobjects.git_env`: the process's own plus `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`. `git status` refreshes `.git/index` on a fresh copy's first run and again after a tracked file's mtime changes, so a launch check wrote the thing it checked, and any digest of a study's artifacts directory that includes `.git` changed after the first launch (a later shard would then have been refused). With the flag `git status` gives the same answer and takes no lock, so the index keeps its bytes and its mtime. The tool server's pin check also reads the user's global and the system git configuration as empty (`GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null`, `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`), as an HPC step's environment already does (`home_env`): a global `core.fsmonitor` program does not run in a launch check, and a clone that relied on a global `safe.directory` is refused. Known limit: the flag does not stop `git diff`, which `package/tracking.py` runs at a package build, so a build still refreshes the author's index. *Learned:* a pilot's scratch copies, 17 Nov 2026: the first launch check rewrote `.git/index` of a fresh copy and again after a tracked file's mtime changed; measured with git 2.43, where `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0` kept `git status` from writing and did not keep `git diff HEAD` from it. *Test:* on a copy whose tracked files are newer than their index entries (a plain `git status` rewrites it, which a test checks first), the pin check and the code identity leave `.git/index` with the same bytes and mtime and answer clean with the right commit; a changed tracked file is still refused or dirty, and the read still writes nothing; a read-only `.git` answers the same; a global `core.fsmonitor` hook runs on a plain status and not on the pin check; and a scan of `src/` requires every git call to pass `env=git_env(...)`.

**O82. A health check can be told which attempt it judges.** A check that decides whom a failure is charged to (a study's marker, charged to the one attempt that left it) is called at the same moment from the pre-run checks of some runs, the post-run checks of others and the dispatcher's own, and was told nothing of the attempt it judged. A study's check therefore read the stacks of its callers for the gate's function names to tell its own post-run check from another attempt's pre-run check that met the marker, a guess that fails silently when a name changes and charges the wrong run when it does. The gate now hands a check that declares a parameter named `context` a frozen `CheckContext` (`expcore.campaign.healthgate`, defined in `health_context.py`): the run key, the attempt's number (the number the store gives it, the `RunIdentity`'s `attempt`; a pre-run check's is the number its try will have), the phase (`pre_run` or `post_run`) and, once its row exists, the store's id of the attempt (`attempt_id`, the one `liveness.owner()` names; None in a pre-run check). It is passed down each call, never kept on the gate that every worker shares, so two checks that meet each get their own attempt's identity. **One rule decides whether a check receives it**, the parameter's name: every other check, one with `*args` or `**kwargs` or with a parameter of another name that has a default included, is called with no argument exactly as before. A signature the gate could never fill (a `context` that cannot be passed by keyword, or a required parameter of another name such as `ctx`) is refused with a `ConfigError` when the scheduler is built, where it used to read as a failed check at every call. The dispatcher's own check, and a call that names no attempt, judge none and give a check that takes the context None. A number alone does not name an attempt: one set aside uncharged is run again under the number it had, so its two attempts differ by `attempt_id` only. *Learned:* a study's pilot: its marker-based check, which charges the attempt that left the marker (O80), had to inspect thread stacks for the health gate's function names to tell that attempt's post-run check from another attempt's pre-run check meeting the same marker. *Test:* the context is frozen and refuses a phase, number or id that names nothing real; the rule is one name, over a table of signatures (positional-or-keyword, keyword-only, with a default, a method, `*args`, another name with a default, a mock, a signature that cannot be read); a `context` that is positional-only or variadic and a required parameter of another name are refused when the gate and the scheduler are built, naming it; a zero-argument check, one with `*args` and `**kwargs` and one with `timeout=30` are called with nothing, with and without an identity at the call; attempt N+1's pre-run check and attempt N's post-run check of one run, held in the one check together, each get their own run, number, id and phase and each caller its own answer, and so do sixteen concurrent checks; the dispatcher's own check and a call with no identity give None; an identity given in part is refused; a raise or a non-answer of a check that takes the context is still a failed check; through the scheduler, each pre-run check is given the number its try then gets, with no id, each post-run check the number and the id of the stored attempt it judges, a retried run's second attempt included, and a run set aside uncharged and run again is told apart from its first attempt by its id.

**O83. A layout's slot users are provisioned the same on Compose and on HPC, an existing one is verified and never changed, and a platform template's slots and tool server are checked against the layout, so the tool server of a federation brought up on HPC starts as on Compose, from the study's own artifacts.** The core starts a tool server only for a slot's user (`sandbox/provider.py` refuses on a platform with no lease), and neither federation provisioned one: Compose's bootstrap made the fixed `[principals]` alone, its live slots used a hand-edited copy of the layout and a platform file nothing committed, and the HPC templates rendered no `[slots]` and no `[toolserver]`, so half the pilot's contrasts, the tool-server arm, could not run on an HPC federation. A slot's users are ordinary principals of the layout, and a platform template declares `[slots.<name>]` (each user's `username` and `user_id = "@user_id.<username>@"`, and the tool-server user's `rights`) and `[toolserver]` (command, pinned clone and commit, per condition the allow-list, rights and frozen manifest, with its paths under `@study_artifacts@`). So every step that provisions principals provisions the slot users, on Compose and on HPC alike and with no second code path: the zone user, its password in a 0600 file of a 0700 directory in the root (never replaced, never in a cell, `gate.json`, the facts, a record, a log or a message), its membership of each declared space with exactly the declared privileges (read back, direct and effective), and on HPC the storage mapping to the job's uid, which the bootstrap makes for every principal. **A slot user that exists is verified and never changed** (`Bootstrap.slot_planes`, `audit_slot_users`): one that exists exactly as declared (its zone user opened by the root's password file, its membership of each declared space with exactly the declared direct and effective privileges, no other space, no group, no cluster) is recorded `matched` in the facts with nothing sent for it (no create, invite, join or privilege change); any difference is refused naming the user and the attribute (`password`, `membership of <space>`, `direct privileges in <space>`, `effective privileges in <space>`, `spaces`, `group`, `cluster`), the zone as it was and a password file this call made for a user the zone refused removed. A fixed principal still converges as it always did. A slot user this bootstrap made and did not finish (an up that stopped midway, as the day-0 reports saw when a panel answered 500) is recorded by name in the root's `secrets/slot-users.pending` before it is made and cleared once its audit has passed, so the rerun finishes it as it finishes any principal while every user that was there before stays read-only. The slot users are read from the template as it stands (`fedtools.slot_users`; a template that declares `[slots]` and is no TOML is refused, not ignored), since provisioning runs before the file is rendered. After the file is written it is loaded as a campaign loads it, its slots as the lease pool reads them, and checked against the layout (`fedtools.check_rendered`): a slot user that is no principal, an id that is not the facts', a user the agent may not use, a data-path user with more than the baseline, a tool-server user whose `rights` are not exactly its principal's privileges (so rights typed in a template cannot differ from what was provisioned), a condition no slot's user could serve, and a user of other spaces than its plane's are each refused naming the slot or the condition by its position. The study's directory is `HPC_STUDY_ARTIFACTS` in `site.env` (`Site.study_artifacts`, the placeholder `@study_artifacts@`; Compose reads `EXPCORE_FED_STUDY_ARTIFACTS`), and `up` refuses, before any job is held, a template that names it with none given, a command that is no executable file, an argument naming a missing file of the study, a clone not at the pinned commit with no change to a tracked file (`expcore_onedata.pin`, the check the launcher makes at every start) and a frozen manifest that is edited or shows other tools than its condition's allow-list. The tool server is started through the resolve launcher of O73, so on HPC its command is a Python interpreter and its arguments start with `-m <module>`: `up` refuses anything else before a job is held, and the platform layer at load, each saying to use `<venv>/bin/python` with `args = ['-m', '<module>', ...]`. The slot users are read as the platform layer's loader reads the template, whatever TOML spelling it uses (a quoted table name, inline tables), and a relative clone or manifest is read from the root, where the platform file lies, as the loader reads it. A layout with no `[slots]` renders the file and the facts it always did. *Learned:* the live probe of the pilot's tool-server arm against main (`sandbox/provider.py` refusing "start a tool server, whose user is a slot's" on a lease-less platform, no slot provisioned, no `[toolserver]` rendered), the Compose slots of the day-0 reports, whose users, rights and platform files were made by hand, and the pilot's own templates, which declare both tables. *Test:* the Compose bootstrap and the HPC one, over one layout and a fake zone each, make the same users (the fixed one and the slots' four), each a member of the same spaces holding exactly its declared privileges, record them `created`, and map every slot user to the job's uid like a fixed user; over users that already exist as declared they record `matched` and send nothing, a rerun after an up that stopped after it made the users finishes those it made and matches the rest, and over each difference an existing slot user may have (a direct or an effective privilege, a privilege short, a membership, another space, a group, a cluster, a password the root's file does not match) they refuse naming the user and the attribute with the zone unchanged, while a fixed principal is converged as before; a password is a 0600 file in a 0700 directory, is not replaced by a later `up`, and is in no other file of the root or in a refusal; both renderers, over one layout and template, write the same `[slots]` and `[toolserver]`; the template's tables are rendered with the facts' ids and the core loads the file and its slots, and each refusal above, a credential a slot user lacks, a template that names the study's artifacts with none given and each artifact that cannot run the server is refused naming no path, a relative clone and manifest being read from the root; the slot users of a template that quotes its table names or uses inline tables are as protected as any; the pilot's form of the tool server (its interpreter, clone and manifests under `toolserver/`, `-m <module>`) loads and starts through the launcher, and the `/usr/bin/env` form is refused at load and before a job is held, naming the form to use; Compose's `up` checks the artifacts before it prepares the root and hands the facts the directory only when given one; the pilot-shape slot layout's file loads with its map and resolver and the server is started as `<interpreter> -I -B <launcher> <map> -- -m <module>`, a `[toolserver]` the launcher cannot start is refused at load, and `up` without `HPC_STUDY_ARTIFACTS` holds no job; the example's template is the pilot's with the tables after it; and the sandbox platform's own lease-group and provider tests, run over a platform whose `[slots]` the renderer wrote, lease, mint, journal, revoke and free its slots as they do a document's. **Not run live, and owed:** the next HPC slot's B2 run, whose tool calls must succeed, whose rights refusals must be the ones Compose gives for the same layout, and whose visible-names counts must be zero; the tool server's start from a venv and a clone shipped in the artifacts directory on a node (the interpreter and `onedata-mcp` under the launcher, which O73 also owes); the zone's acceptance of the slot users and memberships, and its answers to the user's own reads of its groups, clusters and spaces that the audit makes (`/user/spaces`, `/user/groups`, `/user/effective_clusters`), which only fakes of the zone's interfaces have answered here.

**O84. The tool server is told to read no `.env` file and starts in an empty private directory, so nothing it is configured with comes from a file the launch does not name.** onedata-mcp read a `.env` when its config module was imported (python-dotenv's `load_dotenv()` at import). Its default search starts at the directory of the file that calls it, the installed package, and walks up to `/` (python-dotenv 1.2.2; only a REPL, a debugger or a frozen program searches from the working directory instead), takes the first `.env` it finds and overrides nothing, so a `.env` in any ancestor of the package (on a cluster account, a home directory several studies share) could set any `ONEDATA_*` setting the launch left unset, `ONEDATA_MCP_NODE_NAMES` among them, with nothing in the launch saying so. Three changes close it. **(1) The word:** onedata-mcp reads `ONEDATA_MCP_DOTENV` from the process environment before any file and, when it is `0`, reads no `.env`; unset, it behaves as before for the server's other users. `toolserver.server_environment` sets it to `0` on every platform, the resolve launcher's included, as a managed setting a caller can neither give nor clear (one function builds the environment, however the server is started). **(2) The directory:** the server's working directory was the driver's (or a placed process's home) and is now a new, empty directory of mode 0700 for each start (`expcore.tools.workdir.private_workdir`, `mkdtemp`, owner only), removed once the server has stopped, because FastMCP separately reads a `.env` in the working directory for its own `FASTMCP_*` settings, which the word does not cover. **(3) The manifest's start:** a condition's frozen manifest is hashed into the campaign fingerprint, and the server shows the tools its allow-list, its hosts and the files it reads configure, so a manifest read from a server the launch would not start (another environment, the driver's directory) could freeze tools no run sees. `python -m expcore_onedata.toolmanifest freeze` and `check` start the server from `toolserver.server_start`, the one function that builds its command, its environment (`server_environment`, the private CA copy included) and its private directory, which the launch uses too; `--pass-env` refuses every variable the launch sets itself; and the core's own command line, for a server the study starts, now starts it in a private directory as well. **(4) The bytecode:** the server is a Python started from trees a study freezes (its clone, its virtualenv, the interpreter's own library), and a Python started with neither `-B` nor `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE` writes a `.pyc` beside every module it imports. On a platform without `[addresses] resolver` the server started as `<python> -m <module>` with neither, and a `check` of its manifest wrote 22 files into the clone, 1,083 into the virtualenv and some into the bundled interpreter's own library (with the variable set nothing was written); a written file changes the digest of the study's frozen artifacts, and a launch does the same. With the resolve launcher the server already ran `-I -B`. `toolserver.server_environment` now sets `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` on every platform, as a managed setting a caller can neither give nor clear (`--pass-env` refuses it, as it refuses the word), so the launch and the manifest command, which start the server through `server_start`, write nothing on either path (the launcher's `-I` ignores the variable and its `-B` does the same there). **What remains:** the guard exists only in onedata-mcp commits that carry it, so a study pins one (an older server ignores the word and reads the file); a manifest frozen against this server with `python -m expcore.tools.manifest` before this change was frozen from the environment and the directory it ran in, so freeze it again with the new command (a changed tool list changes the condition's hash in the campaign fingerprint, and a store started with the earlier manifest is not resumed with it); the core command line (`python -m expcore.tools.manifest`) still starts whatever command it is given with only the variables `--pass-env` names and knows nothing of a platform, so nothing stops a study from pointing it at this server: the study uses `expcore_onedata.toolmanifest` for it; it assumes the server's tool list does not depend on its token (the stand-in shows it, the real server is not tried: `--token-env` supplies one); a relative path in `[toolserver] command` or `args` no longer resolves against the driver's working directory; a server whose declared `[toolserver] args` run Python with `-E` or `-I` and no `-B` makes the interpreter ignore `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE`, so such a study passes `-B` itself; and a platform with another tool server needs a mechanism of its own. *Learned:* the pilot's review of how the server is launched on HPC, and its measurement of a manifest check that wrote bytecode into the tree it certifies. *Test:* the server's environment holds `ONEDATA_MCP_DOTENV=0` on a Compose platform and on an HPC platform whose server starts through the launcher, and a caller can neither set nor clear it; each start gets a directory of its own, empty, mode 0700, owned by the driver's user, not the driver's working directory and gone once the server has stopped, and a second start gets another; a real child process started by the launch, on both platforms, that imports a stand-in config behind the same guard, with a `.env` in an ancestor directory of its code that sets the node names, loads nothing, while the same import without the word, or with another value, loads them (python-dotenv is no dependency of the core, so the stand-in follows its search; the library itself is exercised by onedata-mcp's own tests, which import its config from a copy planted under a tree with such a `.env` and find `os.environ` unchanged with the word `0` and the file read otherwise); `freeze` and `check` of the new command, beside a launch of the same condition and on both platforms, start a real child that records what it received: its Onedata settings equal the launch's one by one (allow-list, hosts, token, the word), its directory is new, empty, mode 0700 and gone afterwards, and with the stand-in's `.env` planted above its code it loads nothing; `server_start`, which both use, gives the environment `server_environment` builds key by key (a caller's own variable kept) and a different directory at each start; every variable the launch sets itself (the managed settings and the two it overrides) is refused by `--pass-env` with a message naming it, set or not, with nothing started or written; a variable that is not set is an error; without `--token-env` both token settings hold the fixed placeholder; `freeze` and `check` refuse a pinned clone at another commit, and one with a local change, naming which, before starting anything or writing the manifest; `freeze` writes the allow-list's tools to the declared manifest, which need not exist yet; and the core command line starts its server in a new, empty, private directory each time, in which a `.env` of the driver's directory is not found. The server's environment holds `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` on a Compose platform and on an HPC platform whose server starts through the launcher, and a caller can neither set nor clear it; `--pass-env` of it is refused, whether or not the driver's environment holds it; a real child Python started from `server_start` on either platform, importing a sibling module and a package of its own tree, leaves no `__pycache__` and no `.pyc` there (the same child without the setting writes them, the control), and `freeze` then `check` of a real server on either platform leave the tree it runs from as it was (`tests/onedata/test_toolserver_bytecode.py`; the HPC cases wrote no files before the fix either, since the launcher ran `-I -B`, so they guard that path and fail on the missing setting alone).

**O85. The data gate never reuses an upstream connection after a request that carried a body, and sends nothing twice.** The gate kept one upstream connection per client connection and sent the next request on it, whatever the last one had carried. Measured live on 17 Nov 2026, directly against two Onedata providers with Python's `http.client` (the gate's own library) and no `Expect` header: after a request with a body of about 1.5 MiB (a PUT), the next request on the same kept connection (a GET, or the next POST) stalled in about half the tries until the client's timeout, while a new connection answered in milliseconds; bodies of 400 KiB and of 4 MiB never stalled the next request. The gate waits on an upstream for 14,400 s, so one request could have hung for hours. The rule is now: after a request whose body the gate handed to the upstream (the body actually sent: any method; a scope inference's rewritten body and every page of a complete listing that has a body count; a request with none or a length of 0 does not), the upstream connection is closed once the answer is read, whatever its status, and the next request opens a new one (`gate._settle`); the request's line says `upstream_closed` `"after_body"`. It changes only whether a connection is reused: the request is sent once, on the connection it was on, and neither a retry, a status, an answer, a header nor a timeout changes; a body-less request keeps today's reuse. The gate is one file on the Docker and the Apptainer backend, so both behave alike. **What the gate relays, and what is unproven:** the gate relays no write (its policy answers a PUT with a 403 of its own, and a request body over 1 MiB, `gate.MAX_BODY`, is refused unread with a 413, a chunked body with a 400), so a body of 1.5 MiB never reaches an upstream through it, and the stall was measured on direct connections, not through the gate. The bodies it does relay are at most 1 MiB (the lookup and scope POSTs, and any allowed call that declares a body), and whether a body of that size can stall the next request is not measured; the rule is a defence for those and for any later widening of the policy, and is applied to every body rather than to a size band the gate would have to guess. The cost is one connect and handshake for the request after each such request. *Learned:* the live measurement of 17 Nov 2026 on two providers (a stall on the request after a body of about 1.5 MiB; none at 400 KiB or 4 MiB), and the earlier stall of content uploads on reused connections (O25), which the client already avoids by sending a large write on a connection of its own; O73 left the gate's connection reuse unchanged "until the stall's mechanism is diagnosed", and this is the change that does not wait for it. **Not run live:** the stall's mechanism, whether a body of 1 MiB or less can stall the next request on the platform, and a run's `duration_ms` and `upstream_conn` over a real federation after the change. *Test:* a real gate and a plain-HTTP upstream that stalls the next request on a connection after a body of 512 KiB or more: a POST of the largest body the gate relays, `MAX_BODY`, is answered, and the GET after it is answered at once on a new upstream connection (it stalled before), each request seen by the upstream once, the POST's line `new` with `upstream_closed` `after_body` and the GET's `new` with no `upstream_stale`, `upstream_retries` or `upstream_closed`; a GET followed by a GET, and a POST with no body followed by a GET, share one upstream connection (`reused`) and no line says closed; an upstream's 403 to a POST with a small body reaches the client as it is and its connection is closed all the same; a POST with a body that follows a GET goes on the GET's kept connection, once, and the GET after it on a new one; and the two pages of a complete listing with a body are on two connections and asked once each.

**O86. The federation's own services are observed over a window, the same on both backends: a restart is explained only by the injector's journal (and, on HPC, by the launches and recorded stops), an unreadable read is unknown and never healthy, and every call only reads.** A study's post-check reads the zone and the providers over a run's or a batch's window. On Compose the driver's read gave an identity (container id and start time) and no restart count; on HPC there was no observed read: the infra readings (O17, O18) cover only the run's own components, and the driver's state file is not an observation (a service restarted outside the driver, or a step the state still names after it ended, reads there as it was). **The read:** each service's state as the backend shows it, by the container (`docker inspect`: id, start, `RestartCount`, health, exit) or by the step's exact id (`squeue -s` and `sacct`: running or not, start, end, exit; an id neither listed nor recorded, or the queue and accounting disagreeing, unknown), and the service's own `GET /configuration` answer at the window's start and at its end, never asked of a service that is not running. **A restart:** a changed container or step id, a changed start, a risen counter. It is labelled `outage` or `heal` only when the fault journal explains it (the journal is append-only and its lines carry no time, so the window is the lines after the start reading's count; an injection whose stop was asked, not `never_down`, with its stop in the window, or whose restore is the start of a service that was down), and on HPC the launch count and the stops the state records agree; a step the state does not name, a risen `RestartCount`, a journal not declared, unreadable or shorter, anything else, is a finding. **Unknown:** a command that fails, a container or step missing, a liveness read that fails, no reading at the start: unknown with the reason, and the window is not `stable`. **Every run carries its window:** `SandboxPlatform(..., federation_watch=)` reads the federation in `launch`, before the run's tokens and sandbox exist and so before any injection its body makes, and reads the window once the body has ended and before the stop, so after the restore of an injector inside the launch; the window is the lines the journal gained between the two (it has no timestamps, so a read made after the run cannot replace it), and it is recorded in the sandbox's `infrastructure_facts` as `federation_window` on both sandbox backends: the verdict, each service's status and restarts, the findings and the unknowns, by label (`zone`, `providers[i]`) and never a service, container, step, node, address or secret. A read that fails is recorded `unknown` with the exception's type and never fails the run; a body's own error and a cancellation stand over it, and the sandbox is still stopped. Every run has the key from its preparation on (`unknown`, "no federation watch configured", for a platform given no watch, so a post-check sees the gap, and a pending placeholder, which the `metadata.json` collect wrote holds, for one given a watch), and the closed window is persisted as `federation-window.json` beside the `metadata.json` of each directory `collect` wrote into (one that is gone is skipped, one that cannot be written is logged, never a failure). **Both executor orders are judged, and only the run's own injection explains anything:** a window asked of a run (`window(start, owner=)`, which the launch passes) counts only the injections whose owner names that run's attempt (#371's `owner`), so another attempt's leftover and an ownerless line explain nothing and are a finding, and a run naming no attempt owns none (`unattributed`). A service not running at the end that an open injection of the run's holds down (the service was down at the start too, or its stop was asked in the window: one the service ran through is another outage) is `held`: explained, no finding and not `restarted` (the pilot's order, injecting before the launch and restoring after it), the window's verdict following the worst service (finding, unknown, held, restarted, stable); only the injection's target may read held or restarted, and another service that restarted or is down is a finding. The restore comes after the window, so the facts point at the injector's `restore_verified` and a held service whose restore failed is no pass. Inside the launch the window reads `restarted` with the restart labelled `outage`. *Learned:* the CCGrid pilot's batch post-check stopped every HPC batch for want of this read (17 Nov). *Accounting lags a stop (17 Nov):* the driver confirms a stop once `squeue -s` no longer lists the step and `sacct` follows seconds later, so the first study's order, which stops the target just before the window's start reading, would have read the target unknown on every run and stopped the batch again. The HPC read now waits when, and only when, the driver's state says the step was stopped (service `stopped`, its last recorded stop confirmed, the step the one of its launch) and `squeue` and `sacct` do not yet agree that it ended: every such service polled every 2 s under one bound, `accounting_wait_s` (a declared setting of the watch, 60 s unless declared, 0 to 300), the seconds recorded per side and per run (`accounting_wait_s_used`); past the bound the service is unknown with the reason `accounting_lag`, never running and never stopped by assumption. A recorded stop alone says nothing (`stops` outlive a restart), and a failed command, a step listed under another id or name, or an unreadable row keeps its own reading and is never waited for. *Test:* a stable window; a restart the journal explains labelled outage and heal; one it does not, one explained only by an injection that finished before the window, a risen `RestartCount`, a restart outside the driver behind an injection's launch, a launch with no recorded stop: findings; an unreadable Docker, Slurm, accounting or `/configuration` and no start reading: unknown with the reason; an HPC step read by its exact id and never a neighbour's; the same shape on both backends; no command that writes and a journal directory left byte for byte. *Per run (ownership, held):* in the pilot's order (the real injector prepared and injected, then the launch, then the restore) the target is `held` and every other service `stable`, with no finding and the restore after the window; inside the launch the restart is `outage` and the run's own; a second provider that restarts is a finding; a target held down by another attempt's injection or an ownerless line is a finding; a window naming no attempt owns nothing; no owner named keeps the batch's reading and says so; the verdict follows the worst service. *Per run:* a run's facts carry the window, stable on a fake federation; an S3-shaped run (the real injector inside the launch) reads `restarted` with the restart labelled `outage`, the read before the injector's stop and the window after its restore, in that order; a service restarted outside the injector is a finding; an unreadable federation, a watch that raises at either end and a start that could not be read are `unknown` and the run finishes with its sandbox stopped; a body's error stands; a cancelled run is stopped and says its window was not read; the window is written as `federation-window.json` beside the `metadata.json` collect wrote, on both backends, for every collected directory that still exists, with a gone one skipped and a write that fails logged and never fatal; a platform with no watch records `unknown` on both backends and writes no file; an HPC federation on an Apptainer sandbox gives the same shape; no fact holds a name of the backend, the configuration or the journal. *Accounting lag:* over a fake Slurm whose accounting shows a stopped step only after a time its clock counts (no row, a row that still runs, one not ended, one without an end, `squeue` still listing it): the read waits and reads the stop with the wait recorded; past the bound, and with a bound of 0, unknown `accounting_lag`; no wait for a service restarted since its stop, never stopped, stopped but not confirmed, listed under another id, of another launch, or for a command that fails (also when it starts failing during the wait); one bound for all services; only `squeue` and `sacct` run; a bound outside 0 to 300 is refused by the command and by `watch_for`, which hands it to the command and allows for it in the command's time; the side and the run's facts carry the wait; in the pilot's order with the real injector the window is `held` after the wait (the default bound) and `unknown`, never held, with no wait.

**O87. The content check reads through the provider a study names, so the local provider keeps the blocks it had, and every run records its node's OS release, glibc and kernel, refusing for none of them.** `[sandbox] content_check_provider = "<a provider's layout name>"` (optional; both backends; only with `content_check_file`) has the pre-campaign content check read through that provider, the file's owner, which holds it whole: the space's root, each listing page, the walk from the file to its space and both content reads all go to it, and the check's detail and the day-0 check `content_type` name it by its position, `providers[i]`. A read through a provider that lacks a file's blocks makes it fetch and keep them, so with the check on the local provider a study whose states want every file empty there before a run could not name a fully local file; unset, or naming the local provider, the check reads through the local provider as before. Loading refuses a name that is no provider of `[providers]` or that does not support the first space the data path reaches, naming the key and never the provider. It is in the platform's fingerprint when it is not the local provider, by position (`sandbox.content_check_provider`), and absent otherwise, so a store older than the key resumes. **Node readings:** each run's `infrastructure_facts.node` (on Apptainer, the node's; on Docker, the host that runs the agent's container, the driver's own machine, in the same shape and under the same name) holds the `ID` and `VERSION_ID` of `/etc/os-release` (read as text, never executed, only its first 64 KiB), the glibc version string (`os.confstr`) and the kernel release (`os.uname`), and `tool_server_host` the same where the tool server's host differs from the node's; the HPC driver's node facts step prints the same and its state keeps them per service. They are recorded and never refused: one that cannot be read is `unknown` with its reason (a missing line, a value of no plain shape, the type of the exception: never a message or a path), and a node whose reading raises or answers in another shape is `unknown`. *Learned:* a study's states (17 Nov) want every file empty at the local provider before a run, and the check's own read, through that provider, would have filled it; a node's C library, kernel and distribution decide what a preloaded shim or a bundled binary loads, and the record named none of them. *Test:* with the key every request of the check goes to the named provider and the local provider's block map is unchanged, and without it the same federation's local provider keeps the file's blocks (the control); a name that is undeclared, not a name, not a supporter or set without a check file is refused at load naming the key; the fingerprint holds the position only for another provider, equal across two federations and with the local provider named or left out; the readings of a machine, of one that cannot be read (every reading unknown with its reason, nothing refused) and of a hostile os-release (shell syntax, bytes that are no text, a huge file) are as stated, the driver's step and the sandbox's reader answer alike, a node's facts step that prints none, or a malformed value, leaves `up` going with the readings unknown, and a Docker run and an Apptainer run of one machine, readable or not, carry the same `node` keys.

## K. Replication packages

**K01. A check with no input fails.** *Learned:* ICSA `tools/artifact/build.py:411-412`, `check_secrets.py:32-34`. *Test:* the secret check on an empty input exits non-zero.

**K02. Canaries run before every build.** A planted case per rule must still be caught. *Learned:* ICSA `tools/artifact/canaries.py:1-7`, `build.py:413-417`. *Test:* disabling a rule fails the canary run.

**K03. The private name list never enters the repository or the output.** *Learned:* ICSA `build.py:304-313`. *Test:* a private list under the repository root refuses the build.

**K04. Anonymisation must not change numbers, model names or parseability.** *Learned:* ICSA `build.py:315-330, 439-465`, `tools/artifact/tablecheck.py:1-6` (1ac2f29b, 5 Nov; 349199da, 6 Nov). *Test:* a mapping that alters a number, a model name or a parse fails the build.

**K05. Findings never carry the matched value.** *Learned:* ICSA `tools/artifact/scan.py:3-4`, `build.py:510-519`. *Test:* a planted secret's finding shows only its masked shape.

**K06. Archives are deterministic.** Sorted names, uid and gid 0, gzip without timestamp or original name; publish by rename after the stream closes. *Learned:* WWW `results/dev-natural-20261020/README.md:39-43`; ICSA 77622e79 and aca37ffb (5 Nov). *Test:* two builds of one tree are byte-identical.

**K07. `make check` regenerates every table from records and compares byte for byte.** *Learned:* ICSOC-pkg `Makefile:7-28`, `regenerate.py:33-58`; ICSA's package (6 Nov). *Test:* the example study's `make check` passes from a clean checkout.

**K08. Shipped code parses on the oldest supported Python.** *Learned:* ICSA 8a578931 (6 Nov; 3.12 f-string syntax broke 3.11 nodes). *Test:* a compile pass under the minimum version.

**K09. Temporary credential copies are removed.** *Learned:* ICSOC `harness/panel.py:134-154, 553` never removed per-leg config directories holding a credentials copy. *Test:* teardown deletes every temporary directory it created.

**K10. A package installs the environment it was checked under, offline.** One wheel per distribution of the environment the replay check ran under, pinned by SHA-256; `make check` installs them with no index, and the package's check refuses any other environment. *Learned:* #45 and #46 (a reviewer could not learn which core to install, and a study on a non-core platform failed at the reader's end). *Test:* the install target takes exactly the vendored wheels with an unreachable index configured; a tampered wheel and a version that differs are refused.

**K11. An anonymised package holds no commit id and no source URL, and passes its own check.** The code identity is the tree hash alone: every commit is replaced by the first 40 digits of its tree hash, in the records, the check, the stamp, the outputs and PACKAGE.json, and the environment keeps names and versions only. The core's own commit and URL go too. *Learned:* #47 (a commit id against a public repository is a lookup key to its authors). *Test:* no commit or URL of the study or the core in any file of the package, the regenerated tables are the author's with the commit replaced, and a commit or URL left in a file refuses the build without showing it.

**K12. A package lists every included file no commit holds.** Files outside the code identity and `out/`, untracked or changed since their commit, are warned about and listed in `PACKAGE.json`; an anonymised build refuses them unless told otherwise. *Learned:* #61 (a build took an uncommitted `package.toml` and untracked files without a word). *Test:* a modified and an untracked file are named in the warning and the list; an anonymised build refuses them unless `--allow-uncommitted`.

**K13. A package never publishes where a model's key lives.** Every build, plain and anonymised, writes each `key_file` and `key_env` of `models.toml` as a fixed placeholder. Where a key lives is deployment, not experiment, so the code identity and the campaign fingerprint hash `models.toml` in that same form, and the shipped file verifies as it is. *Learned:* #126 (a `key_file` path naming a private directory went into a package without a word). *Test:* a package of a study whose key file lies under a folder named after an earlier study holds no part of that path, nor the key variable's name, in any file or member name, and passes its own check in both builds; a package whose `models.toml` or any other file still names a key location is refused, as is a second `models.toml`, the key file itself (by name, whatever the case, and by its size and SHA-256 under any name, in any file, attested or not, at any depth of its archives and encoded runs, behind whatever the content check refuses, and in any member of any wheel, while a file that walk cannot finish is refused too; and a key file of 16 bytes or more by its content, as it is and without the white space around it, anywhere inside the bytes of any stream, so one in a tar in the v7 format, behind junk, in a write-ahead log or in a script is refused as well, while a shorter key is found only as a whole file), a path inside a longer one, after `file://` or before a `:`, the same path written `$HOME/...`, `${HOME}/...`, `~/...` or under another host's home, a bare dotted file name in prose, and a key location in any wheel not from an index but the core's, while `my-key.txt`, `import secrets` and a core that names `API_KEY` are not; the rewrite changes no byte inside a multi-line string or under an array of tables; moving a key leaves the campaign one experiment.
